Confessions

The first autobiography: a brilliant, restless man tells God everything, and invents the inner life on the way

by Augustine of Hippo

c. 400 AD 2026

This is an AI modernization of Confessions into contemporary English. The original is available from Project Gutenberg.

Book One: Infancy, Boyhood, and the Beginnings of Sin

You are great, Lord, and greatly to be praised; great is Your power, and Your wisdom has no limit. And a human being wants to praise You — a human being, one small piece of what You have made, carrying his own mortality about with him, carrying the evidence of his sin and the evidence that You resist the proud. Still he wants to praise You, this small piece of what You have made. You stir us to take delight in praising You, because You made us for Yourself, and our heart is restless until it rests in You.

Grant me, Lord, to know and understand which comes first: calling on You or praising You? And again, knowing You or calling on You? For how can anyone call on You without knowing You? Not knowing You, he might call on something else instead. Or is it rather that we call on You in order to know You? But how shall they call on him in whom they have not believed? And how shall they believe without a preacher? And they that seek the Lord shall praise him — for those who seek will find him, and those who find will praise him. So let me seek You, Lord, by calling on You, and call on You by believing in You; for You have been preached to us. My faith calls on You, Lord — the faith You gave me, the faith You breathed into me through the humanity of Your Son, through the work of the man who preached.

And how shall I call on my God, my God and Lord, when calling for him means calling him into myself? What room is there in me for my God to come into? Where can God come into me — God, who made heaven and earth? Is there really anything in me, Lord my God, that can hold You? Do heaven and earth hold You, which You made, and in which You made me? Or is it that, since nothing that exists could exist without You, whatever exists holds You? Then since I exist too, why do I ask You to come into me, when I would not be at all if You were not in me already? Why? Because I have not gone down to hell — and yet You are there too. For if I go down into hell, You are there. I could not exist, my God, could not exist at all, if You were not in me — or rather, unless I were in You, from whom are all things, by whom are all things, in whom are all things. Just so, Lord. Just so. Where am I calling You to, when I am already in You? Or where can You come into me from? Where could I go, beyond heaven and earth, for my God to come to me from there — my God, who has said, I fill heaven and earth?

Do heaven and earth hold You, then, since You fill them? Or do You fill them and overflow, since they cannot hold You? And when heaven and earth are full, where do You pour out the rest of Yourself? Or do You have no need of anything to hold You, since You hold all things — filling what You fill by holding it? The vessels You fill do not hold You up: they could all be smashed and You would not spill out. And when You are poured out over us, You are not brought down but lift us up; You are not scattered but gather us together. But You, who fill all things: do You fill them with the whole of Yourself? Or, since nothing can hold all of You, does each thing hold a part? And the same part, all at once? Or each its own part, the larger things more and the smaller less? Is one part of You larger, then, and another smaller? Or are You wholly everywhere, while nothing holds the whole of You?

What are You, then, my God? What, except the Lord God? For who is Lord but the Lord? Who is God but our God? Highest, best, most powerful, most all-powerful; most merciful and most just; most hidden and most present; most beautiful and most strong; steady and beyond comprehension; unchangeable and changing everything; never new, never old, and making all things new, while bringing the proud into old age without their noticing; always working, always at rest; still gathering and lacking nothing; supporting, filling, sheltering; creating, feeding, ripening; searching, though You have everything. You love, without being agitated by it; You are jealous, without anxiety; You repent, but You do not grieve; You are angry, and You are calm; You change Your work, and Your purpose does not change; You take back what You find, and never lost it; You are never in need, and You are glad of what You gain; You are never greedy, and You demand interest. We give You more than we owe, so that You may be in our debt — and who has anything that is not Yours? You pay debts, owing nothing; You cancel debts, losing nothing. And what have I said now, my God, my life, my holy joy? What does anyone say when he speaks about You? And yet woe to the man who says nothing, since even the most eloquent are struck dumb.

Oh, that I might rest in You! Oh, that You would come into my heart and flood it, so that I might forget my troubles and take hold of You, my one good! What are You to me? Have pity, and teach me how to say it. What am I to You, that You demand my love and are angry with me if I do not give it, and threaten me with terrible miseries? Is it a small misery, then, not to love You? Oh, for Your mercy's sake, Lord my God, tell me what You are to me. Say to my soul, I am your salvation. Say it so that I hear it. Look: my heart is in front of You, Lord; open its ears and say to my soul, I am your salvation. Let me run after that voice and take hold of You. Do not hide Your face from me. Let me die — so that I do not die — only let me see Your face.

The house of my soul is too narrow for You to come into; enlarge it. It is falling down; repair it. There are things in it that must offend Your eyes; I confess it and I know it. But who is going to clean it out? Whom should I cry to but You? Lord, cleanse me from my secret faults, and spare Your servant from the power of the enemy. I believe, and so I speak. Lord, You know. Have I not confessed my sins against myself to You, and have You not, my God, forgiven the wickedness of my heart? I will not go to law with You, who are the truth. I am afraid of deceiving myself, of letting my own wrongdoing lie to itself. So I will not go to law with You — for if You, Lord, should keep account of our sins, Lord, who could stand it?

Yet let me speak to Your mercy, dust and ashes that I am. Let me speak, since it is Your mercy I am speaking to, and not to some man who would laugh at me. Perhaps You laugh at me too — but You will turn back and have compassion on me. For what would I say, Lord my God, except that I do not know where I came from into this dying life, or should I call it living death? And straight away the comforts of Your compassion took me up, as I have heard from the parents of my flesh, out of whose substance You once shaped me — I do not remember it myself. So I was met by the comfort of a woman's milk. My mother and my nurses did not fill their own breasts for me; it was You who gave me the food of my infancy through them, according to Your arrangement, by which You distribute Your riches through the hidden depths of things. You also gave me not to want more than You gave, and gave my nurses the willingness to give me what You had given them. They gave me what they had from You, and they gave it gladly, out of a love You had taught them. What was good for me from them was good for them too. And in fact it was not from them but through them — because all good things are from You, God, and all my health is from my God. I learned this only later, when You called out to me through these gifts of Yours, inside me and outside me. In those days all I knew was how to suck, to be content with what pleased me and cry at what hurt me; nothing more.

Later I began to smile, first in my sleep and then awake — so I was told about myself, and I believed it, because we see other babies do the same, though I remember nothing of my own. Bit by bit I began to notice where I was, and to want to make my wants known to the people who could satisfy them; and I could not, because the wants were inside me and the people were outside, and no sense of theirs could get into my mind. So I flung my arms and legs and voice about at random, making the few signs I could — signs very little like what I actually meant. And when I was not obeyed at once, either because my wants were harmful or because nobody understood them, I was outraged: outraged that my elders would not do as they were told, that free people would not serve me, that people who owed me nothing would not wait on me — and I punished them for it by crying. That is what babies are like; I have watched them and learned it. And that I was like that myself, they have shown me better, without knowing it, than my nurses could who did know it.

And now my infancy is long dead, and I am alive. But You, Lord, live for ever, and nothing in You dies. Before the foundation of the world, before anything that can be called "before", You are, and You are God and Lord of everything You created. In You stand fixed for ever the first causes of everything that does not stand; in You, unchanging, are the sources of everything that changes; in You live the eternal reasons of everything unreasoning and temporary. Tell me, Lord, tell Your suppliant; have pity, and tell me who need pity: did my infancy come after some earlier age of mine that had already died? Was that the time I spent inside my mother? I have heard something about that, and I have seen pregnant women myself. But before that again, God my joy — was I anywhere, was I anybody? There is nobody to tell me. Not my father, not my mother, not other people's experience, not my own memory. Are You laughing at me for asking, and telling me to praise You and acknowledge You for what I do know?

I acknowledge You, Lord of heaven and earth, and praise You for the first rudiments of my being and for my infancy, which I remember nothing of; because You have arranged that a man should guess much about himself from other people, and believe a great deal of it on the word of mere women. Even then I had being and life, and by the end of my infancy I could look for signs by which to make my feelings known to others. Where could such a creature come from, Lord, if not from You? Can anyone be his own maker? Is there some other channel anywhere that could pour existence and life into us, except from You, Lord, in whom existing and living are one thing — since You Yourself are supremely existence and supremely life? For You are most high, and You do not change, and today does not come to an end in You. And yet it does come to an end in You, since all such things are in You too; they would have no way of passing at all unless You held them up. And since Your years do not run out, Your years are a single today. How many of our years, and our fathers' years, have flowed away through Your "today", taking from it the measure and the shape of whatever being they had? And still others will flow away and take their shape in turn. But You are still the same, and everything of tomorrow and beyond, and everything of yesterday and behind it, You have done today. What is it to me if somebody cannot follow this? Let him be glad and say, What is this? Let him be glad even so, and be content to find You by not finding You, rather than to miss You by finding.

Hear me, God. How terrible human sin is! So says a man, and You pity him, because You made him and did not make the sin in him. Who will remind me of the sins of my infancy? For in Your sight nobody is free of sin, not even a baby whose life on earth is one day long. Who will remind me? Does not every small child, in whom I can see what I do not remember of myself? What was my sin, then? Was it that I cried for the breast? If I cried now for food suitable to my age, I should rightly be laughed at and told off. What I did then deserved telling off; but since I could not have understood it, custom and reason forbade anyone to tell me off. Those are habits we dig out and throw away once we have grown — and no one who prunes a tree knowingly throws away the good wood. So was it good, even for a time, to cry for what would have hurt me if I got it? To be bitterly angry that free people, my own elders, the very people who gave me life, did not wait on me? That plenty of others, wiser than I was, did not jump at my nod? To do my best to hit and hurt them, because they would not obey orders that it would have harmed me to have obeyed? It is the weakness of a baby's limbs that is innocent, not its will. I have seen a baby jealous myself. It could not talk yet, and it went pale and glared at the other child at the breast. Everybody knows this. Mothers and nurses will tell you they have their own remedies for it. But is that innocence — when the fountain of milk is running rich and full, to refuse to let another share it, though he needs it desperately and his whole life depends on it? We put up with all this gently, not because it is no evil, or a small one, but because the child will grow out of it. It is tolerated now, and exactly the same temper is completely intolerable in an older person.

You then, Lord my God, who gave life to this infancy of mine, and fitted out the body You gave me with senses, as we can see, and joined its limbs together, and shaped it well, and planted in it all the functions of life for its safety and good — You command me to praise You for these things, to confess to You and sing to Your name, most high. For You are God, almighty and good, even if You had done nothing but this, which nobody but You could do: You whose oneness is the pattern of all things; who out of Your own beauty make all things beautiful; who order all things by Your law. This age, then, Lord, which I have no memory of, which I take on other people's word and guess at from other babies — true though the guess may be — I am still reluctant to count as part of this life of mine that I live in this world. It is hidden from me in the shadows of forgetting, exactly as the time I spent in my mother's womb is. But if I was shaped in wickedness, and my mother conceived me in sin, then where, I beg You, my God — where, Lord, or when, was I, Your servant, ever innocent? Still, I pass that period by. What have I to do now with a time I can recall no trace of?

Leaving infancy behind, I came to boyhood — or rather it came to me, and pushed infancy out. Not that infancy went away, for where would it have gone? And yet it was no longer there. I was no longer a speechless infant but a talking boy. This I do remember, and I have worked out since how I learned to talk. It was not that my elders taught me words by any set method, the way they taught me other things later. It was that I longed to express what I was thinking, by cries and broken noises and various movements of my limbs, so as to get my own way; and being unable to express everything I wanted, or to the person I wanted, I practised the sounds in my memory with the mind You had given me, my God. When people named something and turned towards it as they spoke, I saw it and understood that the thing they were pointing out was called by the sound they made. And that they meant this thing and not another was obvious from the movement of their bodies — the natural language of all nations, made up of expression, glances, gestures and tone of voice, which shows what the mind is feeling as it goes after something, or holds it, or refuses it, or runs from it. So by hearing words over and over as they turned up in different sentences, I gradually collected what they stood for; and having broken my mouth in to these signs, I used them to say what I wanted. In this way I traded the current signs of our wants with the people around me, and launched further out into the stormy business of human life — still depending, of course, on the authority of my parents and the nod of my elders.

My God, my God, what miseries and mockeries I met with then, when as a boy I was told that the right thing was to obey my teachers, so that I might get on in this world and excel in the science of the tongue, which serves to win the praise of men and money that deceives. Next I was sent to school to get an education, though I had no idea, poor wretch, what it was good for; and yet if I was lazy at my lessons I was beaten. Our forefathers thought this the right way, and plenty of people had gone the same road before us and laid out these weary paths, which we were made to walk, multiplying toil and misery for the sons of Adam. But, Lord, we found that people called on You, and we learned from them to think of You — as far as we were able — as somebody great, who could hear us and help us, though hidden from our senses. So as a boy I began to pray to You, my help and my refuge, and I loosened my tongue to call on You, and I prayed, small as I was, with no small earnestness, not to be beaten at school. And when You did not hear me — which was not abandoning me to my folly — my elders, and even my parents, who wished me no harm at all, laughed at my beatings, which were my great and grievous trouble.

Is there anyone, Lord, of a soul so large, so passionately attached to You — for a kind of stupidity will do it after a fashion, but I mean anyone made so great-hearted by devotion to You — that he can think as lightly of the racks and hooks and other tortures that people all over the world beg You in terror to be spared, and laugh at those who dread them most bitterly, as our parents laughed at the torments we suffered from our schoolmasters as boys? For we were no less afraid of ours, and prayed to You no less to escape them. And still we sinned, by writing or reading or studying less than we were set. It was not that we lacked memory or ability, Lord; Your will had given us plenty for our age. It was that our whole delight was in play — and for that we were punished by people who were doing exactly the same. But an adult's idleness is called business; a boy's, which is the same thing, gets him punished by those adults, and nobody feels sorry for either the boys or the men. For would anyone with any sense approve of my being beaten as a boy because playing ball slowed my progress in studies that were to fit me, as a man, for playing at something less becoming? And what else was the man doing who beat me? If he came off worse in some trivial argument with a fellow teacher, he was more embittered and jealous than I was on losing a ball game to a playmate.

And yet I did sin in this, Lord God, orderer and creator of everything in nature — orderer only, of sin. I sinned, Lord my God, by disobeying the commands of my parents and my teachers. Whatever their motive, what they wanted me to learn I might have put to good use later. I disobeyed, not because I had chosen something better, but out of love of play: I loved the pride of winning my contests, and having my ears tickled with lying stories so that they itched all the more; and the same curiosity kept flashing brighter in my eyes for the shows and games of my elders. And those who put on such shows are held in such esteem that almost everyone wants the same for their children — while being perfectly willing to have them beaten if the games get in the way of the studies by which they hope the children will grow up to put such shows on. Look on all this with pity, Lord; and rescue us who call on You now; and rescue those too who do not yet call on You, so that they may call on You, and You may rescue them.

As a boy I had already heard about an eternal life promised to us through the humility of the Lord our God stooping down to our pride; and from my mother's womb — she hoped greatly in You — I was marked with the sign of his cross and seasoned with his salt. You saw, Lord, how once when I was still a boy I was seized by a sudden stomach complaint and was close to death. You saw, my God, because You were already my keeper, how eagerly and with what faith I begged for the baptism of Your Christ, my God and Lord, from the loving care of my mother and of Your Church, the mother of us all. And my mother in the flesh was greatly troubled, since with a heart pure in Your faith she was labouring even more lovingly to give birth to my salvation; she would have hurried to see me consecrated and cleansed by the healing sacraments, confessing You, Lord Jesus, for the forgiveness of sins — except that I suddenly recovered. So my cleansing was put off, on the grounds that if I lived I was bound to be polluted again, and that the stains of sin after that washing would carry greater and more dangerous guilt. I already believed by then; so did my mother and the whole household, except my father. But he did not defeat my mother's devotion in me, and win the point that because he did not yet believe, I should not believe either. She took great care that You, my God, should be my father rather than he was; and in this You helped her to prevail over her husband, whom she went on obeying, better woman though she was — obeying You in that too, since You have commanded it.

I beg You, my God, I would very much like to know, if You are willing to tell me: why was my baptism put off then? Was it for my good that the rein was let out, so to speak, and I was free to sin? Or was it not let out? If it was not, why do we still hear on every side, "Leave him alone, let him do what he likes, he is not baptised yet"? Whereas about bodily health nobody says, "Let him be wounded worse, he is not healed yet." How much better it would have been to be healed at once, and then, by my own efforts and my friends', to keep my soul's recovered health safe in Your keeping, since You gave it. Better indeed. But what great waves of temptation seemed to be hanging over me after boyhood! My mother could see them coming, and preferred to expose the raw clay to them, from which I might be moulded afterwards, rather than the finished cast.

In boyhood itself, though — so much less feared for me than adolescence was — I did not love study, and I hated being made to do it. But I was made to do it, and it was good for me, though I did not do well; because I would not have learned unless I had been forced. And nobody does well against his will, even if what he does is good in itself. Nor did the people forcing me do well either; the good came to me from You, my God. They cared nothing for what I would do with what they forced me to learn, except to satisfy the insatiable appetites of a rich poverty and a shameful glory. But You, who have numbered the very hairs of our head, used the error of everybody who pushed me to learn for my good — and used my own error, in refusing to learn, as my punishment, which was a fair penalty for so small a boy and so great a sinner. So by people who did wrong You did right by me; and by my own sin You justly punished me. For You have ordered it, and so it is, that every disordered love is its own punishment.

But why did I so hate Greek, which I studied as a boy? I still do not entirely know. I loved Latin — not what my first teachers taught, but what the so-called grammarians taught. Those first lessons, reading and writing and arithmetic, I found as much of a burden and a penalty as any Greek. And where did that come from, again, except the sin and vanity of this life, because I was flesh, and a breath that passes away and does not come back? Those first lessons were certainly the better ones, because they were the more certain; by them I gained, and still have, the power to read whatever I find written and to write whatever I want. Whereas in the others I was made to learn the wanderings of a man called Aeneas, while forgetting my own wanderings, and to weep for a dead Dido because she killed herself for love — while with dry eyes I put up with my own wretched self, dying among these things, far from You, God my life.

For what is more wretched than a wretch who has no pity on himself, and weeps at Dido's death for love of Aeneas but does not weep at his own death for want of love of You, God? Light of my heart, bread of the inmost part of my soul, power that gives vigour to my mind and quickens my thought: I did not love You. I was unfaithful to You, and all around me, in the same unfaithfulness, the voices echoed, "Well done! Well done!" For friendship with this world is unfaithfulness to You — and the "Well done! Well done!" goes on until a man is ashamed not to be like that. And for none of this did I weep, I who wept for Dido killed, "seeking with the sword the last extremity of her wound", while I myself was seeking a worse extremity: the lowest of Your creatures, having abandoned You, earth going back into earth. And if I was forbidden to read all this, I was miserable that I could not read what made me miserable. Madness like that is reckoned a higher and richer education than the one that taught me to read and write.

But now, my God, cry aloud in my soul, and let Your truth tell me: Not so, not so. That first study was far better. For I would far rather forget the wanderings of Aeneas and all the rest than forget how to read and write. There is a curtain hanging over the door of the grammar school, true enough. But it is less a symbol of anything mysterious than a cover for error. Let the people I no longer fear not shout me down, while I confess to You, my God, whatever my soul wants to confess, and consent to the condemnation of my bad ways in order to love Your good ones. Let neither the buyers nor the sellers of grammar shout me down. If I ask them whether it is true that Aeneas once came to Carthage, as the poet says, the less educated will answer that they do not know and the better educated that he never did. But if I ask what letters the name "Aeneas" is spelled with, everyone who has learned it will answer correctly, according to the signs people have agreed on among themselves. And if I ask which could be forgotten with the least damage to the business of living, reading and writing or these poetic fictions, who cannot see what everybody must answer, unless he has entirely forgotten himself? So I sinned as a boy in preferring the empty studies to the useful ones — or rather in loving the one and hating the other. "One and one is two, two and two is four" was a hateful singsong to me; while "the wooden horse packed with armed men", and "the burning of Troy", and "the sad ghost of Creusa" were the favourite show of my vanity.

Then why did I hate the Greek classics, which tell the same sort of tales? Homer too is a skilful weaver of such fictions, and is most sweetly empty, and yet he was bitter to my boyish taste. And I suppose Virgil would be the same to Greek children, made to learn him as I was made to learn Homer. The truth is that difficulty — the difficulty of a foreign language — poured gall over all the sweetness of Greek storytelling. I did not understand a word of it, and to make me understand I was driven hard with cruel threats and punishments. There was a time when as an infant I knew no Latin either; but I learned that without fear or suffering, simply by paying attention, among the caresses of my nurses and the jokes of the people around me, smiling and playfully egging me on. I learned it with no punishment pressing me forward, because my own heart pressed me to give birth to what was in it — which I could only do by learning words, not from the people who taught but from the people who talked to me, and in whose ears I in turn gave birth to whatever I was thinking. So there is no doubt that free curiosity has more power to make us learn these things than terrified compulsion. Only, compulsion restrains the wanderings of that freedom, by Your laws, my God — Your laws, running from the schoolmaster's cane to the martyr's trials, which are able to mix a wholesome bitterness for us, calling us back to Yourself from the deadly sweetness that lures us away.

Hear my prayer, Lord. Do not let my soul give way under Your discipline, and do not let me give way in confessing to You all the mercies by which You have drawn me out of my worst ways, so that You might become a delight to me above all the attractions I once chased; so that I may love You completely, and take hold of Your hand with all my heart, and You may rescue me from every temptation right to the end. For look, Lord, my king and my God: let whatever useful thing I learned as a child be for Your service — that I speak, write, read, count, let it be for Your service. For You gave me Your discipline while I was learning trivialities, and You have forgiven my sin of enjoying them. In them, it is true, I learned many a useful word — but those can be learned just as well in things that are not trivial, and that is the safe road for a young person's feet.

But woe to you, torrent of human custom! Who can stand against you? How long before you run dry? How long will you go on rolling the sons of Eve into that huge, hideous ocean, which even the ones who climb the cross barely get across? Did I not read in you about Jove the thunderer and adulterer? He could hardly be both — but the invented thunder was there to give cover and encouragement to the real adultery. And now which of our gowned professors will listen soberly to a man from their own school who cries out, "These were Homer's fictions, transferring human things to the gods; if only he had brought divine things down to us"? Though it would have been truer to say: "These are indeed his fictions — but by giving a divine nature to wicked men he made crimes stop being crimes, so that anybody committing them could seem to be imitating not degraded men but the gods in heaven."

And still, you hellish torrent, the sons of men are thrown into you with rich rewards for mastering such learning. A great ceremony is made of it when it is done in the forum, in full view of laws granting a salary on top of the pupils' fees — and you lash your rocks and roar, "This is where words are learned! This is where you get the eloquence you must have to win your case or defend your position!" As if we should never have known words like "golden shower", "lap", "seduce", "the temples of heaven", and the rest of that passage, unless Terence had put a dissolute young man on the stage holding Jupiter up as his model for seduction:

	Looking at a picture where the story was painted
	of Jove coming down in a shower of gold
	into Danaë's lap, to seduce a woman.

And then see how he works himself up to lust as if on heavenly authority:

	And what god? Great Jove,
	who shakes the highest temples of heaven with his thunder,
	and I, a poor mortal man, not do the same?
	I did it, and I did it with all my heart.

Not one word is learned any more easily for all this filth; but by means of the words the filth is committed with less shame. I do not blame the words themselves, which are like choice and precious vessels — I blame the wine of error that drunken teachers pour out to us in them, and if we will not drink it too we are beaten, with no sober judge to appeal to. Yet, my God, in whose presence I can now remember this without being hurt by it, I learned all this willingly, unhappily, with great delight, and for it I was pronounced a boy of promise.

Bear with me, my God, while I say something about my cleverness, which was Your gift, and about the follies I wasted it on. A task was set me, troublesome enough to my soul, with praise or disgrace at stake and a beating to fear: to speak the words of Juno as she raged and grieved that she could not

	turn this Trojan prince away from Italy.

I had heard that Juno never said any such words; but we were made to go wandering after these poetic fictions, and to say in prose roughly what the poet had said in verse. And the boy who was applauded most was the one in whom rage and grief stood out most, dressed in the most fitting language and keeping up the dignity of the character. What is that to me, my true life, my God — that my recitation was applauded above so many others of my own age and class? Is it not all smoke and wind? Was there nothing else to exercise my wits and my tongue on? Your praises, Lord, Your praises might have propped up the still tender shoot of my heart with the support of Your Scriptures, and it would not have trailed away among these empty trifles, a dirtied prey for the birds of the air. For there is more than one way of sacrificing to the rebel angels.

But is it any wonder that I was carried off into trivialities and went away from Your presence, my God, when the models set in front of me were men who, if they made a grammatical slip in describing some action of their own that was not in itself wicked, were criticised and ashamed — but who, when they described their own disordered lives in rich, well-ordered, ornamented language, were praised and gloried in it? You see all this, Lord, and keep quiet; slow to anger, and full of mercy and truth. Will You keep quiet for ever? Even now You are drawing out of this horrible pit the soul that seeks You, that thirsts for Your delights, whose heart says to You, I have sought Your face; Your face, Lord, will I seek. For to have one's love darkened is to be far away from You. It is not with our feet, or by changing place, that people leave You or come back to You. Did that younger son of Yours look for horses or carriages or ships, or fly away on visible wings, or travel by moving his legs, in order to waste in riotous living, in a far country, everything You gave him when he left? You were a loving father when You gave it, and more loving still when he came back with nothing. So it is in lustful, that is to say darkened, love that the real distance from Your face consists.

Look, Lord God — look patiently, as You always do — at how carefully the sons of men keep the agreed rules about letters and syllables handed down to them by those who spoke before, while neglecting the eternal covenant of everlasting salvation handed down from You. So much so that a man teaching or learning the inherited laws of pronunciation will offend people more by dropping his aitch and saying "a uman being", against the laws of grammar, than if he, a human being, hates a human being, against Your law. As if any enemy could do more harm than the hatred with which he is inflamed against him; or could wound the man he persecutes more deeply than he wounds his own soul by his hostility. Certainly no knowledge of letters can be so deeply rooted in us as the record of conscience — that he is doing to another what he would hate to have done to himself. How deep Your ways are, God, the only great one, sitting silent on high and by a tireless law dealing out a punishing blindness to lawless desires. Chasing a reputation for eloquence, a man standing before a human judge, surrounded by a human crowd, denouncing his enemy with the fiercest hatred, will take the greatest care not to murder the word "human being" by a slip of the tongue — and take no care at all not to murder a real human being in the fury of his heart.

This was the world at whose gate I lay, unhappy, in my boyhood; this was the arena where I was more afraid of committing a grammatical error than, having committed one, of envying those who had not. These things I say and confess to You, my God; and for these things I was praised by the people whom I then thought it the whole of virtue to please. For I could not see the pit of foulness I had been thrown into, away from Your eyes. In their sight, what was fouler than I already was, displeasing even people like myself, deceiving my tutor and my teachers and my parents with countless lies, out of love of play and eagerness to watch pointless shows and restlessness to copy them? I stole, too, from my parents' cellar and table, driven by greed, or so that I would have something to give the boys who sold me their games — games they enjoyed just as much as I did. And in these games I often cheated my way to victory, conquered myself all the while by a vain desire to be first. And what could I bear less, and denounce more fiercely when I caught somebody at it, than the very thing I was doing to others? And when I was caught and denounced myself, I would rather pick a fight than give in. Is this the innocence of boyhood? It is not, Lord, it is not; I beg Your mercy, my God. These very same sins, as the years go on, simply move from tutors and teachers to magistrates and kings, from nuts and balls and pet sparrows to gold and estates and slaves — exactly as harsher punishments take the place of the cane. It was the small stature of childhood that You, our king, praised as an emblem of humility, when You said, Of such is the kingdom of heaven.

Yet, Lord, to You, creator and governor of the universe, most excellent and most good, thanks would still be due, our God, even if You had meant me to have boyhood and nothing more. For even then I existed, I was alive, I felt; I had a built-in care for my own well-being, a trace of that mysterious oneness I came from. I guarded the wholeness of my senses by an inward sense; and in these tiny pursuits, and in my thoughts about tiny things, I learned to take delight in truth, I hated being deceived, I had a strong memory, I was given the gift of speech, I was comforted by friendship, I avoided pain and lowness and ignorance. In so small a creature, what was not remarkable, what was not admirable? But all of it is my God's gift; I did not give it to myself. And these things are good, and all of them together are me. So he who made me is good, and he is my good; and I will rejoice before him for every good thing I had as a boy. It was my sin that I looked for pleasures and heights and truths not in him but in his creatures — in myself and others — and so fell headlong into sorrows and confusions and errors. Thanks be to You, my joy and my glory and my confidence, my God; thanks be to You for Your gifts. Only keep them for me. For then You will keep me, and the things You have given me will be enlarged and made perfect, and I myself will be with You, since even my existing is Your gift.

Book Two: The Pear Tree

Now I want to call to mind my past foulness, and the bodily corruptions of my soul — not because I love them, but so that I may love You, my God. I do it for love of Your love, going back over my worst ways in the very bitterness of remembering them, so that You may grow sweet to me: sweetness that never fails, blissful and certain sweetness. I do it to be gathered up again out of the scattering in which I was torn to pieces, when I turned away from You, the one good, and lost myself among a multitude of things. For there was a time in my youth when I burned to be filled full with things down here, and I dared to run wild with all these shadowy loves; my beauty wasted away and I stank in Your sight — while pleasing myself, and anxious to be pleasing in the sight of men.

And what was it I took delight in, except loving and being loved? But I did not keep to the proper measure of love, mind to mind, which is the bright boundary of friendship. Out of the muddy craving of the flesh and the bubbling up of adolescence, a fog rose that clouded my heart over, so that I could not tell the clear brightness of love from the mist of lust. Both were boiling together in me confusedly and sweeping my unsteady youth over the cliff of unholy desires, sinking me in a pit of shameful things. Your anger had gathered over me and I did not know it. I had gone deaf from the clanking of the chain of my mortality, which was the punishment for my soul's pride; and I wandered further from You, and You let me alone, and I was thrown about and squandered and scattered and boiled over in my promiscuity — and You kept quiet, my slow-coming joy. You kept quiet then, and I wandered further and further from You, into more and more barren seedbeds of sorrow, with a proud despondency and a restless exhaustion.

If only somebody had brought my disorder into some kind of order then, and made good use of the fleeting beauty of these lowest things You have made — had set a limit to their pleasure, so that the tides of my youth might have broken on the shore of marriage, if they could not be calmed. If they could have been kept inside the purpose of raising a family, as Your law prescribes, Lord — You who shape the offspring of this our death, and are able with a gentle hand to blunt the thorns that were shut out of Your paradise. For Your all-powerfulness is not far from us, even when we are far from You. Otherwise I ought to have paid closer attention to the voice from the clouds: Nevertheless such shall have trouble in the flesh, but I spare you. And, It is good for a man not to touch a woman. And, He that is unmarried thinks of the things of the Lord, how he may please the Lord; but he that is married cares for the things of this world, how he may please his wife.

I should have listened to those words more carefully; and if I had made myself a eunuch for the kingdom of heaven's sake, I would have waited more happily for Your embrace. But I, poor wretch, foamed like a rough sea, following the rush of my own tide, abandoning You, and I overran every limit You had set — and I still did not escape Your whip. What mortal ever does? You were always with me, mercifully severe, sprinkling the bitterest additive over all my forbidden pleasures, so that I might go looking for a pleasure with nothing bitter in it. And where to find one I could not discover, except in You, Lord, who teach by pain, and wound in order to heal, and kill us so that we shall not die away from You. Where was I, and how far was I exiled from the delights of Your house, in the sixteenth year of the age of my flesh, when the madness of lust took charge of me — the madness human shamelessness gives free licence to, though Your laws do not — and I handed myself over to it completely? Meanwhile my family took no trouble to save me from falling by marrying me off. Their only concern was that I should learn to speak excellently and be a persuasive public speaker.

That year my studies were interrupted. I had come back from Madaura, a nearby town where I had gone to learn grammar and rhetoric, and money was being put together for a longer journey to Carthage — put together more by my father's determination than by his means, since he was only a poor freeman of Thagaste. Whom am I telling this to? Not to You, my God; but in Your presence I am telling it to my own kind, to whatever small part of the human race may come across these writings of mine. And why? So that anyone who reads this may consider out of what depths we have to cry to You. And what is nearer to Your ears than a confessing heart and a life of faith? Everybody praised my father for providing his son with everything necessary for a long journey for the sake of his studies, well beyond what he could afford; plenty of far better-off citizens did nothing of the kind for their children. And yet that same father had no concern at all about how I was growing towards You, or whether I was chaste — so long as I was fluent in speech, however barren I might be of Your cultivation, God, who are the one true and good owner of Your field, which is my heart.

But while I was living with my parents in that sixteenth year, out of school for a time because a period of idleness had been forced on us by my parents' narrow finances, the briars of unclean desire grew up over my head, and there was no hand to root them out. When my father saw me at the baths, growing towards manhood and full of a restless adolescence, he told my mother about it gladly, as though already anticipating grandchildren — delighting in that upheaval of the senses by which the world forgets You its creator and falls in love with what You made instead of with You, drunk on the invisible wine of its own self-will, turning aside and bowing down to the very lowest things. But in my mother's heart You had already begun Your temple and laid the foundations of Your holy dwelling, while my father was still only a catechumen, and a recent one. So she was startled into a holy fear and trembling; and though I was not yet baptised, she was afraid for me of the crooked paths walked by those who turn their backs to You and not their faces.

How wretched I am — and do I dare say that You kept quiet, my God, while I wandered further from You? Did You really keep quiet then? And whose were those words if not Yours, which You sang in my ears through my mother, Your faithful servant? Not one of them sank into my heart deep enough to act on. She wanted, and I remember her warning me privately with great anxiety, that I should not commit fornication, and above all should never touch another man's wife. This struck me as women's advice, which I should have been embarrassed to take. But it was Yours, and I did not know it; I thought You were silent and it was she who was speaking — and it was through her that You were not silent to me, and in her that I, her son, the son of Your handmaid, Your servant, despised You. But I did not know it, and I ran headlong, so blind that among my equals I was ashamed of not being shameless enough. I would hear them boasting of their disgraces, and the more degraded they were the more they boasted; and I took pleasure not only in the pleasure of the act but in the credit for it. What deserves blame except vice? But I made myself out worse than I was, so as not to be blamed; and where I had not sinned as far as the worst of them, I would claim to have done things I had not done, so as not to seem contemptible in proportion as I was innocent, or to count for less the chaster I was.

Look at the company I walked the streets of Babylon in, wallowing in its mud as though in a bed of spices and precious ointments. And to fasten me tighter to its very centre, my invisible enemy trod me down and led me astray, because I was easy to lead. Even my mother in the flesh — who had already fled from the centre of Babylon, though she still moved slowly through its outskirts — even she, while she urged me to be chaste, did not act on what her husband had told her about me by trying to keep within the bounds of marriage what could not be cut away altogether. She did not act on what she felt was already dangerous and would be more dangerous later. She did not, because she was afraid that a wife would be a weight and a hindrance to my prospects. Not the hopes of the world to come, which my mother rested in You, but the hopes of an education, which both my parents were far too eager for me to get: my father because he had next to no thought of You and only empty ideas about me; my mother because she reckoned that the usual course of study would be no obstacle to reaching You, and might even help. That is my guess, at least, recalling as best I can what my parents were like. Meanwhile the reins were let out for me far past any reasonable severity, to spend my time in amusement and even in dissipation, at whatever I felt like. And over all of it hung a fog, cutting me off, my God, from the brightness of Your truth; and my wickedness burst out as if from sheer overfeeding.

Theft is punished by Your law, Lord, and by the law written in human hearts, which not even wickedness can rub out. What thief will put up with another thief? Not even a rich thief will put up with one who steals out of need. And yet I wanted to steal, and did it, driven by no hunger and no poverty, but out of a surfeit of doing well and a pampered appetite for wrongdoing. For I stole something I had plenty of already, and better. Nor did I want to enjoy what I stole; my enjoyment was in the theft, in the sin itself. There was a pear tree near our vineyard, loaded with fruit, tempting neither in colour nor in taste. A gang of us worthless young men went to shake it down and rob it late one night, having dragged our games out in the streets till then, as our poisonous custom was. We carried off huge loads — not to eat, but to throw to the pigs, after tasting a few. And we did it purely because we liked doing what we were not allowed to do. Look at my heart, God, look at my heart, which You had pity on at the bottom of the bottomless pit. Now let my heart tell You what it was after down there, that I should be gratuitously evil, with no motive for wickedness except wickedness itself. It was foul, and I loved it. I loved my own ruin. I loved my own failing — not the thing I failed over, but the failing itself. My soul was foul, falling away from Your firmament into utter destruction, seeking nothing through the disgrace but the disgrace itself.

For there is an attraction in beautiful bodies, in gold and silver, in everything; and in the touch of the body there is a strong sympathy, and every other sense has its own object properly suited to it. Worldly honour has its charm too, and the power of winning and of mastery, which is also where the thirst for revenge comes from. But we may not leave You, Lord, or turn aside from Your law, in order to get any of it. The life we live here has its own enchantment as well, through a certain proportion of its own and a correspondence with all the beautiful things down here. Human friendship, too, is sweetly binding, because of the unity it makes out of many souls. It is over all these things, and things like them, that sin gets committed — when through an immoderate leaning towards these goods of the lowest order, the better and higher goods are abandoned: You, our Lord God, and Your truth and Your law. These lower things have their delights, but not like my God who made everything, for it is in him that the righteous delight, and he is the joy of the upright in heart.

So when we ask why a crime was committed, we do not believe the answer unless it appears that there might have been some desire to get one of the lower goods we mentioned, or some fear of losing it. They are beautiful and attractive, after all, though compared with the higher and blessed goods they are cheap and low. A man has murdered another. Why? He loved his wife, or his property; or he wanted to rob him to live on; or he was afraid of losing something to him; or, having been wronged, he was on fire for revenge. Would anybody commit a murder for no reason at all, simply enjoying the murdering? Who would believe it? Even of that furious and savage man who is said to have been gratuitously evil and cruel, a reason is given: so that, as he put it, his hand and his heart should not go soft through idleness. And to what end? So that by practising crime he might take the city and get office and power and money, and be free of his fear of the laws and of his difficulties from debts at home and from knowing what he had done. So not even Catiline loved his own crimes; he loved something else, and it was for that he committed them.

What then did wretched I love in you, my theft, my deed of darkness, in that sixteenth year of my life? Lovely you were not, since you were a theft. But are you anything at all, that I should be talking to you? The pears we stole were lovely, because they were Your creation, most lovely of all, creator of all, good God, God, the highest good and my true good. Those pears were lovely; but it was not the pears my wretched soul wanted, since I had plenty of better ones, and I picked these only in order to steal. Once picked, I threw them away; my whole feast in them was my own sin, which I was pleased to enjoy. If any of those pears did go into my mouth, what sweetened it was the sin. And now, Lord my God, I ask what it was in that theft that delighted me — and look, there is no loveliness in it at all. I do not mean the loveliness there is in justice and wisdom; nor the loveliness in the human mind and memory and senses and animal life; nor the way the stars are glorious and beautiful in their courses, or the earth and the sea, full of unborn life, replacing what decays by what is born. Not even that false and shadowy beauty that belongs to the vices that deceive us.

For pride imitates height, when You alone are God, high above everything. Ambition — what does it want but honour and glory, when You alone are to be honoured above everything, and are glorious for ever? The cruelty of the powerful wants to be feared: but who is to be feared except God alone, out of whose power what can be wrenched or withheld, and when, and where, and how, and by whom? The caresses of the promiscuous want to be counted as love — yet nothing is more tender than Your love, and nothing is loved more wholesomely than Your truth, brighter and more beautiful than anything. Curiosity puts on the appearance of a desire for knowledge, when You supremely know everything. Even ignorance and stupidity dress themselves up under the names of simplicity and harmlessness, since nothing is found more single than You, and what is less harmful, since it is the sinner's own works that damage him? Laziness would like to be at rest: but what steady rest is there apart from the Lord? Extravagance wants to be called plenty and abundance: but You are the fullness and the never-failing plenty of pleasures that do not go bad. Wastefulness offers a shadow of generosity: but You are the most overflowing giver of every good thing. Greed wants to own many things, and You own everything. Envy argues about who is better: what is better than You? Anger looks for revenge: who takes revenge more justly than You? Fear startles at whatever is strange and sudden and endangers what it loves, and takes thought for its safety: but to You what is strange or sudden, and who separates from You what You love? Where is unshakeable safety except with You? Grief wastes away over things it has lost, the delight of its desires, because it wants nothing taken away from it — as nothing can be taken from You.

This is how the soul is unfaithful: when she turns away from You, looking outside You for what she will not find pure and unspoiled until she comes back to You. So everyone who goes far away from You and lifts himself up against You is imitating You in a twisted way. But even by imitating You like that, they show that You are the creator of the whole of nature, and that there is nowhere to retreat to that is altogether away from You. So what did I love in that theft? In what way was I even corruptly and perversely imitating my Lord? Did I want, at least by stealth, to do the opposite of Your law, because by power I could not — so that being a prisoner I might act out a crippled liberty, doing with impunity what was not permitted me, a darkened likeness of Your all-powerfulness? Look at Your servant, running away from his Lord and catching a shadow. What rottenness, what a monstrous life, what a depth of death: could I like a thing only because it was not allowed?

What shall I give back to the Lord, that my memory can go over these things and my soul not be terrified by them? I will love You, Lord, and thank You, and confess to Your name, because You have forgiven me these enormous and hateful deeds of mine. I put it down to Your grace and Your mercy that You have melted my sins away like ice. And I put down to Your grace as well every evil I did not do — for what might I not have done, when I loved a sin for its own sake? I confess that all of it has been forgiven me: both the evils I committed by my own will, and the ones I did not commit because You guided me. What man is there who, weighing up his own weakness, dares to credit his purity to his own strength, so as to love You the less, on the grounds that he needed less of the mercy by which You forgive the sins of those who turn to You? If anyone was called by You and followed Your voice and avoided the things he reads me recalling and confessing about myself, let him not laugh at me: I was sick and was cured by the same doctor whose help kept him from being sick, or from being as sick. Let him love You for that as much and more, since he can see that I was brought back from so deep a wasting of sin by the very one who kept him from wasting the same way.

What did I get out of those things then, wretched man, that I am now ashamed even to remember? Above all out of that theft, which I loved for the theft's own sake — and it was nothing in itself, which makes me the more miserable for loving it. And yet I would not have done it alone. That is what I was then, as I remember it: alone I would never have done it. So did I love the company of my accomplices as well as the theft? Then it was not the theft alone I loved — or rather it was nothing else I loved, because that circumstance of the company was nothing either. What is the truth about this? Who can teach me but the one who lights up my heart and uncovers its dark corners? What is it that has come into my mind to ask and turn over and think about? If I had loved the pears I stole and wanted to enjoy them, I could have done it alone, if simply committing the theft would have been enough for my pleasure; I would not have needed to inflame the itch of my desire by the excitement of accomplices. But since my pleasure was not in the pears, it was in the offence itself, and the company of fellow sinners is what produced it.

What was this feeling, then? Certainly it was too foul, and it was my misfortune to have it. But what was it? Who can understand his own errors? It was the joke — the way it tickled our hearts to fool people who had no idea what we were doing and would have hated it. Why then was my delight the kind I could not take alone? Because nobody normally laughs alone. Normally nobody does; and yet laughter does sometimes overcome a man by himself, with nobody at all beside him, if something very funny strikes his senses or his mind. Yet I would not have done this alone; alone I would never have done it. Look, my God, at the vivid memory of my soul, here in front of You: alone I would never have committed that theft, in which what pleased me was not what I stole but the stealing — and stealing alone would not have pleased me, so I would not have done it. What an unfriendly friendship, an inexplicable seducer of the mind, a greed for doing harm out of fun and mischief, a thirst for other people's loss with no appetite for my own gain or revenge! But when somebody says, "Come on, let's do it," we are ashamed not to be shameless.

Who can untangle that twisted, knotted thing? It is foul; I hate to think about it or look at it. But it is You I long for, righteousness and innocence, beautiful and attractive to all clean eyes, and satisfying without ever cloying. With You is complete rest and a life nothing can disturb. Whoever comes into You comes into the joy of his Lord, and shall not be afraid, and shall be at his best in the one who is best of all. I fell away from You and wandered, my God, much too far astray from You, my support, in those days of my youth, and I became to myself a barren land.

Book Three: Carthage, the Theatre, and the Manichees

I came to Carthage, where a cauldron of unholy loves sang all around my ears. I was not yet in love, but I was in love with being in love, and out of a deep-seated need I hated myself for not needing more. I looked for something to love, being in love with loving; and I hated safety, and a road without traps. Inside me there was a famine of that inward food which is You, my God — and yet the famine did not make me hungry. I had no appetite at all for food that does not go bad, not because I was full of it but because the emptier I got the more I loathed it. And so my soul was sick and covered in sores, and it flung itself outside, desperate to be scratched by the touch of things it could feel. Yet if these things had no souls in them, they would not be objects of love. To love and to be loved was sweet to me, and sweeter still when I got to enjoy the person I loved. So I dirtied the spring of friendship with the filth of lust, and clouded its brightness over with the hell of appetite; and foul and ugly as I was, I still wanted, out of enormous vanity, to be thought elegant and sophisticated. I fell headlong into the love I had been longing to be caught by. My God, my mercy, out of Your great goodness how much bitterness You sprinkled over that sweetness for me! For I was loved in return, and I secretly reached the point of enjoying it; and I was joyfully tied down with chains that brought misery, so that I could be beaten with the red-hot rods of jealousy and suspicion and fear and anger and quarrelling.

The theatre carried me away as well, full of pictures of my own miseries and fuel for my fire. Why is it that a man wants to be made sad by watching tragic and terrible things that he would not put up with for a moment himself? And yet he wants to feel grief at them as a spectator, and that very grief is his pleasure. What is this but a miserable madness? For the less free a man is from such feelings himself, the more these performances affect him. When he suffers in his own person we usually call it misery; when he feels for other people we call it compassion. But what sort of compassion is this, for staged and invented sufferings? The listener is not being called on to help, only to grieve — and the more he grieves the more he applauds the actor who is inventing it. And if the disasters of those characters, whether ancient or made up, are acted so badly that the spectator is not moved to tears, he leaves disgusted and complaining; whereas if he is moved, he stays riveted and weeps with enjoyment.

So griefs are loved as well? Surely everybody wants joy. Or is it that although nobody likes being miserable himself, he does like being merciful — and since mercy cannot exist without feeling pain, that is the only reason such pains are loved? This too springs from that same vein of friendship. But where does that vein go? Where does it flow to? Why does it run off into a boiling torrent of pitch, throwing up those monstrous tides of foul appetite, into which it deliberately changes itself, flinging itself down of its own will from its heavenly clarity? Should compassion be got rid of, then? By no means. So griefs may sometimes be loved. But beware of uncleanness, my soul, under the protection of my God, the God of our fathers, who is to be praised and lifted high above everything for ever — beware of uncleanness. I have not stopped pitying people now. But in those days, in the theatres, I rejoiced with lovers when they wickedly enjoyed one another, though it was only make-believe on stage; and when they lost each other I grieved with them, as though full of compassion — and enjoyed myself either way. Now I pity far more the man who is delighted with his own wickedness than the man who is supposed to be suffering hardship by missing out on some destructive pleasure and losing some wretched happiness. That is certainly the truer mercy, but there is no delight in the grief of it. For although a man who grieves for the miserable is to be commended for his charity, a genuinely compassionate person would much rather there were nothing to grieve about. If goodwill could be ill-willed, which it never can, then a man who truly pities others might wish there were some miserable people about for him to pity. So some grief may be allowed; none may be loved. That is how You do it, Lord God, who love souls far more purely than we do and pity them more incorruptibly, and are yet wounded by no grief. And who is equal to that?

But I in my misery loved grieving in those days and went looking for things to grieve at; and in somebody else's suffering, invented and acted, the performance that pleased me best and drew me hardest was the one that made me cry. What wonder that an unhappy sheep, straying from Your flock and impatient of Your keeping, should catch a foul disease? Hence the love of griefs — not the kind that sink deep into me, since I did not want to suffer what I liked to look at, but the kind that scratched the surface lightly when I heard the story. And as when nails are poisoned, what followed the scratching was inflammation and swelling and an infected sore. My life being like that, was it a life at all, my God?

And Your faithful mercy hovered over me from far off. On what dreadful wickednesses I wore myself out, pursuing a sacrilegious curiosity which, now that it had abandoned You, was to bring me down to the treacherous depths and the cheating service of devils, to whom I sacrificed my bad actions — and in all this You were whipping me. I even dared, while Your solemn services were being celebrated inside the walls of Your church, to want and to arrange a piece of business whose fruits deserved death; and for that You whipped me with heavy punishments, though nothing next to what I deserved, my enormous mercy, my God, my refuge from those terrible destroyers among whom I wandered with a stiff neck, going further and further from You, loving my own ways and not Yours — loving a runaway liberty.

The studies that were reckoned respectable had their eye on excelling in the law courts: the more praised, the more crooked. Such is human blindness, glorying even in its blindness. By now I was top of the school of rhetoric, and I was proudly delighted with it and swollen with arrogance — though, Lord, You know, I was far quieter and had nothing at all to do with the wreckings of the "Wreckers", as that ill-omened and devilish name was the very badge of style among the people I lived with. I lived among them with a shameless shame at not being like them. I lived with them and sometimes enjoyed their friendship, while always detesting what they did — their "wreckings", by which they wantonly persecuted the modesty of newcomers, breaking in on them with gratuitous mockery and feeding their own malicious nature on it. Nothing could be more like the actual behaviour of devils. So what could they more truly be called than "Wreckers"? They were wrecked themselves and completely perverted first, with the deceiving spirits secretly laughing at them and leading them on, in the very thing they enjoy — mocking and deceiving others.

Among people like this, at that unsettled time of my life, I studied books of rhetoric, in which I wanted to be outstanding, for a damnable and boastful purpose: delight in human vanity. In the ordinary course of study I came across a book of Cicero's, whose language nearly everybody admires, though not his heart. That book of his is an exhortation to philosophy, and is called the Hortensius. But this book changed what I felt. It turned my prayers towards You, Lord, and gave me different aims and desires. Every empty hope became worthless to me at a stroke, and I longed with an incredible burning of the heart for the immortality that wisdom gives, and began to get up and go back to You. It was not to sharpen my tongue that I took up that book — which sharpening seemed to be what I was buying with my mother's allowance, in my nineteenth year, my father having died two years before. It was not to sharpen my tongue: what got into me from it was not its style but its content.

How I burned then, my God, how I burned to fly back up from earthly things to You — and I did not know what You meant to do with me. For with You is wisdom. But love of wisdom is called philosophy in Greek, and it was philosophy this book set me on fire for. There are people who use philosophy to lead others astray, colouring and disguising their own errors with a great, smooth, respectable name; and almost everyone of that kind, in Cicero's time and before it, is criticised and exposed in that book. It also makes plain that wholesome warning of Your Spirit, given through Your good and devoted servant: Beware lest any man spoil you through philosophy and empty deceit, after the tradition of men, after the rudiments of the world, and not after Christ. For in him dwells all the fullness of the Godhead bodily. And since at that time — You know it, light of my heart — the writings of the Apostles were unknown to me, my delight in that exhortation went only so far as this: that it strongly roused and kindled and inflamed me to love and seek and win and hold and embrace, not this school or that, but wisdom itself, whatever it might be. And one thing alone checked me in the middle of this blaze: that the name of Christ was not in it. For by Your mercy, Lord, this name of my saviour, Your Son, my young heart had devoutly drunk in with my mother's milk and stored up deep; and whatever lacked that name, however learned and polished and true, could not take complete hold of me.

So I decided to turn my mind to the holy Scriptures, to see what they were like. And look: I see something the proud do not understand and that is not laid open to children — humble to approach, lofty in its inner rooms, and veiled in mystery. And I was not the sort who could get inside it, or bend my neck to follow where it went. For I did not feel then as I speak now when I turned to those Scriptures. They struck me as unworthy to be set beside the grandeur of Cicero. My swollen pride recoiled from their plainness, and my sharp wit could not get through to what was inside. Yet they were the kind of thing that grows up along with a little child. But I disdained to be a little child, and being puffed up with pride I took myself for a great man.

So I fell in among men who were proud and delirious, extremely fleshly and full of chatter, whose mouths were traps of the devil, limed with a mixture of the syllables of Your name and of our Lord Jesus Christ and of the Holy Spirit, our Comforter. These names never left their mouths — but only as sound and the noise of the tongue, for their hearts were empty of truth. Still they cried out "Truth! Truth!" and said a great deal to me about it, and it was not in them: they told lies, and not only about You, who truly are Truth, but even about the elements of this world, which are Your creatures. And I ought to have passed over even philosophers who told the truth about those elements, for love of You, my Father, supremely good, beauty of everything beautiful. Truth, Truth — how the marrow of my soul panted after You even then, when they kept sounding Your name at me in all sorts of ways, in many enormous books, and it was only an echo! And these were the dishes on which, while I was starving for You, they served me the sun and moon instead — beautiful works of Yours, but Your works and not You, and not even Your first works. For Your spiritual works come before these bodily ones, however heavenly and shining they are. But it was not even those first works of Yours I hungered and thirsted for; it was You Yourself, the Truth, in whom is no variableness, neither shadow of turning. And still they set before me on those dishes a set of glittering fantasies — and it would have been better to love this actual sun, which is at least real to our sight, than the fantasies that fool the mind through the eyes. But because I took them for You, I fed on them; not eagerly, since You did not taste to me in them as You are, because You were not those empty things; and they did not nourish me but wore me out. Food in a dream looks very like our food when awake, and yet sleepers get no nourishment from it, because they are asleep. But these things were not even in any way like You, as You have now spoken to me, for they were bodily fantasies, false bodies — and the true bodies we see with our physical sight, in the sky or on the earth, are far more certain than they are. Animals and birds can make out those as well as we can, and they are more certain than when we imagine them. And again, we imagine them more certainly than we can guess from them at other vaster and infinite bodies that have no existence at all. Those were the empty husks I was fed on then, and I was not fed. But You, love of my soul, in looking for whom I fail so that I may grow strong, are neither the bodies we see, even in heaven, nor the ones we do not see there; for You created them, and do not count them among the greatest of Your works. How far, then, are You from those fantasies of mine, fantasies of bodies that do not exist at all — when the images of bodies that do exist are far more certain, and the bodies themselves more certain still, which even so You are not; nor are You the soul, which is the life of bodies. So the life of bodies is better and more certain than the bodies. But You are the life of souls, the life of lives, having life in Yourself, and You do not change, life of my soul.

Where were You then, in relation to me, and how far off? Far indeed I was straying from You, shut out even from the husks of the pigs I was feeding with husks. For how much better are the fables of the poets and grammarians than these traps! Verses and poems and "Medea flying" are truly more useful than these men's five elements, variously disguised to answer to five dens of darkness, which do not exist and yet kill the man who believes in them. Verses and poems I can turn into real food; and as for "Medea flying", I might sing it but I did not maintain it, and I heard it sung but I did not believe it — whereas those other things I did believe. What steps I went down by into the depths of hell, toiling and churning for want of truth! For I sought You, my God — I confess it to You, who had mercy on me before I confessed anything — not with the understanding of the mind, by which You wanted me to be above the animals, but with the senses of the flesh. And You were more inward to me than my most inward part, and higher than my highest. I ran into that brazen woman, foolish and knowing nothing, who is pictured in Solomon, sitting at the door and saying, Eat bread of secrecy gladly, and drink stolen waters, which are sweet. She seduced me, because she found my soul living outdoors, in the eye of my flesh, chewing over the sort of food I had swallowed through it.

For I did not know anything else, anything that really is; and I was persuaded, by what felt like sharpness of wit, to agree with foolish deceivers when they asked me, "Where does evil come from?" and "Is God confined to a bodily shape, and does he have hair and nails?" and "Are men to be counted righteous who had several wives at once, and killed people, and sacrificed animals?" I was badly troubled by these questions in my ignorance, and while walking away from the truth I thought I was walking towards it — because I did not yet know that evil is nothing but the absence of good, right down to the point where a thing stops existing altogether. And how was I to see that, when the sight of my eyes reached only to bodies and the sight of my mind only to a phantom? And I did not know God to be a Spirit, not something with parts stretched out in length and breadth, or whose being was bulk; because every bulk is less in a part than in the whole, and if it is infinite it must be less in a part marked off by a certain space than in its infinity, and so is not wholly everywhere, as Spirit is, as God is. And what it was in us by which we resembled God, and could rightly be said to be made in the image of God, I had no idea at all.

Nor did I know that true inward righteousness which judges not by custom but by the most upright law of God almighty — the law by which the ways of particular places and times were arranged to suit those times and places, while the law itself stays the same always and everywhere, not one thing here and another there. By that law Abraham and Isaac and Jacob and Moses and David were righteous, and so were all the others whom God's own mouth commended; and they were judged unrighteous by silly people, judging by human standards and measuring the moral customs of the whole human race by their own small habits. It is as if a man in an armoury, not knowing what fitted which part of the body, put leg-armour on his head and tried to wear a helmet on his feet and then complained that they did not fit. Or as if, on a day when business is publicly closed in the afternoon, somebody were angry at not being allowed to keep his shop open, when he had been open in the morning. Or as if in one house he saw a servant handling something the butler is not allowed to touch, or something permitted outdoors that is forbidden in the dining room, and were angry that in one house and one family the same thing is not allowed everywhere and to everybody. Such are the people who are irritated to hear that something was lawful for righteous men in the past and is not now; or that God, for particular reasons of time, commanded those men one thing and these another, when both were obeying the same righteousness. They can see in one man, one day, one household, that different things suit different parts and different seasons and different persons, and that what was lawful before a certain time is not lawful after it, and that a thing permitted in one corner is rightly forbidden and punished in another. Is justice, then, variable and changeable? No; but the times over which it presides do not flow evenly, because they are times. Men, whose days on earth are few, cannot use their senses to harmonise the causes of things in earlier ages and other nations, which they have no experience of, with the ones they do have experience of; whereas in a single body or day or family they easily see what suits each part and season and person. So they take exception to the one and submit to the other.

These things I did not know then and did not notice. They struck my eye from every side and I did not see them. I wrote verses, in which I could not put every foot everywhere but had to put different feet in different metres, and could not even put the same foot in every position within a single metre. Yet the art itself, by which I wrote, did not have different principles for these different cases: it took them all in together. Still I could not see how that righteousness, which good and holy men obeyed, contained all the things God had commanded far more excellently and loftily in one, and varied in no part at all — although in varying times it did not prescribe everything at once, but allotted and required what was right for each. And in my blindness I criticised the holy fathers, not only where they were using what was to hand as God commanded and inspired them, but even where they were foretelling things to come, as God was revealing through them.

Can it ever, anywhere, be unjust to love God with all your heart and all your soul and all your mind, and your neighbour as yourself? Therefore those foul offences that are against nature are to be detested and punished everywhere and always, such as the offences of the men of Sodom — which, if all nations committed them, would leave them all guilty of the same crime by the law of God, who did not make men to abuse one another in that way. For even the dealing that ought to be between God and us is violated when the nature he is the author of is polluted by a perversion of lust. But actions that are offences against human custom are to be avoided according to whatever customs prevail in each place, so that something agreed and settled by the custom or law of any city or nation may not be broken by the lawless whim of anyone, native or foreigner. For any part that does not harmonise with its whole is offensive. But when God commands something to be done that goes against the customs or agreements of a people, it must be done, even if they have never done it before; and if it has lapsed, it must be restored; and if it was never established, it must be established now.
