For if it is lawful for a king in the state he rules to command something nobody before him and he himself had never commanded, and obeying him cannot be against the common good of the state — indeed it would be against the common good if he were not obeyed, since obeying rulers is a general agreement of human society — how much more unhesitatingly ought we to obey God in everything he commands, the ruler of all his creatures? Just as among the powers in human society the greater authority is obeyed in preference to the lesser, so God must be obeyed above all.

The same goes for acts of violence, where there is a wish to hurt, by insult or injury; and these are done either for revenge, as one enemy against another; or for some profit that belongs to somebody else, as a robber does to a traveller; or to avoid some harm, against a man one is afraid of; or out of envy, as a less fortunate man treats a more fortunate one, or a man doing well treats one whose coming level with him he fears or resents; or simply for the pleasure of another's pain, as with spectators at gladiator shows, or people who jeer at and mock others. These are the main headings of wickedness, which spring from the appetite of the flesh, or of the eye, or for power — either singly, or two together, or all three; and so men live badly against the three and the seven, that instrument of ten strings, Your Ten Commandments, God, most high and most sweet. But what foul offence can there be against You, who cannot be defiled? What act of violence against You, who cannot be harmed? What You avenge is what men commit against themselves; because even when they sin against You they are acting wickedly against their own souls, and wickedness gives itself the lie — either by corrupting and perverting the nature You created and ordered, or by an immoderate use of what is allowed, or by burning for what is not allowed, in a use that is against nature. Or they are found guilty of raging in heart and tongue against You, kicking against the goads; or of bursting out of the fence of human society and boldly delighting in self-willed factions and splits, according to whatever they have to gain or to take offence at. And all this is done when You are abandoned, fountain of life, the one true creator and governor of the universe, and when some single false thing is picked out of what You made and loved, out of self-willed pride. So it is by a humble devotion that we come back to You; and You cleanse us of our bad habits, and are merciful to the sins of those who confess, and hear the groaning of the prisoner, and loosen the chains we made for ourselves — as long as we do not raise against You the horns of an unreal liberty, losing everything through greed for more, by loving our own private good more than You, the good of all.

Among these offences of foulness and violence, and so many other wickednesses, there are the sins of men who are on the whole making progress; and those who judge rightly criticise these by the standard of perfection while commending the men themselves, in hope of future fruit, as one does with the green blade of growing corn. And there are things that look like offences of foulness or violence and are yet not sins at all, because they offend neither You, our Lord God, nor human society: when, for instance, things suitable to a particular time are obtained for the service of life, and we cannot tell whether it was out of an appetite for possessing; or when things are punished by a constituted authority for the sake of correction, and we cannot tell whether it was out of an appetite for hurting. So many an action disapproved of in men's sight is approved by Your testimony, and many that men praise are condemned with You as witness — because the look of the action, the mind of the man doing it, and the unknown needs of the moment all vary independently. But when You suddenly command something unaccustomed and unimagined, even something You once forbade, and keep the reason for Your command hidden for the time, and it goes against the rules of some human society — who doubts that it must be done, since the human society that is just is the one that serves You? But blessed are those who know Your commands! For everything Your servants did was either to show something needed at the time, or to foreshadow what was to come.

Being ignorant of all this, I mocked those holy servants and prophets of Yours. And what did I gain by mocking them, except to be mocked by You, drawn on imperceptibly, step by step, into such follies as believing that a fig tree wept when its fruit was picked, and that the mother tree shed milky tears? And yet if some Manichaean saint had eaten that fig — picked by somebody else's guilt and not his own — and mixed it with his insides, he would breathe angels out of it; indeed particles of divinity would burst out at every sigh and groan of his prayer, particles of the most high and true God, which would have stayed imprisoned in that fig if they had not been set free by the teeth and stomach of some "Elect" saint. And I, poor wretch, believed that more mercy was owed to the fruits of the earth than to the men they were created for. For if some hungry man who was not a Manichaean asked for a piece, the mouthful given to him would be as good as condemned to death.

And You reached down Your hand from above and pulled my soul out of that deep darkness, while my mother, Your faithful servant, wept to You for me more than mothers weep at the bodily deaths of their children. For by the faith and the spirit she had from You she could see the death I was lying in, and You heard her, Lord. You heard her and did not scorn her tears, when they streamed down and watered the ground under her eyes wherever she prayed. Yes, You heard her. For where else did that dream come from, by which You comforted her, so that she let me live with her and eat at the same table in the house — which she had begun to shrink from, hating and detesting the blasphemies of my error? She saw herself standing on a wooden measuring rule, and a shining young man coming towards her, cheerful and smiling at her, while she was grieving and overwhelmed with grief. He asked her the reasons for her grief and her daily tears — asking, as such messengers do, in order to teach rather than to be taught — and when she answered that she was mourning my ruin, he told her to be easy, and to look and see that where she was, I was too. And when she looked, she saw me standing beside her on the same rule. Where did this come from, except that Your ears were turned towards her heart? Good and all-powerful one, who care for every one of us as if You cared for him alone, and for all of us as if we were one!

And where did this come from too: that when she had told me the vision, and I tried to bend it to mean that she should not despair of one day being what I was, she replied at once, without any hesitation, "No. It was not said to me, 'Where he is, there you will be,' but 'Where you are, there he will be.'" I confess to You, Lord, that as far as I can remember — and I have often spoken of this — Your answer through my waking mother moved me even more at the time than the dream itself: that she was not thrown by the plausibility of my false reading, and saw so quickly what was there to be seen, which I certainly had not noticed before she spoke. The dream had signalled, so long in advance, a joy for that holy woman which was to be fulfilled so long afterwards, and was given to console her present anguish. For nearly nine years went by in which I wallowed in the mud of that deep pit and the darkness of falsehood, often trying to get up and being knocked down harder. All that time that chaste, devout, level-headed widow — the kind You love — now more cheerful with hope, but relaxing not at all in her weeping and mourning, never stopped lamenting my case to You at every hour of her prayers. And her prayers came into Your presence; and still You let me be tangled and re-tangled in that darkness.

Meanwhile You gave her another answer, which I remember; for I am passing over a great deal, hurrying on to the things that press me harder to confess to You, and much of it I do not remember. You gave her another answer, then, through a priest of Yours, a bishop brought up in Your church and well read in Your books. When this woman begged him to be so good as to talk to me, refute my errors, unteach me the bad things and teach me the good ones — which he was in the habit of doing when he found people fit to receive it — he refused, wisely, as I realised later. He answered that I was still unteachable, being puffed up with the novelty of that heresy, and had already tied several unskilled people in knots with trick questions, as she had told him. "Just leave him alone for a while," he said. "Only pray to God for him. He will find out by his own reading what that error is and how great its impiety." At the same time he told her how he himself, as a small boy, had been handed over to the Manichees by his own misled mother, and had not only read but frequently copied out almost all their books, and had seen — without anybody arguing him out of it — how much that sect was to be avoided, and had avoided it. When he had said this and she was still not satisfied but pressed him harder, with pleading and many tears, to see me and talk to me, he was a little annoyed at her persistence and said, "Go your way, and God bless you; it is not possible that the son of these tears should perish." And she took that answer, as she often mentioned when talking with me afterwards, as though it had sounded out of heaven.

Book Four: Friendship, Grief, and the Death of a Friend

For nine years, from my nineteenth to my twenty-eighth, we lived seduced and seducing, deceived and deceiving, in one appetite after another: openly, through the disciplines they call liberal; secretly, through a religion with a false name. Proud on the one side, superstitious on the other, and empty on both. On the one side chasing the emptiness of popular applause, right down to theatre ovations and poetry prizes and contests for wreaths of grass, and the follies of public shows and the intemperance of desire. On the other, wanting to be cleansed of those pollutions by carrying food to the men they called the "elect" and the "holy", out of which, in the workshop of their stomachs, they were to forge angels and gods for us, who might then do the cleansing. These were the things I chased and practised with my friends, who were deceived by me and along with me. Let the arrogant mock me, and everyone who has not been struck down and knocked flat by You for the health of his soul, my God — I will still confess my own shame to You in Your praise. Bear with me, I beg You, and give me the grace to go over the wanderings of my past in my present memory, and to offer You the sacrifice of thanksgiving. For what am I to myself without You but a guide to my own downfall? What am I even at my best but a baby sucking the milk You give, and feeding on You, the food that does not perish? What sort of thing is any man, seeing he is only a man? Let the strong and the powerful laugh at us; let us, poor and needy, confess to You.

In those years I taught rhetoric, and, mastered by greed, sold a talkativeness for mastering others. And yet, Lord, You know, I preferred students who were honest, as honesty is reckoned; and I taught them tricks without any tricks of my own — tricks not to be used against an innocent man's life, though sometimes to save a guilty one's. And You, God, saw me from far off, stumbling on that slippery road and sending out a few sparks of good faith through a great deal of smoke; and I showed those sparks in the way I guided people who loved emptiness and went looking for lies, being their companion in it. In those years I had a woman — not in what is called lawful marriage, but one I had found in a wayward passion with no understanding in it. But only one, and I stayed faithful to her. And in my own case I learned the difference between the self-restraint of the marriage covenant, entered into for the sake of children, and the bargain of a lustful love, where children are born against their parents' wishes — though once born they compel love.

I remember too that when I had decided to enter for a prize at the theatre, some fortune-teller asked me what I would pay him to win. I detested and loathed those foul mysteries, and answered, "Even if the wreath were of imperishable gold, I would not let a fly be killed to win it for me." For he was going to kill living creatures in his sacrifices, and by those honours invite the demons to favour me. But I rejected this evil too, and not out of any pure love for You, God of my heart; for I did not know how to love You, not knowing how to conceive of anything beyond a material brightness. And does not a soul sighing after such fictions commit adultery against You, trusting in unreal things and feeding on wind? Still, I did not want sacrifices offered to demons on my behalf — while by that superstition I was sacrificing myself to them. For what else is feeding on wind but feeding them, that is, becoming their entertainment and their joke by going astray?

Those frauds they call astrologers, then, I consulted without hesitation, because they seemed to use no sacrifices and to pray to no spirit for their divinations — an art which Christian and true piety nevertheless consistently rejects and condemns. For it is a good thing to confess to You and say, Have mercy on me, heal my soul, for I have sinned against You; and not to abuse Your mercy as a licence to sin, but to remember the Lord's words, See, you are made whole; sin no more, in case something worse happens to you. All of that wholesome advice they work to destroy, saying, "The cause of your sin is fixed inevitably in heaven," and "This was the doing of Venus, or Saturn, or Mars" — so that a man, flesh and blood and proud rottenness, may be blameless, while the creator and orderer of heaven and the stars takes the blame. And who is he but our God? The very sweetness and wellspring of righteousness, who repay every man according to his works, and will not despise a broken and contrite heart.

There was in those days a wise man, very skilled in medicine and famous for it, who had with his own proconsular hand set the winner's wreath on my diseased head — though not as a physician, for this disease is cured only by You, who resist the proud and give grace to the humble. But did You fail me even through that old man, or hold back from healing my soul? For I got to know him better and hung on his conversation steadily, since although his words were simple they were vivid and lively and in earnest; and when he had gathered from my talk that I was devoted to the books of the horoscope-casters, he advised me kindly, like a father, to throw them away and not waste on such empty things the care and diligence that useful work needed. He said he had studied that art in his earliest years, meaning to take it up as the profession he would live by; that if he could understand Hippocrates, he could soon have understood a study like this; and that he had nevertheless given it up and turned to medicine, for no other reason than that he had found it completely false — and being a serious man he would not make a living by deceiving people. "But you," he said, "have rhetoric to keep yourself by, so you are following this by choice and not from necessity. All the more reason to believe me about it, since I worked to master it well enough to live by it and nothing else." When I asked him how so many true things could be predicted by it, he answered as best he could: that the force of chance, spread through the whole order of things, brought it about. For if when a man opens a book of poetry at random — a poet who was thinking and writing about something completely different — a line often turns up that fits the present business remarkably well, it would be no wonder if out of a man's soul, which does not know what is going on inside it, some higher instinct should produce an answer matching the affairs and actions of the person asking: by luck, not by art.

And this much, from him or through him, You conveyed to me and traced in my memory, for me to examine for myself later. But at the time neither he nor my dearest Nebridius, an exceptionally good young man with a holy fear in him, who laughed at the whole business of divination, could persuade me to throw it out. The authority of the writers still swayed me, and I had as yet found no certain proof, of the kind I was looking for, that would show beyond all doubt that what had been truly predicted by the people consulted was the result of chance and not of the star-gazers' art.

In the years when I first began teaching rhetoric in my home town, I had made a friend, too dear to me, out of a shared set of interests: a man of my own age, and like me in the first opening flower of youth. He had grown up with me from childhood, and we had been schoolfellows and playfellows. But he was not yet my friend in the way he became later, nor even then in the way that true friendship is; for friendship cannot be true unless You cement it together, in people who hold fast to You by the love which is poured into our hearts by the Holy Spirit, who is given to us. And yet it was all too sweet, ripened in the warmth of shared studies. For I had twisted him away from the true faith, which as a young man he had not thoroughly taken in, and towards those superstitious and destructive fables that my mother wept over me for. Now his mind went wrong along with mine, and my soul could not be without him. But look: You were close behind Your runaways, God of vengeance and fountain of mercies at once, turning us back to Yourself by remarkable means. You took that man out of this life when he had barely completed a single year of my friendship, a friendship sweeter to me than all the sweetness of that life of mine.

Who can recount all Your praises, which he has felt in his own single self? What did You do then, my God, and how unsearchable is the depth of Your judgements? For he was long and badly sick with a fever, and lay unconscious in a death sweat; and when his recovery was despaired of, he was baptised without knowing it. I meanwhile hardly cared, assuming that his soul would keep what it had got from me rather than what had been done to his unconscious body. But it turned out quite otherwise: he revived and recovered. As soon as I could speak to him — and I could as soon as he was able, since I never left him and we depended far too much on each other — I tried to joke with him, expecting him to join me in laughing at the baptism he had received while completely absent in mind and feeling, but had now been told about. But he recoiled from me as from an enemy, and with a remarkable and sudden independence told me that if I wanted to remain his friend I would stop talking to him like that. Astonished and confused, I held back everything I felt until he should get well and be strong enough for me to handle him as I wished. But he was taken away from my madness, to be kept safe with You for my consolation. A few days later, while I was away, he was attacked by the fever again, and died.

At this grief my heart went completely dark, and everything I looked at was death. My own country was a torture to me and my father's house a strange misery; and everything I had shared with him became, without him, an agony. My eyes looked for him everywhere and did not get him; and I hated every place, because he was not in it, and because they could not say to me, "He is coming," as they had when he was alive and away. I became a great riddle to myself, and I asked my soul why she was so sad and why she troubled me so badly — and she did not know what to answer. And if I said, Trust in God, she quite rightly did not obey me; because the dearest friend she had lost was, being a man, both truer and better than the phantom she was being told to trust. Only tears were sweet to me, for they had taken my friend's place in the dearest of my affections.

And now, Lord, these things are past and time has taken the edge off my wound. Let me learn from You, who are the truth, and bring the ear of my heart up to Your mouth, so that You may tell me why weeping is sweet to the wretched. Have You, though present everywhere, thrown our misery far away from You? You stay in Yourself, while we are thrown about among all sorts of trials. And yet unless we did our mourning in Your hearing, we should have no hope left. So where does sweet fruit come from, gathered out of the bitterness of life, out of groaning and tears and sighs and complaints? Is it hoping that You hear us that sweetens it? That is true of prayer, since there is a longing in prayer to come close to You. But is it also true of grief for something lost, and the sorrow I was drowning in then? For I did not hope he would come back to life, and I did not ask for that with my tears. I only wept and grieved. I was miserable, and I had lost my joy. Or is weeping really a bitter thing, which pleases us out of sheer disgust at what we used to enjoy, at the moment when we recoil from it?

But why am I talking about this? Now is no time for questions but for confessing to You. I was wretched, and every soul is wretched that is tied down by love of things that die. It is torn apart when it loses them, and only then does it feel the wretchedness it was in before it lost them. So it was with me then. I wept most bitterly and found my rest in bitterness. That was how wretched I was, and I held that wretched life dearer than my friend. For although I would gladly have changed it, I was still less willing to give it up than to give him up — indeed I do not know whether I would have given it up even for him, as the story goes, if it is not invented, about Pylades and Orestes, who would gladly have died for each other or together, since living apart was worse to them than death. But something had risen in me that was quite the opposite of this and that I cannot explain: I loathed living and was afraid to die, both at once. I suppose the more I loved him the more I hated and feared, as the cruellest of enemies, the death that had taken him from me; and I imagined it would make a quick end of everybody, since it had had power over him. That is how it was with me, I remember. Look at my heart, my God, look inside me; I remember it well, my hope, who cleanse me of the impurity of such loves, turning my eyes towards You and pulling my feet out of the trap. For I was amazed that other people, who are also going to die, were alive, when the man I had loved as if he would never die was dead; and I was more amazed still that I myself, who had been a second self to him, could be alive when he was dead. Somebody said of his friend, "You half of my soul" — and I felt that my soul and his soul had been one soul in two bodies; and so my life was a horror to me, because I did not want to live as a half. And that was perhaps why I was afraid to die: so that the man I had loved so much should not die completely.

What madness, not to know how to love people as people! What a fool I was then, unable to bear a human being's lot with any patience! I fretted and sighed and wept and was distracted, with no rest and no counsel. For I was carrying about a shattered and bleeding soul that could not bear to be carried by me — and where to set it down I could not find. Not in pleasant woods, not in games and music, not in scented gardens, not in elaborate dinners, not in the pleasures of the bed, and not, in the end, in books or poetry either. Everything looked ghastly, even the daylight. Whatever was not what he had been was disgusting and hateful — except groaning and tears, which were the only things I found any relief in. But when my soul was pulled away from those, an enormous weight of misery pressed me down. It should have been lifted up to You, Lord, for You to lighten it; I knew that, and I could not and would not — all the more since when I thought about You, You were nothing solid or substantial to me. For You were not Yourself but a phantom, and my error was my god. If I tried to unload my burden onto that, so that it might rest, it slid through the emptiness and came rushing back down on me; and I was left to myself as a miserable place where I could neither stay nor get away from. For where could my heart run to, away from my heart? Where could I run from myself? Where would I not follow myself? And yet I ran out of my own country, so that my eyes should look for him less, in places where they were not used to seeing him. And so I came from Thagaste to Carthage.

Time does not waste time; it does not roll idly by. Through our senses it works strange changes on the mind. Look: the days came and went, and by coming and going they brought other images and other memories into my mind, and patched me back together bit by bit with my old sort of pleasures, to which that grief of mine gave way. And what followed was not exactly other griefs, but the causes of other griefs. For how had that first grief got so easily into the deepest part of me, except that I had poured out my soul on dust, by loving a man who had to die as if he never would? What mainly restored and refreshed me was the comfort of other friends, with whom I loved what I was loving instead of You; and this was a huge fable and an extended lie, whose adulterous prodding defiled our souls as they lay itching in our ears. But that fable would not die for me, however many of my friends died. There were other things in them that took my mind more: talking and laughing together; doing each other kindnesses by turns; reading good books together; playing the fool together and being serious together; disagreeing occasionally without any ill feeling, as a man does with himself, and using the very rarity of those disagreements to season our far more frequent agreements; sometimes teaching and sometimes learning; longing impatiently for the ones who were away, and welcoming them with delight when they came. These signs and others like them, coming out of the hearts of people who loved and were loved in return, showing in the face and the tongue and the eyes and a thousand pleasing gestures, were fuel to melt our souls together and make one out of many.

This is what is loved in friends, and so loved that a man's conscience condemns him if he does not love someone who loves him, or does not love back the man who loves him, looking for nothing from him but the signs of his love. Hence that mourning when one of them dies, and the darkenings of sorrow, and the steeping of the heart in tears, and all sweetness turned bitter; and when the dying lose their life, the living die. Blessed is the man who loves You, and loves his friend in You, and his enemy for Your sake. He alone loses nobody dear to him, since everybody is dear to him in the one who cannot be lost. And who is that but our God, the God who made heaven and earth and fills them, because he created them by filling them? Nobody loses You except the man who leaves You; and the man who leaves You, where does he go or run to, except from You pleased to You displeased? For where does he not find Your law in his own punishment? And Your law is truth, and truth is You.

Turn us again, God of hosts, show us Your face, and we shall be whole. For wherever the human soul turns, unless it turns towards You, it is nailed to sorrows — even when it is nailed to beautiful things. And yet those things, outside You and outside the soul, would not exist unless they were from You. They rise and set; and by rising they begin, so to speak, to be; they grow, so as to become complete; and once complete they age and wither. Not all of them age, but all of them wither. So when they rise and press towards being, the faster they grow towards being, the faster they hurry towards not being. That is the law of them. That is the share You have allotted them, because they are parts of a thing that does not exist all at once, but by passing away and being succeeded makes up together the whole universe they are parts of. And in the same way our speech is completed by signs that make a sound — but this too is not finished unless one word passes away when it has sounded its part, so that another can follow. Out of all these things let my soul praise You, God, creator of everything; but let my soul not be nailed to these things with the glue of love, through the senses of the body. For they go where they were always going, into not being, and they tear her with sickly longings, because she longs to be, and loves to rest in what she loves. But in these things there is no resting place; they do not stay, they run. And who can follow them with the senses of the flesh? Who can even grasp them when they are close at hand? For the sense of the flesh is slow, because it is the sense of the flesh, and it is limited by that. It is enough for what it was made for, but it is not enough to hold things that are running their course from their appointed start to their appointed end. For in Your Word, by which they are created, they hear their sentence: this far and no further.

Do not be foolish, my soul, and do not let the ear of your heart go deaf with the din of your own folly. Listen, you too. The Word himself is calling you to come back; and there is the place of rest that nothing can disturb, where love is not abandoned if it does not do the abandoning itself. Look: these things pass away so that others can take their place, and so this lower universe is completed by all its parts. But do I go anywhere? says the Word of God. Fix your home there. Trust to that place whatever you have from it, my soul — now at least, when you are worn out with empty things. Entrust to Truth whatever you have from the Truth, and you will lose nothing; and what has rotted in you will bloom again, and all your diseases will be healed, and your mortal parts will be reshaped and made new and fastened round you. They will not lay you down where they themselves go down; they will stand firm with you and remain for ever before God, who remains and stands firm for ever.

Why then be twisted about and follow your flesh? Let it be turned round and follow you. Whatever you get from it a sense of is only a part; the whole, of which these are parts, you do not know — and yet the parts delight you. But if the sense of your flesh had the capacity to take in the whole, and had not itself been justly restricted, for your punishment, to a part of the whole, you would want everything that exists at this moment to pass away, so that the whole might please you better. For what we are saying now, you hear by that same sense of the flesh; and you would not want the syllables to stand still but to fly away, so that others might come and you might hear the whole. And so it always is: when one thing is made up of many that do not all exist together, the many would please more taken all together than they do separately, if they could all be taken in at once. But far better than any of this is the one who made it all; and he is our God, and he does not pass away, since nothing follows after him.

If bodies please you, praise God for them, and turn your love back on their maker, so that in the things that please you, you do not displease. If souls please you, let them be loved in God; for they too can change, but in him they are firmly settled — otherwise they would pass, and pass away. So let them be loved in him, and take along with you to him whatever souls you can, and say to them: "Let us love him, let us love him. He made these things and he is not far off. He did not make them and then leave; they are from him and in him. Look, there he is, wherever truth is loved. He is inside the very heart — and the heart has wandered away from him. Go back into your heart, you rebels, and hold fast to the one who made you. Stand with him and you will stand firm. Rest in him and you will be at rest. Where are you going, over these rough roads? Where are you going? The good thing you love is from him — but it is good and pleasant only in relation to him, and it will justly turn bitter, because anything that comes from him is unjustly loved if he is abandoned for it. Why then do you go on and on walking these hard and exhausting roads? There is no rest where you are looking for it. Look for what you are looking for, but it is not where you are looking. You are looking for a blessed life in the land of death; it is not there. How could there be a blessed life where there is no life at all?

"But our true life came down here and bore our death, and killed it out of the abundance of his own life; and he thundered, calling aloud to us to come back from here to him, into that hidden place he came out from to reach us — first into the Virgin's womb, where he was joined to the human creation, our mortal flesh, so that it should not be mortal for ever; and then coming out like a bridegroom from his chamber, rejoicing like a strong man to run his race. For he did not linger but ran, calling aloud by words, deeds, death, life, descent, ascent — crying aloud to us to come back to him. And he went out of our sight, so that we might go back into our heart and find him there. He went away, and look, he is here. He would not stay with us long, and he did not leave us; for he went back to the place he had never left, since the world was made by him. And he was in this world, and came into this world to save sinners, to whom my soul confesses, and he heals it, since it has sinned against him. Sons of men, how long will you be so slow of heart? Even now, after life has come down to you, will you not rise and live? But where are you rising to, when you are already on high and have set your mouth against the heavens? Come down, so that you may rise, and rise to God. For you fell by rising up against him." Tell them this, so that they may weep in the valley of tears, and so carry them up with you to God; because it is out of his spirit that you say this to them, if you say it burning with the fire of love.

I did not know these things then. I loved these lower beauties and I was sinking to the very bottom, and I said to my friends, "Do we love anything but the beautiful? What then is the beautiful? What is beauty? What is it that attracts us and wins us over to the things we love? If there were no grace and beauty in them, they could not possibly draw us to them." And I noticed and worked out that in bodies themselves there is one beauty that comes from their forming a kind of whole, and another that comes from a fitting correspondence with something else, as a part of the body corresponds to the whole body, or a shoe to a foot, and so on. This line of thought came up in my mind out of my inmost heart, and I wrote two or three books, I think, called On the Beautiful and the Fitting. You know, Lord; it is gone from me. I do not have them; they have strayed away from me somehow.

But what moved me, Lord my God, to dedicate those books to Hierius, an orator in Rome whom I did not know by sight, but loved for the fame of the learning he was distinguished for, and for a few words of his I had heard and liked? He pleased me more because he pleased others, who praised him highly, amazed that a Syrian, trained first in Greek eloquence, should afterwards turn into a remarkable Latin orator and a man deeply learned in philosophy. A man is praised and, unseen, is loved. Does this love pass into the hearer's heart out of the mouth of the man praising him? Not at all. But one who loves kindles another. That is why a man who is praised is loved: when the praiser is believed to be praising him with an honest heart, that is, when the man praising him loves him.

For that is how I loved men then — on other men's judgement, not on Yours, my God, in whom nobody is deceived. But then why not for qualities like those of a famous charioteer, or a beast-fighter in the arena, known far and wide by a popular fame? Because I loved this differently, and seriously, and in the way I would want to be praised myself. I would not want to be praised or loved as actors are, though I praised and loved them myself; I would rather be unknown than known that way, and even hated than loved that way. Where are all the weights of these various and different kinds of love stored up in one soul? Why, since we are equally men, do I love in another man what, if I did not hate it, I would not push away from myself? It will not do to say that a good horse is loved by a man who would not be that horse even if he could, and that the same holds of an actor, who shares our nature. So do I love in a man what I hate to be, being a man myself? Man himself is a great deep, whose very hairs You have numbered, Lord, and they do not fall to the ground without You. And yet the hairs of his head are easier to number than his feelings and the beatings of his heart.

But that orator was the kind of man I loved because I wanted to be like him. I went wrong through swollen pride and was tossed about by every wind — and still I was being steered by You, very secretly. And how do I know, and how can I confess it to You with confidence, that I loved him more for the love his admirers had for him than for the very things he was admired for? Because if he had gone unpraised, and these same men had run him down and told me exactly the same things about him with contempt, I would never have been fired up to love him. And the facts would have been no different, and he would have been no different; only the feelings of the people reporting. Look where the helpless soul lies stretched out, when it is not yet held up by the solidity of truth! Just as the gales of talk blow from the chests of people with opinions, so the soul is carried this way and that, driven forward and back, and the light is clouded over for it, and the truth goes unseen. And there it is, right in front of us. It was a great matter to me that my writing and my work should be known to that man. If he approved, I would burn all the brighter; if he disapproved, my empty heart, with none of Your solidity in it, would have been wounded. And yet the beautiful and the fitting, which I wrote to him about, I dwelt on with pleasure and looked over and admired, whether or not anybody joined me in it.

But I could not yet see where the whole weight of this matter turned in Your wisdom, all-powerful one, who alone do wonders. My mind ranged over bodily shapes; I defined and distinguished "beautiful", which is so in itself, from "fitting", whose beauty lies in corresponding to something else — and I backed this up with bodily examples. Then I turned to the nature of the mind, and the false notion I had about spiritual things would not let me see the truth. And yet the force of truth flashed into my eyes by itself, and I turned my panting soul away from incorporeal substance to outlines and colours and physical magnitudes; and being unable to see these in the mind, I concluded I could not see my mind. And whereas in virtue I loved peace, and in vice I hated discord, in the first I noticed a unity and in the second a kind of division. And I took that unity to be where the rational soul lay, and the nature of truth and of the highest good; while in the division I miserably imagined some unknown substance of irrational life, the nature of the highest evil, which should be not only a substance but real life too, and yet not derived from You, my God, from whom all things come. The first of these I called a Monad, as though it were a soul without sex, and the second a Dyad — anger, in acts of violence, and lust, in acts of disgrace — not knowing what I was talking about. For I had not learned that evil is not a substance, and that our soul is not that supreme and unchangeable good.

For just as acts of violence arise when that emotion of the soul from which forceful action springs becomes corrupted and stirs itself up insolently and out of control; and just as lusts arise when the affection by which bodily pleasures are drunk in goes ungoverned — so errors and false opinions spoil the conduct of life, if the reasoning soul itself is corrupted. And it was corrupted in me then, since I did not know that it must be lit by another light in order to share in truth, seeing it is not itself the nature of truth. For You will light my candle, Lord my God; You will lighten my darkness; and of Your fullness we have all received, for You are the true light that lights every man who comes into the world; for in You there is no variableness, neither shadow of change.

But I pressed towards You and was pushed away, so that I might taste death; for You resist the proud. And what could be prouder than for me, in a strange madness, to maintain that I was by nature what You are? For although I was subject to change — which was obvious enough to me, since my very desire to become wise was a wish to go from worse to better — I still chose rather to imagine You subject to change, and myself not to be what You are. So I was pushed back by You, and You resisted my empty stiff-neckedness; and I imagined bodily shapes, and, being flesh myself, accused the flesh; and being a wind that passes away, I did not turn back to You, but went further and further on into things that have no being at all, neither in You nor in me nor in bodies. They were not created for me by Your truth but invented by my own vanity out of bodily things. And I used to ask Your faithful little ones, my fellow citizens — from whom I stood exiled without knowing it — I used to ask them, chattering and stupid, "Why then does the soul go wrong, when God created it?" But I would not be asked, "Why then does God go wrong?" And I maintained that Your unchangeable substance went wrong under compulsion, rather than confess that my changeable substance had gone astray of its own accord, and now lay in error as its punishment.

I was some twenty-six or twenty-seven years old when I wrote those volumes, turning over inside me bodily fictions that buzzed in the ears of my heart — ears I was trying to turn, sweet truth, towards Your inward music, while I thought about the beautiful and the fitting, longing to stand still and listen to You and to rejoice greatly at the bridegroom's voice. And I could not; for I was hurried outside by the voices of my own errors, and by the weight of my own pride I was sinking into the lowest pit. For You had not made me hear joy and gladness, and the bones did not rejoice which had not yet been humbled.

And what good did it do me that when I was barely twenty a book of Aristotle's called the Ten Categories came into my hands — on whose very name I used to hang as on something great and divine, whenever my rhetoric master at Carthage and others reckoned learned mouthed it with their cheeks bursting with pride — and I read it and understood it with no help at all? When I compared notes with others, who said they had only just understood it with very able tutors, who not only explained it aloud but drew a great many things in the sand, they could tell me no more about it than I had learned reading it by myself. The book seemed to me to speak very clearly about substances, such as "man", and about their qualities: the shape of a man, what sort it is; his height, how many feet; his relations, whose brother he is; where he is placed; when he was born; whether he is standing or sitting; whether he is shod or armed; whether he is doing something or having something done to him — and all the countless things that can be arranged under these nine categories, of which I have given a few samples, or under the chief category of Substance.

What did all this do for me, when in fact it got in my way? Imagining that whatever existed was covered by those ten categories, I tried to understand Your wonderful and unchangeable oneness in the same terms, my God, as though You too had been made a subject of Your own greatness and beauty, so that they existed in You as their subject, the way they do in bodies — whereas You Yourself are Your greatness and Your beauty, while a body is not great or beautiful by being a body, since it would still be a body if it were less great or less beautiful. But what I conceived about You was falsehood, not truth: fictions of my own misery, not the realities of Your blessedness. For You had commanded, and it was carried out in me, that the earth should bring forth briars and thorns for me, and that I should eat my bread in the sweat of my brow.

And what good did it do me that I read and understood by myself, vile slave of vile appetites that I was, every book I could get hold of on the so-called liberal arts? I took delight in them and did not know where whatever was true or certain in them came from. For I had my back to the light and my face to the things it lit, so that my face, with which I made out the things that were lit, was not itself lit. Whatever was written on rhetoric or logic or geometry or music or arithmetic I understood by myself, without much difficulty and with no teacher; You know it, Lord my God, because both quickness of understanding and sharpness in distinguishing are Your gift. But I did not sacrifice any of it to You. So it did not serve my good but my ruin, since I was busy getting so good a portion of my inheritance into my own keeping, and did not keep my strength for You, but wandered away from You into a far country to spend it on prostitutes. For what use were good abilities not put to good use? I did not realise how hard those arts were to master, even for the studious and the talented, until I tried explaining them to such people — and the best of them was the one who was not too far behind me.

But what good did any of it do me, when I imagined that You, Lord God, the truth, were an enormous shining body and I was a fragment of that body? What monstrous perversity! But that is what I was. And I do not blush, my God, to confess Your mercies to me and to call on You, when I did not blush then to declare my blasphemies to men and to bark at You. What good was my nimble wit in those sciences then, and all those knotty volumes untangled by me with no help from any human teacher, when I was going so foully and so sacrilegiously wrong about the doctrine of true religion? And what harm did a far slower wit do to Your little ones, since they did not go far away from You, and could grow their feathers safely in the nest of Your church, and feed the wings of love on the food of a sound faith? Lord our God, let us hope under the shadow of Your wings; protect us and carry us. You will carry us when we are small and carry us to our grey hairs; for our firmness, when it is You, is firmness indeed, but when it is our own it is weakness. Our good always lives with You, and when we turn away from it we are turned aside. Let us come back now, Lord, so that we are not overturned; because with You our good lives without any decay, and that good is You. And we need not be afraid that there will be nowhere to come back to because we fell from it — for our being away did not bring our home down. Our home is Your eternity.

Book Five: Faustus, Rome, and the Road to Milan

Accept the sacrifice of my confessions from the ministry of my tongue, which You shaped and stirred up to confess to Your name. Heal all my bones, and let them say, Lord, who is like You? A man who confesses to You is not informing You what goes on inside him, since a closed heart does not shut out Your eye, and human hard-heartedness cannot push back Your hand: You dissolve it whenever You like, in pity or in vengeance, and nothing can hide from Your heat. But let my soul praise You, so that it may love You; and let it confess Your own mercies to You, so that it may praise You. The whole of Your creation never stops praising You and never falls silent: not the human spirit with its voice turned towards You, and not creation, living or lifeless, speaking through the voices of those who consider it. So our souls may rise up out of their weariness towards You, leaning on the things You have made and passing on to You, who made them wonderfully. There is refreshment, and real strength.

Let the restless and the godless leave You and run away — and still You see them, and divide the darkness. And look: the universe with them in it is beautiful, though they are foul. What harm have they done You? How have they disgraced Your rule, which is just and complete from heaven down to this lowest earth? Where did they run to, when they ran from Your presence? Where do You not find them? They ran so as not to see You seeing them, and so as to blunder blindly into You — because You abandon nothing You have made — so that the unjust should stumble against You and be justly hurt, pulling away from Your gentleness, tripping on Your uprightness, and falling on their own roughness. They do not know that You are everywhere, and that no place contains You, and that You alone are near even to those who take themselves far away from You. Let them turn round, then, and look for You; for You have not abandoned Your creation the way they have abandoned their creator. Let them turn round and look for You, and there You are, in their heart — in the heart of those who confess to You and throw themselves on You and weep on Your chest after all their rough roads. Then You gently wipe away their tears, and they weep the more, and rejoice in weeping; because it is You, Lord, not some man of flesh and blood but You, Lord, who made them, who remake them and comfort them. But where was I when I was looking for You? You were in front of me, and I had gone away from You; and I could not even find myself, let alone You.

I want to lay open before my God that twenty-ninth year of my life. There had come to Carthage a certain bishop of the Manichees called Faustus, a great trap of the devil, and many people were caught in it by the bait of his smooth talk. I admired that talk myself, but I could tell it apart from the truth of the things I was so anxious to learn; I was not so much interested in the serving-dish of his oratory as in the knowledge that this Faustus, so praised among them, was setting in front of me to feed on. Rumour had already announced him as an expert in every valuable branch of learning and finely skilled in the liberal arts. And since I had read and remembered a good deal of the philosophers, I compared some of their views with the long fables of the Manichees and found the philosophers more probable — even though they could get no further than judging this lower world, and could not by any means find out its Lord. For You are great, Lord, and You have regard for the humble, while the proud You watch from far off. You come near only to the contrite in heart, and are not found by the proud, not even if by ingenious skill they could number the stars and the sand, and measure the starry heavens, and track the courses of the planets.

For with the understanding and wit You gave them they investigate these things, and they have found out a great deal. They have predicted eclipses of those two great lights, the sun and the moon, many years in advance — the day, the hour, and how much would be covered — and their calculations did not fail, and it happened as they said. They wrote down the rules they had discovered, and those rules are read today; and from them others predict in what year and month and on what day of the month and at what hour of the day, and what fraction of its light, the moon or sun will be eclipsed. And so it happens, exactly as foretold. People who do not know the science marvel at this and are astonished; and those who do know it exult and are puffed up, and by an irreligious pride they go away from You and lose Your light. They foresee a failure of the sun's light so long beforehand, and do not see the failure of their own, which is present now. For they do not enquire religiously where the wit comes from with which they investigate all this. And when they find that You made them, they do not give themselves up to You to be kept safe, or sacrifice to You what they have made of themselves. They do not kill off their own soaring fancies, as birds of the air; nor their own diving curiosities, with which like the fishes of the sea they wander the unknown paths of the deep; nor their own indulgences, as beasts of the field — so that You, Lord, a consuming fire, might burn up those dead concerns of theirs and remake the men themselves immortally.

But they did not know the way, Your Word, by whom You made the things they count, and the men who do the counting, and the sense by which they perceive what they count, and the understanding out of which they count — and that of Your wisdom there is no counting. But the Only Begotten was himself made wisdom and righteousness and sanctification for us, and was counted among us, and paid tax to Caesar. They did not know this way, by which to come down to him from themselves and by him climb up to him. They did not know this way, and thought themselves high up among the stars and shining; and look, they fell to the earth, and their foolish heart was darkened. They say a great many true things about the created world; but Truth, the maker of that world, they do not seek reverently, and so they do not find him — or if they do find him, and know him to be God, they do not glorify him as God, nor are they thankful, but become empty in their imaginings and declare themselves wise, taking to themselves what is Yours. And thereby, with the most perverse blindness, they take pains to attribute to You what is their own, inventing lies about You, who are the truth, and exchanging the glory of the incorruptible God for an image made like corruptible man, and like birds and four-footed animals and creeping things — changing Your truth into a lie, and worshipping and serving the creature more than the creator.

Yet I did retain from these men many true things about the created world, and saw the reasons for them from calculations, from the sequence of the seasons, and from the visible evidence of the stars. And I compared these with the sayings of Manichaeus, who in his frenzy had written at enormous length on these subjects; and I could find no account of the solstices or the equinoxes or the eclipses of the greater lights, or anything else of the kind I had learned in the books of secular philosophy. And I was ordered to believe — and it did not match what had been established by calculation and by my own eyes, but was flatly contrary to it.

Does a man who knows these things please You just for that, Lord God of truth? Surely a man who knows all of them and does not know You is unhappy; and happy is the man who knows You, even if he knows none of them. And the man who knows both You and them is not the happier for them but only for You, if in knowing You he glorifies You as God and is thankful and does not become empty in his imaginings. For a man is better off who knows how to own a tree and give You thanks for the use of it, though he does not know how many feet high it is or how wide it spreads, than one who can measure it and count every branch and neither owns it nor knows or loves its creator. In the same way a believer, to whom all this world of wealth belongs, and who having nothing yet possesses everything by holding fast to You, whom all things serve — even if he does not know the circling of the Great Bear, it would be folly to doubt that he is in a better state than someone who can measure the sky and count the stars and weigh the elements, and neglects You, who have made all things in number, weight and measure.

But who told that Manichaeus to write about these subjects too, when skill in them is no part of true religion? For You have said to man: reverence — that is wisdom. Of these he might have been ignorant while knowing everything about the stars. But since he dared, most impudently, to teach these things while not knowing them, he plainly could have no knowledge of reverence. It is empty to make a profession of these worldly matters even when you do know them; but confession to You is reverence. So this wanderer talked at length about these things for this purpose: that when he had been shown up by people who had really learned them, it would become obvious what sort of understanding he had of the more obscure matters. For he did not want to be thought little of, but set out to persuade people that the Holy Spirit, the comforter and enricher of Your faithful, was personally inside him with full authority. So when he was caught teaching falsehoods about the heavens and the stars and the motions of the sun and moon — although these things do not belong to religious doctrine — his sacrilegious presumption became obvious enough, since he delivered things he not only did not know but had got wrong, with such a mad vanity of pride that he wanted them credited to himself as to a divine person.

For when I hear some Christian brother who is ignorant of these matters and mistaken about them, I can watch such a man holding his opinion patiently. I cannot see that any ignorance about the position or character of the physical creation can hurt him, so long as he believes nothing unworthy of You, Lord, the creator of everything. It does hurt him if he imagines it belongs to the substance of religious doctrine, and insists stubbornly on what he does not know. And yet even that weakness, in the infancy of faith, is carried by our mother, Love, until the newborn grows into a full-grown man and can stop being blown about by every wind of doctrine. But in a man who presumed to be the teacher, the source, the guide, the head of everyone he could so persuade — so that whoever followed him believed he was following not a mere man but Your Holy Spirit — who would not judge that such enormous madness, once it was convicted of teaching anything false, should be detested and utterly rejected? But at that stage I had not yet established for certain whether the changes between longer and shorter days and nights, and between day and night itself, and the eclipses of the greater lights, and everything else of the kind I had read about elsewhere, could possibly be explained consistently with what he said. If they somehow could be, it would still remain an open question for me whether they were so or not; but I might rest my belief on his authority, on account of his reputed holiness.

And for almost all those nine years in which I had been their disciple with an unsettled mind, I had been longing far too intensely for this Faustus to come. Whenever the rest of the sect that I happened to run into could not solve my objections on these matters, they still held out the coming of this Faustus, in conversation with whom these and any greater difficulties I might have would be cleared up most readily and abundantly. When he did come, I found him a man of agreeable conversation who could talk fluently and in better words — but still the very same things they were all in the habit of saying. What good was the neatest of wine-waiters to my thirst for a more precious drink? My ears were already sick of this sort of thing, and it did not seem better to me for being better said, or truer for being eloquent, or the soul wiser for the face being handsome and the language graceful. But the people who had held him out to me were poor judges of things, and so to them he seemed intelligent and wise because his words were pleasing. I noticed that another sort of people are suspicious even of the truth and refuse to accept it if it is delivered in a smooth and copious style. But You, my God, had already taught me by wonderful and secret ways, and I believe You taught me because it is the truth, and there is no teacher of truth besides You, wherever it may shine on us from. From You, then, I had learned by now that nothing ought to seem true because it is eloquently said, or false because the words come out awkwardly; nor again true because it is roughly delivered, or false because the language is rich; but that wisdom and folly are like wholesome and unwholesome food, and ornate or plain phrases are like court dishes and country dishes: either kind of food can be served up in either kind of dish.

So the greediness with which I had waited so long for this man was certainly delighted with his manner and his feeling in argument, and with the choice and readiness of the words he dressed his ideas in. I was delighted then, and along with many others, and more than they, I praised and extolled him. What troubled me, though, was that in the crowd of his listeners I was not allowed to put in the questions that were bothering me and talk them over with him privately. When I could, and began with my friends to get his ear at times when it was not unsuitable for him to discuss things with me, and brought forward the questions that were troubling me — I found him first of all entirely ignorant of the liberal arts, apart from grammar, and that only in an ordinary way. But because he had read some of Cicero's speeches, a very few books of Seneca, some poetry, and the few volumes of his own sect that were written in neat Latin, and because he practised speaking daily, he had picked up a certain eloquence, which was all the more pleasing and seductive for being guided by a good wit and a kind of natural grace. Is that not how it was, as I recall it, Lord my God, judge of my conscience? My heart is in front of You, and my memory, You who at that time were directing me by the hidden mystery of Your providence, and setting those shameful errors of mine in front of my face, so that I might see them and hate them.

For once it was clear that he was ignorant of the arts I had thought he excelled in, I began to despair of his opening up and solving the difficulties that puzzled me — though ignorant of all that, he might still have held the truths of religion, had he not been a Manichee. For their books are stuffed with long-winded fables about the heaven and the stars and the sun and moon; and I no longer thought him able to settle satisfactorily what I very much wanted settled: whether, comparing these things with the calculations I had read elsewhere, the account in the books of Manichaeus was preferable, or at least as good. When I proposed that this be considered and discussed, he modestly shrank from the burden. He knew that he did not know these things, and was not ashamed to admit it. He was not one of those talkers, many of whom I had put up with, who undertook to teach me these things and said nothing. This man had a heart that was not right towards You, and yet was not altogether treacherous to himself either. He was not entirely ignorant of his own ignorance, and he would not be rashly tangled up in an argument he could neither back out of nor get out of honourably. I liked him the better even for that. For the modesty of an honest mind is finer than the knowledge of the things I wanted; and that is how I found him in all the harder and subtler questions.

With my enthusiasm for the writings of Manichaeus blunted like this, and despairing all the more of their other teachers, seeing how the most famous of them had turned out on various points that puzzled me, I began working with him on the literature he was keen on too — and which as a lecturer in rhetoric I was at that time teaching to young men at Carthage. We read together either what he wanted to hear or what I judged suited his abilities. But all the efforts I had planned to make to rise in that sect came completely to an end once I knew the man; not that I detached myself from them altogether, but as one who could find nothing better, I settled for being content in the meantime with whatever I had happened to land in, unless something more eligible should happen to dawn on me. So this Faustus, who to so many had been a trap of death, had now, without wanting to or knowing it, begun to loosen the trap I was caught in. For Your hands, my God, in the secret purpose of Your providence, did not abandon my soul; and out of my mother's heart's blood, through the tears she poured out day and night, a sacrifice was being offered for me to You, and You dealt with me in wonderful ways. You did it, my God: for the steps of a man are ordered by the Lord, and he shall direct his way. How else shall we obtain salvation, but from Your hand, remaking what it made?

So You arranged that I should be persuaded to go to Rome and teach there what I had been teaching at Carthage. And how I was persuaded to this I shall not fail to confess to You, because here too the deepest recesses of Your wisdom and Your most present mercy to us must be considered and confessed. I did not want to go to Rome because higher pay and higher standing were promised me by the friends who urged it, though at the time those things did have an influence on me. My chief and almost only reason was that I heard the young men there studied more peaceably and were kept quiet under a more regular discipline, so that they did not go bursting into the classroom of a teacher whose pupils they were not, whenever they felt like it, and were not even admitted without his permission. Whereas at Carthage there is a thoroughly disgraceful and unruly licence among the students. They burst in insolently, and with almost frantic behaviour they wreck whatever order a man has established for the good of his pupils. They commit various outrages with a remarkable stupidity, punishable by law were custom not on their side — that same custom showing them to be the more wretched, since they now do as lawful what by Your eternal law will never be lawful, and they think they get away with it unpunished, when they are punished by the very blindness that makes them do it, and suffer incomparably worse than what they inflict. So the behaviour I would not adopt as a student, I was obliged to put up with in others as a teacher; and so I was glad to go where everyone who knew assured me that nothing of the kind happened. But You, my refuge and my portion in the land of the living: so that I should change my earthly dwelling for the salvation of my soul, You goaded me at Carthage to tear me away from it, and dangled attractions at Rome to draw me there — through men in love with a dying life, the one lot doing frantic things and the other lot promising empty ones. And to correct my steps You secretly used both their perversity and my own. For those who were disturbing my peace were blinded by a disgraceful frenzy, and those who were inviting me elsewhere had the taste of earth about them. And I, who hated real misery here, was over there looking for an unreal happiness.

But why I left there and went here, You knew, God, and You did not show it either to me or to my mother, who grieved bitterly over my journey and followed me as far as the sea. She held on to me by force, so as either to keep me back or come with me — and I deceived her, pretending I had a friend I could not leave until he had a fair wind to sail. I lied to my mother, and to such a mother, and got away. And You have mercifully forgiven me even for that, preserving me from the waters of the sea, full of loathsome filth as I was, and keeping me for the water of Your grace, by which, when I was washed clean, the streams from my mother's eyes would be dried up — the streams with which she daily watered the ground under her face for me. And since she refused to go home without me, I only just persuaded her to spend that night in a place near our ship, where there was a chapel in memory of the blessed Cyprian. That night I slipped away, and she stayed behind weeping and praying. And what, Lord, was she asking of You with all those tears, except that You would not let me sail? But You, in the depth of Your counsels, hearing the main point of her desire, took no notice of what she was asking at that moment, so as to make me what she was always asking for. The wind blew and filled our sails and drew the shore out of our sight; and in the morning she was there, frantic with grief, filling Your ears with complaints and groans, which You then disregarded — while through my own desires You were hurrying me on to the end of all desire, and the earthly part of her love for me was being chastened by the appointed whip of sorrow. For she loved having me with her, as mothers do, but far more than most; and she did not know what great joy You were about to make for her out of my absence. She did not know, and so she wept and wailed; and in that agony there appeared in her the inheritance of Eve, looking in sorrow for what she had brought forth in sorrow. And yet, after accusing me of treachery and hard-heartedness, she turned again to pleading with You for me, went back to her usual place, and I went to Rome.

And there I was met by the whip of bodily illness, and I was going down to hell carrying all the sins I had committed against You and against myself and against others — many and serious, over and above the bond of original sin by which we all die in Adam. For You had forgiven me none of these things in Christ, and he had not abolished by his cross the enmity I had incurred with You by my sins. How could he, by the crucifixion of a phantom, which is what I believed him to be? The death of my soul was as real as the death of his flesh seemed to me false; and as real as the death of his body was, so unreal was the life of my soul, which did not believe it. And now, as the fever rose, I was going, and going for ever. If I had left this world then, where would I have gone but into the fire and torments my deeds deserved in the truth of Your ordering? And she did not know this, and prayed for me while she was away. But You, present everywhere, heard her where she was, and where I was You had compassion on me: so that I recovered my bodily health, still deranged as I was in my sacrilegious heart. For in all that danger I did not want Your baptism. I had been better as a boy, when I begged for it from my mother's devotion, as I have already recounted and confessed. But I had grown up to my own shame, and I madly laughed at the prescriptions of Your medicine, when You would not let me, such as I was, die a double death. If my mother's heart had been pierced by that wound, it could never have healed. For I cannot describe the love she had for me, and with how much more violent anguish she was now in labour with me in the spirit than she had been in childbirth in the flesh.

I do not see how she could have been healed, if such a death of mine had struck through the depths of her love. And where would those strong, unceasing prayers of hers have been, addressed to You alone without a break? But would You, God of mercies, despise the contrite and humbled heart of that chaste and sober widow — so frequent in almsgiving, so full of duty and service to Your saints, letting no day pass without an offering at Your altar, coming to Your church twice a day, morning and evening, without any interruption, and not for idle gossip and old wives' tales, but so that she might hear You in Your discourses and You might hear her in her prayers? Could You despise the tears of such a woman, and refuse Your help — tears with which she begged You not for gold or silver or any changeable and passing good, but for the salvation of her son's soul, You by whose gift she was what she was? Never, Lord. You were at hand, hearing and acting, in the order You had already determined it should be done. Far be it from You to have deceived her in the visions and answers You gave her, some of which I have mentioned and some of which I have not, which she stored up in her faithful heart and, always praying, pressed on You as Your own handwriting. For since Your mercy endures for ever, You are willing to become a debtor by Your promises to those whose debts You forgive entirely.

So You brought me through that illness and healed the son of Your handmaid, in body for the time being, so that he might live for You to give him a better and more lasting health. And even then, in Rome, I attached myself to those deceiving and deceived "holy ones" — not only to their hearers, of whom the man in whose house I fell ill and recovered was one, but also to those they call "the Elect". For I still thought that it is not we who sin, but some other nature, I do not know what, that sins in us. It pleased my pride to be free of blame; and when I had done something wrong, not to confess I had done anything, so that You might heal my soul because it had sinned against You. I preferred to excuse it and accuse something else, I did not know what, which was with me but was not me. But in truth it was entirely me, and my irreligion had divided me against myself; and that sin was the more incurable in that I did not judge myself a sinner. It was a loathsome wickedness that I would rather have You, almighty God, defeated in me to my destruction, than myself defeated by You to my salvation. You had not yet set a watch before my mouth and a door of safekeeping round my lips, so that my heart should not turn aside to wicked talk, making excuses for sins along with men who do evil; and so I was still attached to their Elect.

But now, despairing of getting anywhere in that false doctrine, I held even the things I had decided to be content with, if I could find nothing better, more loosely and carelessly. For a thought half rose in me that those philosophers they call the Academics were wiser than the rest, because they held that men ought to doubt everything and laid it down that no truth can be grasped by a human being — for at that stage I did not even understand what they meant, and I too was firmly convinced that they thought what they are commonly reported to have thought. And I quite freely and openly discouraged my host from the over-confidence I could see he had in the fables the books of Manichaeus are full of. And yet I lived on more intimate terms with them than with people outside the heresy. I no longer maintained it with my old eagerness; but my closeness to that sect — Rome secretly harbouring a great many of them — made me slower to look for another way, especially since I despaired of finding, in Your church, Lord of heaven and earth, creator of everything visible and invisible, the truth they had turned me away from. It seemed to me utterly unfitting to believe that You had the shape of human flesh and were bounded by the bodily outlines of our limbs. And because, when I tried to think about my God, I could think of nothing but a mass of bodies — since whatever was not that did not seem to me to be anything at all — this was the greatest and almost the only cause of my unavoidable error.

For it made me believe that evil too was some substance of the same kind, with its own foul and hideous bulk: either thick, which they called earth, or thin and subtle like the body of the air, which they imagine to be some malignant mind creeping through that earth. And because a sort of reverence obliged me to believe that the good God never created any evil nature, I imagined two masses, opposed to each other, both without limit, but the evil one narrower and the good one broader. And from this poisonous beginning the rest of the sacrilegious notions followed. For when my mind tried to get back to the Catholic faith, I was driven off, because what I thought was the Catholic faith was not. And it seemed to me more reverent to believe You, my God, to whom Your mercies confess out of my mouth, to be unbounded at least on the other sides, though on the one side where the mass of evil was set against You I was forced to admit You bounded — rather than to imagine You bounded on all sides by the shape of a human body. And it seemed better to believe that You had created no evil, which in my ignorance seemed to me not merely something but a bodily substance, since I could not conceive of mind except as a subtle body spread out in definite spaces, than to believe that the nature of evil, as I conceived it, could come from You. And I believed even our saviour himself, Your Only Begotten, had been stretched out, so to speak, for our salvation from the mass of Your most shining substance — so as to believe nothing about him except what I could imagine in my emptiness. His nature being like that, I thought it could not be born of the Virgin Mary without being mixed with the flesh; and how what I had pictured to myself could be mixed with flesh and not defiled, I could not see. So I was afraid to believe him born in the flesh, in case I should be forced to believe him defiled by the flesh. Your spiritual ones will now smile at me mildly and lovingly, if they read these confessions of mine. But that is what I was.

Furthermore, the criticisms the Manichees made of Your Scriptures I thought could not be answered. And yet at times I really did want to talk these various points over with somebody very well versed in those books, and test what he thought about them. For the words of a man called Helpidius, speaking and arguing face to face against the Manichees, had begun to stir me even at Carthage; because he had produced things out of the Scriptures that were not easily resisted, and the Manichees' answer struck me as weak. That answer they did not like to give in public, but only to us in private: that the Scriptures of the New Testament had been corrupted by somebody or other, who wanted to graft the law of the Jews onto the Christian faith — and yet they never produced any uncorrupted copies themselves. But I, who could conceive only of bodily things, was held down mainly by those "masses", violently oppressed and practically suffocated by them; and panting under them for the breath of Your truth, I could not breathe it pure and untainted.

So I began working hard at the thing I had come to Rome for, teaching rhetoric; and first at gathering a few pupils at my house, through whom and to whom I was beginning to be known. And then I found other offences going on in Rome that I had not been exposed to in Africa. It was true that the "wreckings" by dissolute young men were not practised here, as I had been told. But all of a sudden, people said, a group of students will conspire together to avoid paying their teacher's fee and move to another one — breakers of faith, who hold justice cheap for love of money. My heart hated these people too, though not with a perfect hatred: for I probably hated them more because of what I stood to lose by them than because of the sheer illegality of what they did. Certainly such people are base, and they whore away from You, loving these fleeting mockeries of temporary things, and filthy money, which dirties the hand that grabs it; hugging the fleeting world and despising You, who stay, and call back, and forgive the adulterous human soul when she comes back to You. And now I hate such crooked people, though I love them if they can be put right, so that they might prefer the learning they are acquiring to money, and prefer You to the learning, God, the truth and fullness of guaranteed good, and the purest peace. But in those days I disliked their being bad more for my own sake than I wanted them good for Yours.
