So when the people of Milan sent to the prefect of the city at Rome, asking him to supply them with a lecturer in rhetoric for their city and send him at public expense, I applied — through the very people drunk on Manichaean vanities whom I was to be freed from by going, though neither they nor I knew it — for Symmachus, then prefect of the city, to test me by setting me a subject and then send me. So I came to Milan, and to Ambrose the bishop, known throughout the world as one of the best of men, Your devoted servant, whose eloquent preaching then dispensed to Your people in abundance the flour of Your wheat, the gladness of Your oil, and the sober intoxication of Your wine. I was led to him by You without knowing it, so that through him I might knowingly be led to You. That man of God received me like a father and showed me an episcopal kindness at my arrival. From then on I began to love him — at first not as a teacher of truth, which I had utterly given up hope of finding in Your church, but as a man who was kind to me. And I listened carefully to him preaching to the people, not with the intention I should have had, but testing his eloquence, as it were, to see whether it lived up to its reputation or ran fuller or thinner than reported. I hung on his words attentively, and about the substance of them I was a careless and scornful bystander. I was delighted by the sweetness of his delivery, which was more learned than Faustus's but less winning and harmonious in manner. As to the substance there was no comparison, for the one was wandering about among Manichaean delusions and the other was teaching salvation soundly. But salvation is far from sinners, and that is what I was as I stood in front of him — and yet I was drawing nearer, little by little and without noticing.

For although I took no trouble to learn what he was saying but only to hear how he said it — that empty concern being all I had left, having despaired of any road to You open to a human being — nevertheless, along with the words I was choosing to attend to, the substance I meant to reject came into my mind as well, since I could not separate them. And while I opened my heart to take in how eloquently he spoke, there also came in how truly he spoke — but gradually. First these matters began to look defensible to me; and the Catholic faith, for which I had thought nothing could be said against the Manichees' objections, I now thought could be maintained without embarrassment, especially after I had heard one or two passages of the Old Testament explained, and often explained figuratively — passages which, when I took them literally, had killed me spiritually. So when a great many places in those books had been explained, I began to blame my own despair for believing that no answer at all could be given to those who hated and mocked the Law and the Prophets. And yet I did not therefore see that the Catholic way was the one to hold, just because it too could produce learned defenders who could answer objections at length and with some show of reason; nor that what I held was to be condemned just because both sides could be defended. For the Catholic case seemed to me not so much defeated as still not yet victorious.

At this point I bent my mind hard to seeing whether I could by any certain proof convict the Manichees of falsehood. If I could once have conceived of a spiritual substance, all their strongholds would have been beaten down and thrown out of my mind altogether; but I could not. Even so, on the structure of this world and the whole of nature that the senses of the flesh can reach, the more I considered and compared, the more I judged the views of most of the philosophers to be far more probable. So, in the manner of the Academics as they are supposed to be, doubting everything and wavering between everything, I settled at least this far: that the Manichees were to be abandoned. I judged that even while I was in doubt I could not stay in a sect I already ranked below some of the philosophers — to which philosophers, however, since they were without the saving name of Christ, I absolutely refused to trust the cure of my sick soul. So I decided to remain a catechumen in the Catholic Church, which my parents had commended me to, until something certain should dawn on me to steer my course by.

Book Six: Ambrose, Alypius, and the Struggle to Believe

My hope from my youth, where were You to me, and where had You gone? Had You not created me and marked me off from the beasts of the field and the birds of the air? You had made me wiser than they were, and I walked in darkness and in slippery places, and I looked for You outside myself and did not find the God of my heart. I had come into the depths of the sea and had lost all trust and all hope of ever finding the truth.

By now my mother had come out to me, made resolute by her faith, following me over sea and land and trusting in You through every danger. In the dangers of the sea she comforted the sailors themselves — though it is usually sailors who comfort passengers unfamiliar with deep water when they are frightened — assuring them of a safe arrival, because You had assured her of it in a vision. She found me in grave danger, having despaired of ever finding the truth. But when I let her know that I was no longer a Manichee, though not a Catholic Christian either, she was not overjoyed as though at something unexpected. She had already been reassured about that part of my misery which had made her mourn me as a dead man, though one You would wake again, carrying me out on the bier of her thoughts so that You might say to the widow's son, Young man, I say to you, get up — and he would revive and begin to speak, and You would give him back to his mother. So her heart was not shaken by any tumult of excitement when she heard that what she had asked of You daily in tears had already so largely come about: that although I had not yet reached the truth, I had been rescued from falsehood. Being certain that You, who had promised the whole thing, would one day give the rest, she answered me quite calmly, with a heart full of confidence, that she believed in Christ that before she left this life she would see me a Catholic believer. That was what she said to me. But to You, fountain of mercies, she poured out still more prayers and tears, that You would hurry Your help and light up my darkness. And she went the more eagerly to church and hung on the lips of Ambrose, praying for the fountain of that water which springs up to everlasting life. She loved that man like an angel of God, because she knew that it was through him that I had been brought, for the moment, to the doubtful state of faith I was now in — through which she confidently expected me to pass from sickness to health, after what would be, so to speak, a sharper attack of the fever, the kind doctors call the crisis.

Once, when my mother had brought to the churches built in memory of the saints some cakes and bread and wine, as her custom had been in Africa, she was stopped by the doorkeeper. And as soon as she learned that the bishop had forbidden it, she accepted his wishes so devoutly and obediently that I was astonished at how readily she found fault with her own practice rather than argue about his ban. Wine-drinking laid no siege to her spirit, and no love of wine drove her to hate the truth, as it does far too many people, men and women alike, who recoil from a lesson in sobriety like a thoroughly drunk man from a cup mixed with water. When she brought her basket of the customary festival food, to be tasted by herself and then given away, she never included more than one small cup of wine, watered down to suit her own abstemious habits, which she would sip out of courtesy. And if there were several churches of departed saints to be honoured in that way, she carried the same single cup round to be used at all of them; and although it grew not only very watery but unpleasantly warm from being carried about, she would share it out among the people there in small sips, since what she was after was devotion and not pleasure. So as soon as she found that this custom had been forbidden by that famous preacher and most devout bishop, even for people who would use it soberly — in case it gave the heavy drinkers an occasion for excess, and because these annual commemorations of the dead were very like the superstition of the pagans — she gave it up quite willingly. Instead of a basket filled with the fruits of the earth, she had learned to bring to the churches of the martyrs a breast filled with purer prayers, and to give what she could to the poor; so that the sharing of the Lord's body should be rightly celebrated in the place where, following the example of his passion, the martyrs had been sacrificed and crowned. And yet it seems to me, Lord my God, and this is what my heart thinks about it in Your sight, that she might not have given up that custom so readily if it had been forbidden by somebody other than Ambrose, whom she did not love as she loved him. She loved him utterly, for my salvation's sake, and he loved her in return for her deeply religious way of life, in which she was constant at church, so fervent in spirit in her good works; so that when he saw me he often broke into praise of her, congratulating me on having such a mother — not knowing what a son she had in me, who doubted every one of these things and imagined the way to life could not be found at all.

Nor did I yet groan in my prayers for You to help me. My mind was entirely set on learning and restless to argue. And Ambrose himself I considered a happy man as the world counts happiness, since such great people held him in such honour; only his celibacy struck me as a painful business. What hope he carried inside him, what struggles he had against the temptations that beset his very excellences, what comfort he had in trouble, and what sweet joys Your bread had for the hidden mouth of his spirit when he chewed it over — of all that I could form no guess and had no experience. Nor did he know the tides of my feelings or the depth of my danger. For I could not ask him what I wanted, in the way I wanted, being shut off from both his ear and his voice by crowds of busy people whose troubles he served. When he was not taken up with them, which was only for a short time, he was either refreshing his body with the absolutely necessary food or his mind with reading.

But when he read, his eye ran over the page and his heart searched out the sense, while his voice and tongue were still. Often, when we had come in — for nobody was forbidden to come in, and it was not his custom to have visitors announced — we saw him reading like this, silently, and never otherwise. And after sitting a long time in silence, since who would dare to interrupt a man so absorbed, we would leave again, guessing that in the small interval he got for refreshing his mind, free from the din of other people's business, he did not want to be taken off it. Perhaps he was also afraid that if the author he was reading said anything obscurely, some attentive or puzzled listener would want it explained, or would want to discuss some of the harder questions; and with his time spent that way he could not get through as many volumes as he wanted. Though preserving his voice, which was easily strained by a very little speaking, may be the truer reason for his reading silently. Whatever his intention was, in a man like that it was certainly good.

I certainly had no chance to ask what I wanted of that holy oracle of Yours, his heart, unless the question could be answered briefly. And those tides in me, which needed pouring out to him, required his full attention, and never found it. I did hear him every Sunday, rightly expounding the word of truth among the people; and I became more and more convinced that all the knots of clever slander which our deceivers had tied against the divine books could be untied. And when I also came to understand that "man created by You in Your own image" was not understood by Your spiritual sons — whom You have brought to birth again by grace out of our Catholic mother — as though they believed and conceived of You as bounded by a human shape, then, although I had not even the faintest shadow of a notion what a spiritual substance might be, I blushed with joy at having spent so many years barking not at the Catholic faith but at the inventions of physical imaginations. For I had been so rash and so irreligious that I had pronounced judgement and condemnation on things I ought to have learned by asking. For You, most high and most near, most hidden and most present, who have no limbs some larger and some smaller, but are wholly everywhere and nowhere in space, are not of any bodily shape; and yet You have made man in Your own image — and look, from head to foot he is contained in space.

Being ignorant, then, of how this image of Yours could exist, I should have knocked and put the difficulty as a question, asking how it was to be believed, instead of insultingly attacking it as if it were believed. So doubt about what to hold for certain gnawed at my heart the more sharply, the more ashamed I was that after being so long deceived by the promise of certainties, I had gone on chattering about so many uncertainties with a childish error and vehemence. That they were falsehoods became clear to me later; what I was certain of at the time was that they were uncertain, and that I had once counted them certain, when with a blind contentiousness I accused Your Catholic Church — which I now found, not indeed to be teaching truly yet, but at least not to be teaching what I had so severely criticised her for. So I was confounded, and turned round. And I was glad, my God, that the one and only Church, the body of Your only Son, in which the name of Christ had been laid on me as a baby, had no taste for these infantile notions; and that in her sound teaching she maintained no doctrine that would cram You, the creator of everything, into a space — however great and wide, but bounded everywhere by the outline of a human body.

I was glad too that the old Scriptures of the law and the prophets were now set before me to be read with different eyes from the ones that had once made them seem absurd, when I attacked Your holy people for thinking what in fact they did not think. And with joy I heard Ambrose in his sermons to the people often recommend this text as a rule: the letter kills, but the Spirit gives life. And he would draw aside the mystical veil and lay open spiritually what, taken literally, seemed to teach something unsound — teaching nothing here that offended me, though he taught things I did not yet know to be true. For I kept my heart from consenting to anything, afraid of falling headlong; and by hanging in suspense I was being killed the more surely. For I wanted to be as sure of the things I could not see as I was that seven and three make ten. I was not so mad as to think that even this could not be grasped; but I wanted other things to be as clear as this — whether physical things not present to my senses, or spiritual things, which I could not conceive of except as bodies. And by believing I might have been cured, so that the eyesight of my soul, being cleared, might somehow be turned towards Your truth, which stays for ever and fails in no part. But it happens that a man who has tried a bad doctor is afraid to trust himself to a good one; and so it was with the health of my soul, which could not be healed except by believing, and refused to be cured in case it should believe falsehoods — resisting Your hands, who have prepared the medicines of faith and applied them to the diseases of the whole world, and given them such great authority.

Being led on from this to prefer Catholic teaching, I felt that its way of proceeding was more modest and more honest, in that it required things to be believed that had not been demonstrated — whether because they could be demonstrated in themselves but not to certain people, or could not be demonstrated at all — whereas among the Manichees our credulity was mocked with a promise of certain knowledge, and then a great many fabulous and absurd things were forced on us to be believed, precisely because they could not be demonstrated. Then You, Lord, little by little, with the most gentle and merciful hand, touching and settling my heart, persuaded me. I thought of the countless things I believed which I had never seen and had not been present at: so many events in secular history, so many reports of places and cities I had not visited, so much from friends, so much from doctors, so much continually from other people, without believing which we should do nothing at all in this life. And last of all, with what unshakeable confidence I believed which parents I was born to, which I could never have known unless I believed it on hearsay. Considering all this, You persuaded me that the ones to blame were not the people who believed Your books, which You have established with such great authority among almost every nation, but the people who did not believe them; and that no attention should be paid to anyone who said to me, "How do you know that those Scriptures were given to mankind by the Spirit of the one true God?" For that was of all things the most to be believed, since no contentiousness of blasphemous questioning, out of all the mass of it I had read in the self-contradicting philosophers, could wring from me the belief that You exist, whatever You are, which I did not know, and that the government of human affairs belongs to You.

This I believed, sometimes more strongly and sometimes more weakly; but I always believed both that You existed and that You cared about us, though I did not know what to think about Your substance, or what road led to You or back to You. So since we were too weak to find out truth by abstract reasoning, and for that very reason needed the authority of holy writ, I had now begun to believe that You would never have given that writing such outstanding authority throughout the world unless You had meant to be believed in through it and looked for through it. For now that I had heard several of the passages that used to offend me by their strange sound expounded satisfactorily, I put them down to the depth of the mysteries; and its authority looked to me all the more venerable and all the more worthy of religious trust in that, while it lay open for everyone to read, it kept the majesty of its mysteries in its deeper meaning — coming down to everyone in the great plainness of its words and the lowliness of its style, and yet demanding the most intense effort from those who are not light-minded. So that it might take everyone into its open arms, and ferry a few across through narrow channels towards You — yet far more than if it did not stand up on such a height of authority, and did not draw crowds into its arms by its holy lowliness. These things I thought about, and You were with me. I sighed, and You heard me. I wavered, and You steered me. I wandered along the broad road of the world, and You did not abandon me.

I panted after honours, money, marriage — and You laughed at me. In these desires I went through the bitterest frustrations, and You were the kinder the less You allowed anything to grow sweet to me that was not You. Look at my heart, Lord, who wanted me to remember all this and confess it to You. Let my soul hold fast to You, now that You have pulled it free of the sticky birdlime of death. How wretched it was! And You kept irritating the feeling of its wound, so that it might leave everything else and turn to You, who are above everything and without whom nothing would exist at all — turn, and be healed.

How miserable I was then, and how You dealt with me to make me feel my misery, on the day when I was preparing to deliver a speech in praise of the emperor, in which I was going to tell a great many lies, and be applauded for lying by people who knew I was lying. My heart was panting with all these anxieties and boiling with the fever of thoughts that were eating me up. And going along one of the streets of Milan I noticed a poor beggar, with a full stomach, I suppose, joking and laughing. And I sighed, and spoke to the friends with me about the many sorrows of our own madness. For by all the efforts I was labouring at then, dragging the burden of my own wretchedness along under the goad of desire and making it heavier by dragging it, we were only hoping to arrive at the very cheerfulness that beggar had arrived at before us — and which we might never reach at all. What he had got with a few coins begged for, I was scheming after by many a laborious twist and turn: the joy of a temporary happiness. He certainly did not have the true joy — but with those ambitions of mine I was looking for one much less true. And he was certainly cheerful and I was anxious; he had no cares and I was full of fears. But if anyone asked me whether I would rather be cheerful or afraid, I would answer cheerful. And if he asked whether I would rather be what that man was or what I then was, I would choose to be myself, worn out with cares and fears — and that would be a bad judgement. For was it the truth? I ought not to have preferred myself to him on the grounds of being better educated, since I got no joy from my education but used it to please people; and not to instruct them, simply to please them. That is why You broke my bones with the rod of Your correction.

So away from my soul with those who say to her, "It makes a difference what a man's joy comes from. That beggar's joy came from drink; you wanted joy from glory." What glory, Lord? One that is not in You. For just as his was no true joy, so that was no true glory, and it overturned my soul the more. He would sleep off his drunkenness that same night; I had slept and got up with mine, and was going to sleep and get up with it again, for how many days You know, God. Yes, it does make a difference what a man's joy comes from; I know it, and the joy of a faithful hope lies incomparably beyond such emptiness. And in fact even then he was beyond me: he was genuinely the happier man, and not only because he was thoroughly soaked in cheerfulness while I was gutted with anxieties, but because he had got his wine by wishing people well, while I was looking for empty, swollen praise by lying. I said a good deal to this effect to my friends then, and I often noticed in them how things stood with me; and I found things were going badly with me, and grieved over it, and so doubled the trouble. And if any success smiled on me, I could not be bothered to snatch at it, since it flew away almost before I could get hold of it.

These were the things we lamented together, we who were living as friends; but I spoke about them mostly and most intimately with Alypius and Nebridius. Alypius was born in my own town, of leading people there, and was younger than I was. He had studied under me both when I first lectured in our town and afterwards at Carthage, and he loved me very much because I struck him as kind and learned, and I loved him for his great promise in virtue, which was conspicuous enough in one so young. But the whirlpool of Carthaginian habits, where those idle entertainments are hotly followed, had drawn him into the madness of the racetrack. And while he was being wretchedly tossed about in it, and I, teaching rhetoric there, had a public school, he was not yet attending my classes, because of some ill feeling that had arisen between his father and me. So I had found out how fatally besotted he was with the races, and was deeply grieved that he looked likely to throw away — or had already thrown away — such great promise; and yet I had no way of advising him, or of reclaiming him by some kind of pressure, either through a friend's kindness or a teacher's authority. For I assumed he thought of me as his father did. But he did not; and putting aside his father's attitude in the matter, he began to greet me, and to come into my lecture room now and then, listen a little, and go.

But I had forgotten to work on him, so that he should not ruin so good a mind through a blind, headlong appetite for empty amusements. But You, Lord, who steer the course of everything You have created, had not forgotten him, who was one day to be among Your children, a priest and a minister of Your sacrament. And so that his reform might plainly be credited to You, You brought it about through me, without my knowing it. For one day, as I sat in my usual place with my pupils in front of me, he came in, greeted me, sat down and set his mind on what I was discussing. I happened to have a passage in hand, and while I was explaining it a comparison with the chariot races occurred to me as likely to make what I wanted to convey pleasanter and plainer, seasoned with a biting mockery of those the madness had enslaved. God, You know that I was not thinking then of curing Alypius of that infection. But he took the whole thing to himself and thought I had said it purely for his sake. And where another man would have taken it as grounds for offence at me, that right-minded young man took it as grounds for being offended at himself, and for loving me more fiercely. For You had said it long ago, and put it in Your book: Rebuke a wise man and he will love you. But I had not rebuked him. You had — You who use everyone, knowing and unknowing, in the order You Yourself know, and that order is just. You made burning coals out of my heart and my tongue, to set light to the hopeful mind that was wasting away, and cure it. Let anyone keep quiet in Your praises who does not consider Your mercies, which confess to You out of the depths of my soul. For at that speech he burst out of the pit he had deliberately plunged into, and been blinded in by its wretched amusements; and he shook his mind free by a strong act of self-command, and all the filth of the racetrack flew off him, and he never went there again. After this he prevailed on his unwilling father to let him be my pupil. His father gave way and gave in. And Alypius, beginning to attend me again, was caught up in the same superstition I was, loving in the Manichees that show of self-restraint which he took to be genuine — whereas it was a senseless and seducing restraint, trapping precious souls that could not yet reach the depths of virtue and were easily taken in by the surface of a shadowy and counterfeit one.

Without giving up the secular career his parents had charmed him into, he had gone ahead of me to Rome to study law; and there he was carried away by an incredible eagerness for the gladiator shows, incredibly. He had been utterly opposed to such spectacles and detested them. Then one day he happened to be met by a number of his acquaintances and fellow students coming from dinner, and with the familiar violence of friends they hauled him — furiously refusing and resisting — into the amphitheatre, during those cruel and deadly shows. He protested as they went: "You can drag my body to that place and sit me down in it, but can you make me turn my mind or my eyes to those shows? I shall be absent while present, and so I shall beat you and them together." Hearing this, they took him along all the same, perhaps wanting to test that very thing, whether he could do what he said. When they got there and had taken whatever seats they could, the whole place was ablaze with that savage entertainment. But he shut the gateway of his eyes and forbade his mind to go out after such evil — and if only he had stopped his ears too! For in the fighting, when one man fell, the enormous roar of the whole crowd struck him hard; and overcome by curiosity, and as though prepared to see whatever it was and despise it and rise above it, he opened his eyes. And he was struck with a deeper wound in his soul than the man he had wanted to look at was struck in his body; and he fell more wretchedly than the man whose fall had raised the great shout that came in through his ears and unlocked his eyes, opening the way for a soul to be struck and beaten down — a soul that was bold rather than resolute, and all the weaker for having relied on itself, when it should have relied on You. For as soon as he saw the blood he drank in savagery along with it; and he did not turn away, but fixed his gaze and swallowed the frenzy without knowing it, and took delight in that guilty fight and was drunk on the bloody entertainment. He was no longer the man who had come in, but one of the crowd he had come into — a true companion of the men who had brought him. Why say more? He watched, he shouted, he caught fire, he carried away with him a madness that would goad him to come back, not only with the friends who first dragged him there but ahead of them, and dragging others in. And yet You pulled him out of it with the strongest and most merciful hand, and taught him to have confidence not in himself but in You. But that came later.

Even now, though, it was being stored up in his memory as a medicine for later. So was this. When he was still studying under me at Carthage, and was going over at midday in the marketplace what he had to recite by heart, as students do for practice, You allowed him to be arrested by the market officers as a thief. I imagine, our God, that You allowed it for no other reason than that a man who was going to turn out so great should begin to learn already that in judging cases a man must not be condemned by another man out of rash credulity. For as he was walking up and down by himself in front of the court, with his notebook and pen, a young man, a lawyer, the real thief, secretly brought a hatchet in, got as far as the lead gratings that fence in the silversmiths' shops without Alypius noticing him, and began to cut away the lead. But the noise of the hatchet was heard, and the silversmiths below began to stir and sent men to arrest whoever they found. Hearing their voices, he ran off, leaving his hatchet behind, afraid of being caught with it. Alypius, who had not seen him come in, was aware of him going, and saw how fast he made off. Wanting to know what the matter was, he went into the place — and there he found the hatchet, and was standing over it, wondering and examining it, when the men who had been sent found him alone with the hatchet in his hand, the noise of which had startled them and brought them there. They seized him and hauled him away, and gathering the people of the marketplace round them, boasted of having caught a notorious thief; and so he was taken off to be brought before the judge.

But this was as far as Alypius's lesson was to go. For at once, Lord, You came to the help of his innocence, of which You alone were the witness. As he was being led off either to prison or to punishment, they were met by an architect who had the chief responsibility for the public buildings. They were especially glad to run into him, since he was the man who usually suspected them of stealing the goods that went missing from the marketplace; now they could show him at last who was committing these thefts. But he had several times seen Alypius at the house of a certain senator whom he often went to pay his respects to; and recognising him at once, he took him aside by the hand, asked what the cause of such a disaster was, heard the whole story, and told everyone there, amid a great deal of uproar and threatening, to come with him. So they came to the house of the young man who had done it. In front of the door was a boy young enough to be likely to give the whole thing away, not realising it could harm his master; and he had gone with his master to the marketplace. As soon as Alypius remembered him he told the architect, who showed the boy the hatchet and asked whose it was. "Ours," he said at once — and being questioned further, he revealed everything. So the crime was transferred to that house, and the crowd who had begun to jeer at Alypius were put to shame; and the man who was to be a minister of Your word, and to examine many cases in Your church, went away with more experience and better instructed.

So I had found him at Rome, and he clung to me by the strongest of ties and went with me to Milan, both so as not to leave me and so as to practise something of the law he had studied — more to please his parents than himself. There he had sat three times as an assessor, with an incorruptibility that others found astonishing, while he found it more astonishing that anyone could prefer gold to honesty. His character was tested besides not only with the bait of greed but with the goad of fear. At Rome he was assessor to the count of the Italian treasury. There was at that time a very powerful senator to whom many people owed favours and many others owed fear. He wanted, by his usual authority, to have something allowed him that the laws did not allow. Alypius resisted. A bribe was offered; he despised it with his whole heart. Threats were made; he trampled on them. Everyone was amazed at so unusual a spirit, which neither wanted the friendship nor feared the enmity of a man so great and so hugely famous for his countless ways of doing good or harm. And the judge himself, whose adviser Alypius was, although he too was unwilling that it should be allowed, would not openly refuse, but put the matter off onto Alypius, claiming that Alypius would not let him do it — when in truth if the judge had done it, Alypius would have left. On one point connected with his studies he was nearly seduced: that he might have books copied for him at official rates. But consulting justice, he changed his mind for the better, reckoning the fairness that stopped him more profitable than the power that would have allowed it. These are small things; but he that is faithful in little is faithful also in much. And nothing can be empty that has come out of the mouth of Your truth: If you have not been faithful in the unrighteous Mammon, who will trust you with true riches? And if you have not been faithful in what belongs to another man, who shall give you what is your own? Being such a man, he was at that time attached to me, and wavered along with me about what course of life was to be taken.

Nebridius was there too. He had left his home near Carthage, and Carthage itself where he had lived a good deal, and left his excellent family estate and house and a mother who was not going to follow him, and had come to Milan for no other reason than to live with me in a burning search for truth and wisdom. Like me he sighed, like me he wavered: a passionate searcher after the true life and a most acute examiner of the hardest questions. So there were the mouths of three beggars, sighing out their needs to one another and waiting on You to give them their food at the proper time. And in all the bitterness that by Your mercy followed our worldly business, when we looked towards the end — why we should be suffering all this — darkness met us, and we turned away groaning and saying, How long is this going to go on? We said it often, and saying it we did not give the things up, because as yet nothing certain had dawned that we might take hold of once we had let them go.

And as I went over things again and again, what most astonished me was the length of time since that nineteenth year of mine, when I first began to burn with the desire for wisdom, and settled that when I found her I would abandon all the empty hopes and lying frenzies of pointless desires. And here I was, in my thirtieth year, stuck in the same mud, greedy to enjoy present things that slipped away and wasted my soul, while I said to myself: "Tomorrow I shall find it. It will show itself plainly and I shall take hold of it. Look, Faustus the Manichee will come and clear everything up. You great men, you Academics — is it true then that no certainty can be reached to order one's life by? No; let us search all the harder and not despair. Look, the things in the church's books are not absurd to us now, which once seemed absurd, and can be taken another way, in a good sense. I shall take my stand where my parents put me as a child, until the plain truth is found. But where is it to be looked for, and when? Ambrose has no free time; we have no free time to read. Where shall we even find the books? Where or when are we to get them? Who can we borrow them from? Let fixed times be set aside and hours arranged for the health of our soul. Great hope has dawned: the Catholic faith does not teach what we thought, and what we pointlessly accused it of; its instructed members hold it profane to believe God is bounded by the shape of a human body. And do we hesitate to knock, so that the rest may be opened to us? Our pupils take up the mornings; what do we do with the rest of the day? Why not this? But then when do we pay our respects to our powerful friends, whose favour we need? When do we compose what we can sell to students? When do we rest, and let our minds off this intensity of care?

"Let everything go to ruin. Let us dismiss these empty vanities and give ourselves to the one search for truth. Life is empty, death is uncertain; if it steals up on us suddenly, in what condition shall we leave here? Where shall we learn what we have neglected to learn here? Shall we not rather suffer the punishment for this neglect? What if death itself cuts off and ends all care and feeling? Then that must be settled. But God forbid it should be so! It is not empty and pointless that the outstanding dignity and authority of the Christian faith has spread over the whole world. God would never have done such great things for us if the life of the soul came to an end with the death of the body. So why put off abandoning worldly hopes and giving ourselves entirely to the search for God and the blessed life? But wait — those things are pleasant too; they have a real and not a small sweetness. We must not give them up lightly, for it would be a disgrace to go back to them again. Look, it is no great matter now to obtain some post — and then what more should we want? We have plenty of powerful friends; if nothing else turns up and we are in a hurry, at least a governorship could be given us. And a wife with some money of her own, so that she does not add to our expenses. And that would be the limit of our wanting. Many great men, very much worth imitating, have devoted themselves to the study of wisdom while married."

While I went over all this, and these winds shifted and drove my heart this way and that, time went on; and I put off turning to the Lord, and from day to day I put off living in You, and did not put off dying daily in myself. Loving a happy life, I was afraid of it in its own home, and looked for it by running away from it. I thought I would be too miserable unless I was folded in a woman's arms; and it did not occur to me that the medicine of Your mercy could cure that weakness, since I had not tried it. As for self-control, I supposed it was in our own power, though I could not find that power in myself, being foolish enough not to know what is written: nobody can be self-controlled unless You give it — and that You would give it, if I knocked at Your ears with inward groaning and threw my care on You with a settled faith.

Alypius did keep me from marrying, arguing that if I married we could by no means live together in the love of wisdom with undistracted leisure, as we had long wanted to. In this matter he was at that time remarkably pure — the more remarkably since at the very start of his adolescence he had gone that way and had not got stuck in it, but had felt remorse and revulsion at it, and had lived with complete self-control ever since. But I opposed him with the examples of men who had cherished wisdom as married men, and served God acceptably, and kept their friends and loved them faithfully. I fell a long way short of their greatness of spirit; tied down by the disease of the flesh and its deadly sweetness, I dragged my chain along, dreading to be unfastened, and pushing his good advice away as though it were the hand of someone trying to unchain me, and as though my wound had been rubbed by it. What is more, the serpent spoke to Alypius himself through me, weaving pleasant traps with my tongue and laying them across his path, where his virtuous and free feet might be caught.

For he was astonished that I, whom he thought well of, should be stuck so fast in the birdlime of that pleasure as to insist, whenever we discussed it, that I could never live a single life. And when I saw him astonished, I said in my own defence that there was a great difference between his momentary and barely remembered experience of that life, which he could therefore easily despise, and my continued familiarity with it — and that if the honourable name of marriage were added to mine, he ought not to be surprised that I could not despise it. And then he too began to want to be married: not overcome by any desire for the pleasure, but out of curiosity. He said he would like to know what this thing was, without which my life, which he found so agreeable, would to me not be life at all but a punishment. For his mind, free of that chain, was amazed at my slavery, and through the amazement was going on to a wish to try it, and from there to the trial itself, and from there perhaps to sink into the very bondage he was amazed at — since he was willing to make a covenant with death, and the man who loves danger will fall into it. For whatever honour there may be in the work of ordering a marriage and a family well, it moved neither of us much. What tormented me for the most part was the habit of satisfying an appetite that could not be satisfied, which held me prisoner; what was leading him captive was an admiring curiosity. That was where we were, until You, most high, not abandoning our dust, and pitying us in our misery, came to our help by wonderful and secret ways.

Continual efforts were made to get me married. I proposed, I was accepted, chiefly through my mother's efforts, on the grounds that once I was married the health-giving baptism might wash me clean — and she was glad to see me being made fitter for it daily, and noticed that her prayers and Your promises were being fulfilled in my faith. At that time, both at my request and out of her own longing, she begged You daily with strong cries of the heart to show her something about my future marriage in a vision. You never would. She did see certain empty and fantastic things, of the kind the energy of a human mind busy with a subject throws together; and she told me about them, not with the confidence she usually had when You showed her something, but dismissively. She said she could tell the difference between Your revelations and her own soul's dreams by a certain feeling she could not put into words. Still, the matter was pushed on, and a girl was asked for in marriage, two years under the proper age; and since she was pleasing, we waited for her.

And a number of us friends, discussing the matter together and loathing the turbulent turmoil of human life, had debated and almost decided on living apart from business and the bustle of people. We were to manage it like this: we would bring in whatever each of us could get, and make one household of everything, so that through the honesty of our friendship nothing would belong to anyone in particular, but the whole thing, drawn from all of us, should belong as a whole to each, and everything to everybody. We thought there might be about ten people in this society, some of them very rich, especially Romanianus, a man from my own town and a very close friend of mine since childhood, whom the difficult tangles of his affairs had brought up to court. He was the keenest on this project, and his voice carried great weight in it, because his ample estate far exceeded anyone else's. We had also settled that two of us should serve as annual officers and provide everything necessary, leaving the rest undisturbed. But when we began to consider whether the wives that some of us already had, and others hoped to have, would allow this, the whole plan we had been shaping so well fell to pieces in our hands, and was smashed and thrown aside. From there we went back to sighs and groans, and our feet went back to following the broad and beaten roads of the world; for there were many thoughts in our heart, but Your counsel stands for ever. Out of that counsel You laughed at ours and prepared Your own, meaning to give us food at the proper time and to fill our souls with blessing.

Meanwhile my sins were multiplying; and when the woman I lived with was torn from my side as an obstacle to my marriage, my heart, which had held fast to her, was torn and wounded and bleeding. And she went back to Africa, vowing to You never to know another man, leaving with me the son I had by her. But I in my unhappiness could not imitate even a woman. I could not bear the delay, since it would be two years before I could have the girl I had asked for; and being not so much a lover of marriage as a slave of lust, I got myself another woman, though not a wife, so that by the servitude of a lasting habit the disease of my soul might be kept going at full strength, or even increased, into the domain of marriage. And that wound of mine was not healed which had been made by cutting the first woman away; after the inflammation and the sharpest pain it went dead, and my pain grew less sharp and more hopeless.

Praise to You, glory to You, fountain of mercies. I was growing more wretched and You were coming nearer. Your right hand was always ready to pull me out of the mud and wash me thoroughly, and I did not know it. Nothing called me back from a still deeper pit of bodily pleasures except the fear of death and of Your judgement to come, which through all my changes never left my chest. And when I argued with my friends Alypius and Nebridius about the nature of good and evil, I held that in my view Epicurus would have carried off the prize, if I had not believed that after death there remained a life for the soul and places of repayment according to what men deserve — which Epicurus refused to believe. And I asked: if we were immortal and going to live in perpetual bodily pleasure with no fear of losing it, why should we not be happy, and what else should we be looking for? I did not know that great misery was involved in this very thing: that being so sunk and blinded, I could not make out that light of excellence and beauty which is to be embraced for its own sake, which the eye of flesh cannot see and which is seen by the inner man. Nor did I, unhappy as I was, consider where it came from that even about these things, foul as they were, I talked with pleasure to my friends; and that I could not be happy without friends, even on the notions of happiness I had then, however great the supply of bodily pleasures. And these friends I loved for their own sake alone, and I felt that they loved me back for mine alone.

What twisted roads! Pity the reckless soul that hoped, by leaving You, to get something better. It turned this way and that, on its back and its sides and its front, and everything hurt; and You alone are rest. And look, You are close at hand, and free us from our miserable wanderings, and set us on Your road, and comfort us and say: "Run; I will carry you; I will bring you through; and there too I will carry you."

Book Seven: The Platonists and the Problem of Evil

My evil and disgusting youth was dead by now and I was passing into early manhood — and the older I grew the dirtier I got with empty things, since I could not imagine any substance except the kind that can be seen with these eyes. I did not think of You, God, in the shape of a human body; from the time I first heard anything about wisdom, I had always avoided that, and I was glad to find the same view in the faith of our spiritual mother, Your Catholic Church. But what else to conceive of You I did not know. And I, a man, and such a man, tried to conceive of You, the sovereign, only, true God. In the depths of my soul I did believe that You were incorruptible and injury-proof and unchangeable; because although I did not know how or where the idea came from, I saw plainly and was certain that what can be corrupted must be inferior to what cannot, and I unhesitatingly preferred what could not be damaged to what could take damage, and the unchangeable to things subject to change. My heart cried out passionately against all my phantoms, and with this one blow I tried to beat away from my mind's eye the whole unclean swarm buzzing round it. And barely had they been driven off when, in the blink of an eye, they crowded thickly back round me, flew at my face and blotted it out; so that although I was not conceiving of You in the shape of a human body, I was still forced to conceive of You — that incorruptible, injury-proof, unchangeable thing which I preferred to the corruptible and damageable and changeable — as existing in space, whether poured into the world or spread infinitely outside it. Because anything I conceived without this space seemed to me to be nothing; utterly nothing, not even a void. It was as though, if a body were taken out of its place and the place remained empty of everything — of earth and water, air and sky — it would still remain an empty place, a spacious kind of nothing.

Being so gross-hearted, then, and not clear even to myself, I thought that whatever was not spread out over some space, or thinned or thickened or swollen out, and did not or could not take on some of these dimensions, was altogether nothing. For my heart ranged over the same sorts of shape my eyes were used to ranging over. And I did not see that the very act of the mind by which I was shaping those images was not of that kind at all — and yet it could not have shaped them unless it were itself something great. So I tried to conceive of You, life of my life, as vast, going through infinite spaces on every side, penetrating the whole mass of the universe, and beyond it in every direction through immeasurable, boundless space; so that the earth had You, and the sky had You, and everything had You, and everything was bounded within You, while You were bounded nowhere. For just as the body of the air above the earth does not stop the light of the sun from passing through it, penetrating it not by bursting it or cutting it but by filling it completely, so I thought that the substance not only of the sky and air and sea but of the earth as well was pervious to You, so that in all its parts, the largest and the smallest, it took in Your presence, by a secret breathing that directed everything You have created from within and from without. That was my guess, only because I could not conceive of anything else, and it was false. For on this account a larger part of the earth would contain a larger portion of You, and a smaller part a smaller portion; and everything would be full of You in such a way that an elephant's body would contain more of You than a sparrow's, in proportion to its being bigger and taking up more room; and so You would be making the several portions of Yourself present to the several portions of the world in fragments — large ones to the large, tiny ones to the tiny. But that is not what You are. And You had not yet lit up my darkness.

It was enough for me, Lord, to set against those deceived deceivers and dumb chatterers — since Your word never sounded out of them — the argument Nebridius used to put forward long ago, when we were still at Carthage, which staggered all of us who heard it. "That nation of darkness which the Manichees are in the habit of setting up as an opposing mass over against You: what could it have done to You, if You had refused to fight it? If they answer that it could have done You some harm, then You would be subject to injury and corruption. But if it could do You no harm, then no reason has been given for Your fighting it — and fighting in such a way that a certain portion or limb of You, an offspring of Your very substance, should be mixed with opposing powers and with natures You did not create, and be corrupted by them and changed for the worse to the point of being turned from happiness into misery, and need help to get it out and clean it up. And this offspring of Your substance was supposed to be the soul, which, being enslaved and defiled and corrupted, Your Word — free, pure and whole — might rescue; while that Word itself would still be corruptible, since it was of one and the same substance. So if they say that You, whatever You are, that is, the substance by which You are what You are, is incorruptible, then all these claims are false and to be cursed; and if they say it is corruptible, then the statement shows itself false and disgusting on the face of it." So this argument of Nebridius' was enough against people who deserved to be vomited up altogether from a stomach that could not hold them; because there was no way out for them, without a horrible blasphemy of heart and tongue, thinking and speaking of You like that.

But I still did not clearly and easily understand the cause of evil, though I held and was firmly convinced that You, our Lord the true God, who made not only our souls but our bodies, and not only our souls and bodies but every being and every thing, were undefilable and unalterable and in no degree changeable. And yet whatever the cause was, I saw that it must be looked for in a way that did not force me to believe the unchangeable God changeable — in case I became the very evil I was looking for. So I set about the search free from that anxiety at least, being certain of the falsity of what the people I recoiled from with my whole heart maintained. For I could see that in enquiring into the origin of evil they were filled with evil, since they preferred to think that Your substance suffered evil rather than that their own committed it.

And I strained to grasp what I now heard: that free will is the cause of our doing evil, and Your just judgement the cause of our suffering it. But I could not make it out clearly. So when I tried to draw my soul's vision up out of that deep pit, I was plunged back into it; and trying often, I was plunged back as often. But one thing did lift me a little way into Your light: I knew as surely that I had a will as that I was alive. When I willed or refused something, I was quite certain that it was nobody but myself who willed and refused; and I very nearly saw that this was where the cause of my sin lay. As for what I did against my will, I saw that I was undergoing it rather than doing it, and I judged that to be not my fault but my punishment — by which, since I held You to be just, I quickly admitted I was not being punished unjustly. But then again I said: who made me? Was it not my God, who is not only good but goodness itself? So where did it come from, my willing evil and refusing good, so that I am justly punished for it? Who put this in me and grafted this bitter plant into me, when I was made entirely by my sweetest God? If the devil is the author, then where does the devil come from? And if he too, by his own perverse will, turned from a good angel into a devil, where did the evil will come from in him by which he became a devil, seeing the whole nature of angels was made by that best of creators? By these thoughts I was pushed under and choked again — though not brought down to that hell of error, where nobody confesses to You, of thinking that You suffer evil rather than that man does it.

For I was trying to work out the rest as a man who had already found that the incorruptible must be better than the corruptible; and I therefore confessed that You, whatever You are, are incorruptible. For no soul ever was or ever will be able to conceive anything better than You, who are the sovereign and the best good. And since it is completely true and certain that the incorruptible is preferable to the corruptible, as I now preferred it, then if You were not incorruptible I could have arrived in thought at something better than my God. So where I saw the incorruptible to be preferable to the corruptible, there I ought to look for You, and there notice where evil itself was — that is, where corruption comes from, by which Your substance can by no means be damaged. For corruption does not damage our God in any way: not by any will, not by any necessity, not by any unforeseen accident. Because he is God, and what he wills is good, and he himself is that good; and being corrupted is not good. Nor are You forced into anything against Your will, since Your will is not greater than Your power. It would have to be greater if You were greater than Yourself; for the will and the power of God are God himself. And what can be unforeseen by You, who know everything? There is no nature among things that You do not know. And what more need we say about why the substance that God is should not be corruptible, seeing that if it were, he would not be God?

And I looked for where evil comes from, and looked in an evil way, and did not see the evil in the very way I was looking. I set before the sight of my spirit the whole of creation, everything we can see in it — sea, earth, air, stars, trees, mortal creatures — and everything in it we do not see, the vault of the sky, and all the angels too, and all its spiritual inhabitants. But my imagination arranged even these beings in places, as though they were bodies. And I made one great mass out of Your creation, sorted by the kinds of bodies in it: some real bodies, and some the ones I had made up for spirits. I made this mass huge — not as it actually was, which I could not know, but as I found convenient, and finite in every direction. And You, Lord, I imagined surrounding and penetrating it on every side, but infinite in every direction: as though there were a sea, everywhere and on every side, through unmeasured space, one single boundless sea, and it held inside it a sponge, huge but bounded, and that sponge would have to be filled in every part from that unmeasurable sea. That was how I conceived of Your creation: finite itself, and full of You, the infinite. And I said, Look, this is God, and this is what God has created; and God is good, mightily and incomparably better than all of this; but still he, the Good, created them good, and see how he surrounds and fills them. Where then is evil, and where does it come from, and how did it creep in here? What is its root and what is its seed? Or has it no being at all? Then why do we fear and avoid what does not exist? Or if we fear it for nothing, then that very fear is an evil, by which the soul is goaded and racked for nothing — and so much the greater an evil, in that we have nothing to fear and are still afraid. So either what we fear is evil, or else the evil is that we fear. Where does it come from, then, seeing God, the Good, created all these things good? He indeed, the greater and chief good, created these lesser goods; but creator and created, all of them are good. Where does evil come from?

Or was there some evil matter which he made and shaped and ordered, and yet left something in it that he did not turn into good? But why? Had he no power to turn and change the whole of it, so that no evil should be left in it, seeing he is almighty? Last of all, why would he make anything at all out of it, instead of using that same almightiness to make it not exist? Or could it exist against his will? Or if it existed from eternity, why did he allow it to be so through infinite stretches of past time, and only decide so long afterwards to make something out of it? Or if he suddenly decided to do something now, then this is what the almighty should have done rather: that this evil matter should not exist, and he alone should be, the whole, true, sovereign and infinite good. Or if it was not good that he who was good should not also frame and create something good, then he might have taken that evil matter away and reduced it to nothing, and formed good matter to create everything out of. For he would not be almighty if he could not create something good without the help of matter he had not created himself. These thoughts I turned over in my miserable heart, weighed down with the most gnawing anxiety in case I should die before I had found the truth. And yet the faith of Your Christ, our Lord and saviour, as professed in the Catholic Church, was firmly fixed in my heart — on many points still unformed, certainly, and drifting away from the rule of doctrine, but my mind did not leave it entirely; rather it took in more and more of it every day.

By this time I had also rejected the lying divinations and irreligious ravings of the astrologers. Let Your own mercies confess to You for this too, God, out of the very depths of my soul. For it was You, entirely You — since who else calls us back from the death of all errors, but the Life that cannot die and the Wisdom that needs no light and lights up the minds that need it, by which the universe is steered, right down to the spinning leaves on the trees? You made provision for the obstinacy with which I resisted Vindicianus, a sharp old man, and Nebridius, a young man of admirable ability. The first asserted vehemently, and the second often said, though with some hesitation, that there is no such art for foreseeing what is to come, and that men's guesses are a kind of lottery: out of the many things they say will happen, some actually do, without the speakers knowing why, since they stumble on it by talking so much. So You provided me with a friend who was no negligent consulter of astrologers — and not much skilled in those arts either, but, as I say, a curious consulter of them, who nevertheless knew one thing which he said he had heard from his father, and had no idea how far it went towards overturning any respect for that art.

This man, Firminus by name, having had a liberal education and been well taught in rhetoric, consulted me as a very dear friend about what I thought, according to his so-called horoscope, of certain business of his in which his worldly hopes had risen. And I, having by now begun to lean towards Nebridius' opinion, did not altogether refuse to guess and tell him what came into my unsettled mind, though I added that I was now almost convinced these were empty and ridiculous follies. At that he told me his father had been very keen on such books, and had a friend as enthusiastic about them as himself; and by studying and discussing together they fanned their affection for these toys into a flame, to the point that they would note the exact moments at which even the dumb animals bred about their houses gave birth, and then note the position of the heavens, so as to make fresh experiments in this so-called art. He said, then, that he had heard from his father that at the time when his mother was about to give birth to him, Firminus, a maidservant of his father's friend was also pregnant — which could not escape her master, who took the most exact care to know the births even of his puppies. And so it happened that, the one for his wife and the other for his servant, with the most careful observation, counting days, hours and even the smaller divisions of hours, both women were delivered at the same instant; so that both men were obliged to allow the same horoscope, down to the smallest points, the one for his son and the other for his newborn slave. For as soon as the women went into labour, each man sent word to the other of what was happening in his house, and had messengers ready to send off the moment there was news of the actual birth — which each had easily arranged for in his own household. And the two sets of messengers, he said, met at such an equal distance from each house that neither man could make out any difference in the position of the stars, or in any other minute detail. And yet Firminus, born to a high position in his parents' house, ran his course along the gilded roads of life, grew rich and rose to honours; while that slave went on serving his masters with his yoke never loosened at all, as Firminus, who knew him, told me.

When I heard these things and believed them, told by a man of such credibility, all my resistance gave way. And first I tried to reclaim Firminus himself from that obsession, by telling him that on inspecting his horoscope I ought, if I were going to predict truly, to have seen in it parents of standing in the neighbourhood, a family noble in its own city, high birth, a good upbringing, a liberal education — whereas if that slave had consulted me about the same horoscope, since it was his as well, I ought again, to tell him equally truly, to see in it the lowest of pedigrees, a slave's condition, and everything else utterly at odds with the first. So if I spoke the truth, I would be saying different things from the same horoscope; and if I said the same thing, I would be speaking falsely. From which it followed quite certainly that whatever is said truly after inspecting a horoscope is said not by art but by chance; and whatever is said falsely is not from ignorance of the art but from a failure of the chance.

With that opening made, I turned the like of it over in my own mind, so that none of those old fools who lived by the trade — whom I was longing to attack and refute with derision — could argue against me that Firminus had told me a lie, or that his father had lied to him. I fixed my thoughts on twins, who for the most part come out of the womb so close together that the small interval between them, whatever force people may claim it has in the nature of things, cannot be noted by human observation or expressed at all in the charts the astrologer has to inspect in order to pronounce truly. And they cannot be pronouncing truly, since looking at the same charts he would have had to predict the same things of Esau and Jacob, and the same things did not happen to them. So either he must be speaking falsely; or, if truly, then looking at the same charts he must not give the same answer. So it is not by art but by chance that he speaks truly. For You, Lord, most righteous ruler of the universe, while neither the man consulting nor the man consulted knows it, bring it about by Your hidden inspiration that the man consulting should hear what, according to the hidden deserts of souls, he ought to hear, out of the unsearchable depth of Your just judgement — to whom let no man say, What is this? Why that? Let him not say it, for he is a man.

By now, my helper, You had freed me from those chains. And I looked for where evil comes from, and found no way through. But You did not let any wavering of my thought carry me away from the faith by which I believed both that You exist and that Your substance is unchangeable, and that You care about men and will judge them, and that in Christ Your Son our Lord, and in the holy Scriptures which the authority of Your Catholic Church pressed on me, You had laid down the way of man's salvation to the life that comes after this death. With these things safe and immovably settled in my mind, I anxiously looked for where evil comes from.

What were the labour pains of my teeming heart, what groans, my God! And Your ears were open there too and I did not know it. And when I searched hard in silence, those silent contritions of my soul were loud cries to Your mercy. You knew what I was going through, and no one else did. For what portion of it was distilled through my tongue into the ears of my closest friends? Did the whole tumult of my soul, which neither time nor speech was enough for, reach them? Yet the whole of it went up to Your hearing, everything I roared out from the groanings of my heart; and my desire was in front of You, and the light of my eyes was not with me. That light was within and I was outside; nor was it confined to a place, while I was intent on things that are in places. And there I found no resting place; they did not receive me in a way that let me say, "This is enough, this is good"; and they did not let me go back to where things might be good enough for me. For I was above these things and below You; and You are my true joy when I am subject to You, and You had made the things You created below me subject to me. And this was the true balance, the middle region of my safety: to remain in Your image, and by serving You, to rule the body. But when I rose up proudly against You and charged at the Lord with my neck under the thick bosses of my shield, even these lower things were set above me and pressed me down, and there was no space anywhere to breathe. They met my eye on all sides in heaps and crowds, and in my thoughts their images presented themselves unasked, whenever I wanted to come back to You, as though they were saying, "Where are you going, unworthy and defiled?" And these things had grown out of my wound, for You humble the proud like a man who has been wounded; and it was through my own swelling that I was cut off from You — my face swollen with pride closed up my eyes.

But You, Lord, remain for ever, and are not angry with us for ever, because You pity our dust and ashes, and it pleased You to reform my deformities. And by inward goads You stirred me up, so that I should be uneasy until You were made plain to my inward sight. So by the secret hand of Your treatment my swelling was reduced, and the troubled and dimmed eyesight of my mind was healed day by day by the stinging ointment of healthy sorrows.

And You, wanting first to show me how You resist the proud but give grace to the humble, and by how great an act of mercy You had marked out for men the way of humility — in that Your Word was made flesh and lived among men — arranged for me, through a man swollen with the most unnatural pride, to get hold of certain books of the Platonists, translated from Greek into Latin. And in them I read, not in these very words but to exactly this effect, backed up with many and various arguments: that in the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and the Word was God; the same was in the beginning with God; all things were made by him, and without him was nothing made that was made; what was made by him is life, and the life was the light of men, and the light shines in the darkness, and the darkness did not take it in. And that the soul of man, though it bears witness to the light, is not itself that light, but the Word of God, being God, is the true light that lights every man who comes into the world. And that he was in the world, and the world was made by him, and the world did not know him. But that he came to his own, and his own did not receive him; and that as many as did receive him, to them he gave the power to become the sons of God, as many as believed in his name — that I did not read there.

Again, I read there that God the Word was born not of flesh nor of blood, nor of the will of man, nor of the will of the flesh, but of God. But that the Word was made flesh and lived among us, I did not read there. For I found in those books that it was said in many different ways that the Son was in the form of the Father, and did not think it robbery to be equal with God, since by nature he was the same substance. But that he emptied himself, taking the form of a servant, made in the likeness of men and found in fashion as a man, and humbled himself and became obedient to death, and that the death of the cross; and that therefore God raised him from the dead and gave him a name above every name, so that at the name of Jesus every knee should bow, of things in heaven and things on earth and things under the earth, and every tongue confess that the Lord Jesus Christ is in the glory of God the Father — those books do not have. That before all time and above all time Your only-begotten Son remains unchangeable, co-eternal with You, and that souls receive of his fullness so that they may be blessed, and are renewed so as to be wise by sharing in the wisdom that lives in them, is there. But that at the proper time he died for the ungodly, and that You did not spare Your only Son but gave him up for us all, is not there. For You hid these things from the wise and revealed them to little children, so that those who labour and are heavy laden might come to him and he might give them rest, because he is meek and lowly in heart; and the meek he directs in judgement, and the gentle he teaches his ways, seeing our lowliness and our trouble and forgiving all our sins. But those who are lifted up on the lofty walk of some supposedly higher learning do not hear him saying, Learn from me, for I am meek and lowly in heart, and you shall find rest for your souls. Although they knew God, they do not glorify him as God, nor are they thankful, but grow empty in their thinking, and their foolish heart is darkened; claiming to be wise, they became fools.

And so I read there too how they had exchanged the glory of Your incorruptible nature for idols and various shapes, for the likeness of the image of corruptible man, and of birds and animals and creeping things — that Egyptian food, in fact, for which Esau lost his birthright, since Your firstborn people worshipped the head of a four-footed beast instead of You, turning back in their hearts towards Egypt, and bowing Your image, which is their own soul, before the image of a calf that eats hay. These things I found here, and I did not feed on them. For it pleased You, Lord, to take away the reproach of being the lesser from Jacob, so that the elder should serve the younger; and You called the Gentiles into Your inheritance. And I had come to You from among the Gentiles, and I set my mind on the gold that You wanted Your people to take out of Egypt, since it was Yours wherever it was. And You said to the Athenians through Your apostle that in You we live and move and have our being, as one of their own poets had said. And those books certainly came from there. But I did not set my mind on the idols of Egypt, which they served with Your gold, exchanging the truth of God for a lie, and worshipping and serving the creature more than the creator.

And being warned by this to come back to myself, I went into my inmost self with You as my guide. And I was able to, because You had become my helper. And I went in, and with the eye of my soul, such as it was, I saw above that same eye of my soul, above my mind, the unchangeable light. Not this ordinary light that any creature can look at, nor a greater light of the same kind, as though this one's brightness were multiplied many times over and filled all space with its greatness. It was not that kind of light but another, utterly different from these. Nor was it above my soul as oil floats above water, or as the sky is above the earth. It was above me because it made me, and I was below it because I was made by it. He who knows the truth knows what that light is, and he who knows it knows eternity. Love knows it.

Eternal truth, and true love, and beloved eternity! You are my God; I sigh for You day and night. When I first knew You, You lifted me up so that I could see there was something for me to see, and that I was not yet the kind of person who could see it. And You beat back the weakness of my sight, blazing Your beams upon me at full strength, and I trembled with love and dread. And I found myself to be far away from You, in a country unlike Yours, as though I heard this voice of Yours from on high: "I am the food of grown men. Grow, and you shall feed on me. And you shall not change me into yourself as you do the food of your body; you shall be changed into me." And I learned that You correct a man for his wrongdoing, and that You made my soul shrivel like a spider's web. And I said, "Is truth nothing, then, because it is not spread through space, finite or infinite?" And You cried out to me from far off, "Yes it is: I am who I am." And I heard as the heart hears, and I had no room left to doubt; I would sooner have doubted that I was alive than that truth exists, which is clearly seen and understood by the things that are made.

And I looked at the other things below You, and I saw that they neither altogether are nor altogether are not. They are, since they are from You; and they are not, since they are not what You are. For that truly is which stays unchangeably. So it is good for me to hold fast to God; because if I do not remain in him I cannot remain in myself, while he, remaining in himself, makes everything new. And You are the Lord my God, since You have no need of anything good from me.

And it was made plain to me that things which are corrupted are nevertheless good; they could not be corrupted if they were supremely good, and they could not be corrupted if they were not good at all. For corruption does damage, and unless it reduced some goodness it could do no damage. So either corruption does no damage, which is impossible; or, which is quite certain, everything that is corrupted is deprived of some good. But if they were deprived of all good, they would cease to exist. For if they still existed and could no longer be corrupted, they would be better than before, since they would go on incorruptibly. And what could be more monstrous than to claim that things become better by losing all their good? So if they were deprived of all good they would no longer exist. Therefore as long as they exist they are good; therefore whatever exists is good. So that evil whose origin I was looking for is not a substance at all; for if it were a substance it would be good. Either it would be an incorruptible substance, and so a supreme good; or a corruptible substance, which could not be corrupted unless it were good. So I saw, and it was made plain to me, that You made all things good, and that there is no substance at all that You did not make. And because You did not make everything equal, therefore everything exists; because each thing is good, and taken all together they are very good, since our God made everything very good.

And to You nothing whatever is evil — not only to You, but to Your creation as a whole, since there is nothing outside it that could break in and wreck the order You have appointed for it. But among its parts, some things are counted evil because they do not harmonise with certain others; while those very things harmonise with others, and are good, and in themselves are good. And all these things that do not harmonise together do harmonise with the lower part, which we call earth, which has its own cloudy and windy sky to match it. Far be it from me to say, "These things ought not to exist." If I saw nothing but these, I would certainly long for better; but even for these alone I would have to praise You. For that You are to be praised is shown from the earth by dragons and all the deeps, fire and hail, snow and ice and stormy wind fulfilling Your word; mountains and all hills, fruit trees and all cedars; wild animals and all cattle, creeping things and flying birds; kings of the earth and all peoples, princes and all judges of the earth; young men and girls, old men and children, praising Your name.
