But when from heaven these praise You — when all Your angels praise You in the heights, our God, and all Your hosts, sun and moon, all the stars and the light, the heaven of heavens and the waters above the heavens, praise Your name — then I no longer longed for anything better, because I could take them all in together. And with a sounder judgement I understood that the things above were better than the things below, but that everything together was better than the things above by themselves.

There is no soundness in people who are displeased by anything in Your creation, any more than there was in me, when a great deal of what You have made displeased me. And because my soul did not dare to be displeased with my God, it preferred not to count as Yours whatever displeased it. That is how it had gone off into the opinion of two substances, and had no rest, and talked nonsense. And coming back from that, it had made itself a God stretched through the infinite measures of all space, and thought this was You, and set it up in its heart, and had again become the temple of its own idol, which You detest. But after You had soothed my head without my knowing it, and closed my eyes so that they should not look at emptiness, I let go a little of my old self, and my madness was lulled to sleep; and I woke up in You, and saw You infinite, but in a different way — and this seeing did not come from the flesh.

And I looked back at other things, and I saw that they owed their being to You, and that they were all bounded in You, but in a different way: not as being in a space, but because You hold all things in Your hand, in Your truth. And all things are true so far as they exist, and there is no falsehood except when something is thought to be that is not. And I saw that all things harmonise not only with their places but with their times. And that You, who alone are eternal, did not begin to work after countless stretches of time had gone by; because all stretches of time, both the ones that have passed and the ones still to come, neither go nor come except through You, who work and remain.

And I noticed, and found nothing strange in it, that bread which is pleasant to a healthy mouth is disgusting to a sick one; and that light which delights sound eyes is offensive to sore ones. Your righteousness displeases the wicked, and so much the more do the viper and the reptiles, which You created good and fitted to the lower parts of Your creation — parts which the very wicked also fit into, the more so the more unlike You they are, while they fit the higher creatures the more they grow like You. And I asked what wickedness is, and found it to be no substance but a perversion of the will, turned aside from You, God, the highest, towards these lower things, throwing away its own insides and swelling up on the outside.

And I was amazed that I now loved You, and not a phantom in Your place. And yet I did not press on to enjoy my God; I was carried up to You by Your beauty and soon carried back down from You by my own weight, sinking with grief into these lower things. That weight was the habit of the flesh. And yet a memory of You stayed with me, and I did not doubt at all that there was someone for me to hold fast to, only that I was not yet the sort of person who could hold fast to You — because the body that is corrupted weighs down the soul, and the earthly tent weighs down the mind that has many things to think about. And I was quite certain that Your invisible things, from the creation of the world, are clearly seen and understood by the things that are made, even Your eternal power and divinity.

For I examined why it was that I admired the beauty of bodies, in the sky or on the earth, and what helped me to judge soundly about changeable things and pronounce, "This ought to be like this, this ought not." And in examining why I judged as I did, given that I did judge, I had found the unchangeable and true eternity of truth above my changeable mind. So step by step I moved up from bodies to the soul, which perceives through the bodily senses; and from there to the soul's inward faculty, to which the bodily senses report external things, and which is as far as the faculties of animals reach; and from there again to the reasoning faculty, to which what has been taken in by the bodily senses is referred for judgement. And this faculty, finding that it too was something variable in me, raised itself to its own understanding, and drew my thoughts away from the power of habit, pulling itself out of the crowds of contradictory phantoms, so as to find what that light was in which it was bathed when it cried out without any doubt at all that the unchangeable was to be preferred to the changeable — and from which it also knew the unchangeable, since unless it had known it in some way it would have had no sure ground for preferring it to the changeable. And so with the flash of one trembling glance it arrived at That Which Is. And then I saw Your invisible things understood by the things that are made. But I could not hold my gaze there; and my weakness was struck back, and I was thrown onto my usual habits again, carrying away with me only a loving memory of it, and a longing for something I had, as it were, caught the smell of and was not yet able to eat.

Then I looked for a way of getting strength enough to enjoy You, and did not find it until I embraced the mediator between God and men, the man Christ Jesus, who is over all, God blessed for ever, calling to me and saying, I am the way, the truth, and the life — and mixing the food I was unable to take in with our flesh. For the Word was made flesh, so that Your wisdom, by which You created everything, might provide milk for our infancy. For I did not hold to my Lord Jesus Christ, I in my humbling to the humble one; nor did I yet know what his weakness was going to teach us. For Your Word, the eternal truth, far above the higher parts of Your creation, raises up to itself those who submit; but in this lower world it built itself a lowly house out of our clay, by which to bring down from themselves those who were to submit, and carry them over to himself — reducing their swelling and nursing their love, so that they should go no further in self-confidence, but rather agree to become weak, seeing at their feet divinity made weak by putting on our coats of skin; and being exhausted, throw themselves down on it, so that it might rise and lift them up.

But I thought otherwise. I conceived of my Lord Christ only as a man of outstanding wisdom, whom nobody could be compared to; especially since, having been marvellously born of a virgin, he seemed accordingly, through God's care for us, to have reached that great eminence of authority as an example of despising temporary things in order to gain immortality. But what mystery there was in "the Word was made flesh" I could not even imagine. All I had learned from what is written about him was that he ate and drank, slept, walked, rejoiced in spirit, was sad, and talked; that flesh did not attach itself to Your Word by itself, but with a human soul and mind. Everybody knows this who knows the unchangeableness of Your Word, which I now knew as far as I could, and did not doubt at all. For to move the limbs of the body by an act of will at one moment and not the next, to be moved by a feeling at one moment and not the next, to deliver wise sayings through human signs at one moment and to keep silent the next, all belong to a soul and mind subject to variation. And if these things were falsely written of him, everything else would be under the same suspicion, and there would be no saving faith for mankind left in those books. So since they were written truly, I acknowledged a complete man in Christ: not only a man's body, nor a body with a sensitive soul but no rational one, but a real man — whom I judged to be preferred above others not only for being a shape of truth, but for a certain great excellence of human nature and a fuller share in wisdom.

But Alypius imagined that Catholics believed God to be so clothed in flesh that besides God and flesh there was no soul at all in Christ, and did not think a human mind was ascribed to him. And because he was firmly convinced that the actions recorded of Christ could only be done by a living and rational creature, he moved the more slowly towards the Christian faith. But when he later understood that this was the error of the Apollinarian heretics, he was glad and fell in with the Catholic faith. I confess it was somewhat later that I learned how, in that saying "the Word was made flesh", the Catholic truth is distinguished from the falsehood of Photinus. For the rejection of heretics makes the doctrines of Your church and sound teaching stand out more clearly. There must also be heresies, so that the approved may be shown up among the weak.

But having read those books of the Platonists, and been taught by them to search for an incorporeal truth, I saw Your invisible things understood by the things that are made; and though I was thrown back, I did grasp what it was that the darkness of my mind was stopping me from contemplating. I was sure that You existed, and were infinite, and yet were not spread through space, finite or infinite; and that You truly are, who are always the same, varying in no part and by no motion; and that everything else is from You, on this one absolutely sure ground, that it exists. Of these things I was sure, and too unsure to enjoy You. I chattered like an expert; and if I had not looked for Your way in Christ our saviour, I should have turned out not an expert but a corpse. For by now I had begun to want to seem wise, being full of my own punishment; and I did not mourn, but rather sneered, puffed up with knowledge. For where was that love that builds on the foundation of humility, which is Christ Jesus? And when were those books going to teach me it? I believe it was for this reason that You wanted me to come across them before I studied Your Scriptures: so that it should be printed on my memory how they affected me, and so that afterwards, when my spirit had been tamed by Your books and my wounds touched by Your healing fingers, I might tell the difference between presumption and confession — between people who saw where they had to go and could not see the road, and the road that leads not merely to seeing the blessed country but to living in it. For if I had been formed first in Your holy Scriptures, and You had grown sweet to me through familiar use of them, and I had then come across those other volumes, they might perhaps have pulled me off the solid ground of true religion; or, if I had held on to the healthy frame of mind I had taken in from Scripture, I might have thought it could have been got from the study of those books alone.

So I seized most eagerly on that venerable writing of Your Spirit, and above all on the apostle Paul. And with that, the difficulties vanished in which he had once seemed to me to contradict himself, and his argument to disagree with the evidence of the Law and the Prophets. The face of that pure word appeared to me as one and the same, and I learned to rejoice with trembling. So I began; and whatever truth I had read in those other books, I found here amid the praise of Your grace — so that a man who sees may not boast as though he had not received, not only what he sees but the fact that he sees it, for what does he have that he has not received? — and so that he may not only be advised to look at You, who are always the same, but healed so as to hold You; and so that a man who cannot see into the distance may still walk the road by which he can arrive and see and hold. For though a man delights in the law of God according to the inner man, what is he to do about that other law in his limbs, which wars against the law of his mind and takes him prisoner under the law of sin that is in his limbs? For You are righteous, Lord; but we have sinned and done wrong and acted wickedly, and Your hand has grown heavy on us, and we are justly handed over to that ancient sinner, the king of death, because he persuaded our will to be like his own will, by which he did not stay in Your truth.

What is a wretched man to do? Who shall free him from the body of this death, but only Your grace, through Jesus Christ our Lord, whom You have begotten co-eternal with Yourself and formed at the beginning of Your ways; in whom the prince of this world found nothing worth death, and yet killed him, so that the record of debt that stood against us was wiped out? None of this is in those books. Those pages do not present the picture of this devotion, the tears of confession, Your sacrifice, a troubled spirit, a broken and contrite heart, the salvation of the people, the bridal city, the pledge of the Holy Spirit, the cup of our redemption. Nobody there sings, Shall not my soul be subject to God? For from him comes my salvation; he is my God and my salvation, my defender, and I shall not be shaken again. Nobody there hears him calling, Come to me, all you who labour. They are too proud to learn from him, because he is meek and lowly in heart; for You have hidden these things from the wise and prudent and revealed them to little children. For it is one thing to see the land of peace from a wooded mountaintop and find no way there, trying uselessly along impassable roads, harassed and ambushed by runaways and deserters under their captain the lion and the dragon; and quite another to keep to the road that leads there, guarded by the army of the heavenly commander, where deserters from the heavenly army do not plunder, since they avoid that road as a torture. These things sank into me wonderfully, when I read that least of Your apostles, and had considered Your works, and trembled greatly.

Book Eight: The Garden at Milan

My God, let me remember with thanksgiving, and confess to You, Your mercies to me. Let my bones be soaked with Your love, and let them say to You: who is like You, Lord? You have broken my chains apart; I will offer You the sacrifice of thanksgiving. And how You broke them I shall now tell; and everyone who worships You, when they hear this, will say, "Blessed be the Lord, in heaven and on earth; great and wonderful is his name."

Your words had stuck fast in my heart, and I was fenced in on all sides by You. Of Your eternal life I was now certain, though I saw it in a riddle and as through a glass. I had stopped doubting that there is an incorruptible substance from which every other substance comes; and I no longer wanted to be more certain about You, but steadier in You. But as for my life in this world, everything was wavering, and my heart had to be purged of the old leaven. The Way, the saviour himself, pleased me well enough, but I still shrank from going through its narrowness. And You put it into my mind, and it seemed a good idea to me, to go to Simplicianus, who struck me as a good servant of Yours, and in whom Your grace shone. I had also heard that from his earliest youth he had lived entirely devoted to You. By now he was well on in years, and after so long a life spent following Your ways so eagerly, he seemed to me likely to have gathered a great deal of experience — and so he had. I wanted him to draw on that store and tell me, once I had laid out my anxieties in front of him, which was the best road for a man in my condition to walk in Your paths.

For I could see the church was full, and one man went this way and another that. And I was unhappy that I was leading a worldly life. Now that my old desires no longer inflamed me with hopes of honour and profit, it was a very heavy burden to carry so heavy a slavery. Compared with Your sweetness and the beauty of Your house, which I loved, those things gave me no more pleasure. But I was still held captive by the love of a woman; and the apostle did not forbid me to marry, although he advised something better, chiefly wishing that all men were as he was himself. But being weak, I chose the more indulgent place; and because of that one thing alone, I was tossed about in everything else, faint and worn out with withering cares, since in other matters I was forced against my will to conform to a married life which I was given over to and enslaved by. I had heard from the mouth of the truth that there are some eunuchs who have made themselves eunuchs for the kingdom of heaven's sake; but, he says, let the man who can take it, take it. Certainly all men are empty who are ignorant of God, and could not from the good things that are seen find out the one who is good. But I was no longer in that emptiness; I had got past it, and by the shared testimony of all Your creatures I had found You, our creator, and Your Word, God with You, and one God together with You, by whom You created everything. There is yet another kind of irreligion: knowing God and not glorifying him as God, and not being thankful. Into that I had fallen too, but Your right hand held me up and took me out of it, and You set me where I might recover. For You have said to man: reverence for the Lord — that is wisdom. And, Do not want to seem wise, because those who claimed to be wise became fools. But I had now found the fine pearl which I should have sold everything to buy, and I hesitated.

So I went to Simplicianus, who had been father to Ambrose, now a bishop, in receiving Your grace, and whom Ambrose truly loved as a father. To him I described the maze of my wanderings. But when I mentioned that I had read certain books of the Platonists, which Victorinus, once professor of rhetoric at Rome and, as I had heard, dead a Christian, had translated into Latin, he said how glad he was that I had not come across the writings of other philosophers, which are full of fallacies and deceptions, after the elements of this world, whereas the Platonists led in many ways towards belief in God and his Word. Then, to urge me towards the humility of Christ, hidden from the wise and revealed to little ones, he spoke of Victorinus himself, whom he had known very intimately at Rome; and about him he told me a story I shall not keep back, since it contains great praise of Your grace, to be confessed to You.

Here was an old man, deeply learned and skilled in the liberal arts, who had read and weighed so many works of the philosophers; the teacher of so many noble senators, who as a monument of his excellent service had both deserved and been given a statue in the Roman forum, which men of this world reckon a high honour. Up to that age he was a worshipper of idols and a sharer in the sacrilegious rites that almost all the nobility of Rome were devoted to, and he had inspired the people with a love of

	Anubis, the barking god, and all
	the monster gods of every kind, who fought
	against Neptune, Venus and Minerva:

gods Rome had once conquered and now worshipped, all of which the aged Victorinus had defended for so many years with thundering eloquence. And now he did not blush to be the child of Your Christ and a newborn baby at Your font, bending his neck under the yoke of humility and submitting his forehead to the disgrace of the cross.

Lord, Lord, who have bent the heavens and come down, and touched the mountains and made them smoke, by what means did You get Yourself into that man's heart? He used to read the holy Scripture, Simplicianus said, and studied and searched all the Christian writings most carefully, and would say to Simplicianus — not publicly, but privately and as a friend — "You should know that I am already a Christian." To which he answered, "I shall not believe it, and I shall not count you among the Christians, until I see you in the church of Christ." And the other would say, teasing, "Is it the walls, then, that make Christians?" He often said he was already a Christian, and Simplicianus as often gave the same answer, and the joke about the walls was as often repeated. For he was afraid of offending his friends, proud demon-worshippers, and he supposed the weight of their enmity would fall on him from the height of their Babylonian dignity, as from the cedars of Lebanon which the Lord had not yet brought down. But after he had gathered firmness by reading and hard thought, and had come to be afraid of being denied by Christ before the holy angels if he was now afraid to confess him before men — and thought himself guilty of a serious offence in being ashamed of the sacraments of the humility of Your Word, while not being ashamed of the sacrilegious rites of those proud demons, whose pride he had imitated and whose rites he had taken up — he became bold-faced against emptiness and shamefaced before the truth, and said suddenly and unexpectedly to Simplicianus, as Simplicianus himself told me: "Let us go to the church; I want to be made a Christian." And he, unable to contain himself for joy, went with him. And having been admitted to the first sacrament and become a catechumen, not long afterwards he gave in his name to be born again by baptism — Rome astonished, the church rejoicing. The proud saw it and were furious; they ground their teeth and melted away. But the Lord God was Your servant's hope, and he paid no attention to empty things and lying madness.

And at last, when the hour came for him to make his profession of faith — which at Rome those about to come to Your grace deliver from a raised place, in front of all the faithful, in a set form of words learned by heart — the presbyters, he said, offered Victorinus the chance to make his profession more privately, as was done for those likely to be alarmed by shyness. But he chose to profess his salvation in the presence of the holy crowd. "For it was not salvation that he had taught in his rhetoric, and he had professed that publicly. How much less, then, should he be afraid of Your gentle flock when pronouncing Your word, when he had not been afraid of a mad crowd while delivering his own?" So when he went up to make his profession, everybody who knew him whispered his name to his neighbour with a note of congratulation. And who there did not know him? A low murmur ran through all the mouths of the rejoicing crowd: Victorinus! Victorinus! There was a sudden burst of delight that they saw him, and a sudden hush so that they could hear him. He pronounced the true faith with an admirable boldness, and everyone wanted to draw him into their very heart; and by their love and joy they did draw him there, for those were the hands they drew him with.

Good God, what is it that happens in a man, that he should rejoice more over the salvation of a soul that had been despaired of, and freed from greater danger, than if there had always been hope for it or the danger had been less? For You too, merciful Father, rejoice more over one man who repents than over ninety-nine righteous people who need no repentance. And we hear with great gladness, every time we hear it, with what joy the sheep that had strayed is brought back on the shepherd's shoulders, and the coin is put back in Your treasury with the neighbours celebrating alongside the woman who found it. And the joy of the solemn service of Your house forces tears out of us, when it is read in Your house about Your younger son, that he was dead and is alive again, was lost and is found. For You rejoice in us, and in Your holy angels, holy through holy love. For You are always the same; and everything that does not stay the same and does not last for ever, You know for ever in the same way.

What then happens in the soul, when it is more delighted at finding or recovering the things it loves than if it had always had them? Other things testify to this too, and everything is full of witnesses crying out, "That is how it is." The victorious general celebrates his triumph — and he would not have conquered if he had not fought, and the more danger there was in the battle the more joy there is in the triumph. The storm throws the sailors about and threatens shipwreck; they all go pale at the approach of death; then the sky and the sea grow calm, and they are as overjoyed as they had been terrified. A friend is ill, and his pulse is alarming; everyone who wants him to recover is sick in mind along with him. He gets better, and though he cannot yet walk with his old strength, there is a joy that was not there before, when he walked about sound and strong. Even the pleasures of human life are things people get at by difficulties — not only difficulties that fall on us unlooked for and against our will, but trouble we deliberately choose and go looking for. Eating and drinking give no pleasure unless the pinch of hunger and thirst comes first. Heavy drinkers eat salty things to work up an uncomfortable heat, which the drink then quenches, and that gives pleasure. And it is the custom that a girl who is engaged should not be handed over at once, in case a husband should hold cheap a woman he had not sighed after as a fiancée.

This law holds in foul and cursed joy; it holds in permitted and lawful joy; it holds in the very purest perfection of friendship; it holds in the man who was dead and lived again, was lost and was found. Everywhere the greater joy is ushered in by the greater pain. What does this mean, Lord my God, when You are everlastingly a joy to Yourself, and some things around You rejoice in You for ever? What does it mean that this portion of things ebbs and flows like this, in turn displeased and reconciled? Is this their allotted measure? Is this all You have assigned them, when from the highest heavens to the lowest earth, from the beginning of the world to the end of the ages, from the angel to the worm, from the first motion to the last, You set each thing in its place and bring each about in its season, everything good after its kind? How high You are in the highest and how deep in the deepest! And You never leave, and we can scarcely get back to You.

Get up, Lord, and act; stir us up and call us back; kindle us and draw us; set us on fire, grow sweet to us; let us love now, let us run. Do not many people come back to You out of a deeper hell of blindness than Victorinus, and come near and are lit up, receiving that light — and those who receive it receive from You the power to become Your sons? But if they are less known to everybody, then even the people who know them rejoice less for them. For when many rejoice together, each of them also has a more abundant joy, because they set each other alight. And also because people known to many have more influence towards salvation, and lead the way for many to follow. And so those who went before them rejoice greatly in them too, because they are not rejoicing in them alone. Far be it that in Your tent the rich should be preferred to the poor, or the noble to the low-born; seeing that You have rather chosen the weak things of the world to confound the strong, and the low things of this world and the things despised You have chosen, and the things that are not, so that You might bring to nothing the things that are. And yet even that least of Your apostles, by whose tongue You sounded out these words, when through his campaign Paulus the proconsul had his pride conquered and was made to pass under the easy yoke of Your Christ, and became a subject of the great king — even he chose to be called Paul instead of his former name Saul, as evidence of so great a victory. For the enemy is more defeated in a man he has more hold over, and through whom he has hold of more people. And he has more hold over the proud, through their high birth, and through them of more people, through their authority. So the more the heart of Victorinus was valued, which the devil had held as an impregnable possession, and the tongue of Victorinus, that mighty and sharp weapon with which he had killed many, the more abundantly ought Your sons to rejoice — because our king has bound the strong man, and they saw his vessels taken from him and cleaned and made fit for Your honour, and become useful to the Lord for every good work.

But when that man of Yours, Simplicianus, told me this about Victorinus, I was on fire to imitate him — which was exactly why he had told it to me. And when he added how in the days of the emperor Julian a law was made forbidding Christians to teach the liberal arts or oratory, and how Victorinus obeyed that law and chose to give up the school of words rather than Your Word, by which You make the tongues of the dumb eloquent, he seemed to me not so much resolute as fortunate, in having found a chance to wait on You alone. And that was what I was sighing for — tied up as I was, not with somebody else's irons but by my own iron will. My will was held by the enemy, and out of it he had made a chain and bound me with it. For out of a twisted will came lust; and lust served became habit; and habit not resisted became necessity. By these links, joined together as it were, which is why I called it a chain, a hard slavery held me fast. But the new will that had begun to be in me, to serve You freely and to want to enjoy You, God, the only reliable pleasure, was not yet able to overcome my old wilfulness, which age had made strong. So my two wills, one new and one old, one of the flesh and one of the spirit, struggled inside me, and by their quarrelling they tore my soul apart.

So I understood from my own experience what I had read: how the flesh craves against the spirit and the spirit against the flesh. I was on both sides myself; but I was more myself in the part I approved of than in the part I disapproved of. For in the second part it was now mostly not me, since in much of it I was suffering against my will rather than acting willingly. And yet it was through me that habit had got this power of making war on me, because I had come willingly where I did not want to be. And who has any right to complain, if a just punishment follows the sinner? I no longer had my old excuse — that the reason I still hesitated to rise above the world and serve You was that the truth was not fully established for me — because now it was. But I was still tied to the service of the earth and refused to fight under Your flag; and I was as afraid of being freed of all my encumbrances as I ought to have been of being encumbered with them.

So I was held down pleasantly by the baggage of this present world, as a man is by sleep; and the thoughts I turned over about You were like the efforts of somebody trying to wake, who is overcome by a heavy drowsiness and sinks back into it. Nobody wants to sleep for ever, and in everybody's sober judgement being awake is better; and yet a man who feels a heavy lethargy in all his limbs usually puts off shaking off sleep, and, though half annoyed with himself, gives in to it with pleasure even after it is time to get up. In the same way I was sure it would be much better to give myself up to Your love than to give myself over to my own appetite; but although the first course satisfied me and had the better of the argument, the second pleased me and had the better of me. And I had nothing to answer when You called to me, Wake up, sleeper, and rise from the dead, and Christ will give you light. When You showed me on every side that what You said was true, I was convicted by the truth and had nothing at all to answer with except those dull, drowsy words: "In a minute." "Soon." "Just leave me a little longer." But "soon, soon" never came to now, and my "little longer" went on a long time. It was no use my delighting in Your law according to the inner man, when another law in my limbs fought against the law of my mind and took me prisoner under the law of sin that was in my limbs. For the law of sin is the violence of habit, by which the mind is dragged and held even against its will — and deservedly, since it fell into the habit willingly. So who was to rescue me, wretched as I was, from the body of this death, but Your grace alone, through Jesus Christ our Lord?

And how You rescued me from the chains of desire, which held me most tightly to the craving of the flesh, and from the drudgery of worldly business, I shall now tell, and confess to Your name, Lord, my helper and my redeemer. In the middle of growing anxiety I was going about my usual work and sighing to You every day. I went to Your church whenever I was free from the business under whose burden I groaned. Alypius was with me, released after his third term from his legal work and waiting for someone to sell his advice to, as I sold the skill of speaking, if teaching can impart it. Nebridius had by now, out of consideration for our friendship, agreed to teach under Verecundus, a citizen and grammarian of Milan and a very close friend of us all, who badly wanted, and claimed by the rights of friendship, the faithful help he greatly needed from our circle. Nebridius was not drawn to this by any desire for advantage, since he could have made much more of his learning if he had wanted to; but being the kindest and gentlest of friends, he would not fail in a good turn or brush off our request. He handled it very discreetly, though, avoiding becoming known to people who count as great in this world, and avoiding the distraction of mind that goes with it, wanting to keep his mind free and at leisure for as many hours as possible to look for or read or hear something about wisdom.

Then one day, when Nebridius was away — I do not remember why — a man called Ponticianus came to see Alypius and me, a fellow African and a high official at the emperor's court. What he wanted with us I do not know, but we sat down to talk; and it happened that on a table in front of us, meant for some game, he noticed a book, took it up, opened it, and, contrary to what he expected, found it was the apostle Paul. He had thought it would be one of the books I was wearing myself out teaching. At that he smiled and looked at me, and said how glad and how surprised he was to have suddenly come across this book, and this book alone, in front of me. For he was a Christian, and baptised, and often bowed himself before You, our God, in church, in frequent and sustained prayer. When I told him that I was taking great pains over those Scriptures, a conversation started, prompted by something he said, about Antony the Egyptian monk, whose name was held in high honour among Your servants, though it was unknown to us until that hour. When he discovered this, he dwelt on the subject the longer, informing us and marvelling at our ignorance of so great a man. And we sat astonished, hearing Your wonderful works attested so fully, in times so recent, almost our own, and done in the true faith and the Catholic Church. We were all amazed: we that they were so great, and he that they had never reached us.

From there his talk turned to the communities in the monasteries, and their holy ways, a sweet-smelling incense to You, and the fruitful deserts of the wilderness, which we knew nothing about. And there was a monastery at Milan, full of good brothers, outside the city walls, under the fostering care of Ambrose — and we did not know about it. He went on talking and we listened in intent silence. And he told us how one afternoon at Trier, when the emperor was taken up with the games, he and three companions went out for a walk in some gardens near the city walls; and as they happened to walk in pairs, one went aside with him and the other two wandered off by themselves. And these two, in their wandering, came on a small house lived in by certain servants of Yours, poor in spirit, of whom is the kingdom of heaven; and there they found a little book containing the life of Antony. One of them began to read it, and to admire it, and to catch fire from it; and as he read, to think about taking up such a life himself, and giving up his official career to serve You. And these two were of the kind they call agents for public affairs. Then suddenly, filled with a holy love and a sober shame, angry with himself, he turned his eyes on his friend and said: "Tell me, I beg you, what are we hoping to get by all this labour of ours? What are we aiming at? What are we serving for? Can our hopes at court rise higher than being the emperor's favourites? And what in that is not fragile and full of dangers? And through how many dangers do we arrive at a greater danger? And when do we get there? Whereas a friend of God — if I want it, I become one right now." So he spoke; and, in labour with the birth of a new life, he turned his eyes back to the book and read on, and was changed inside, where You could see; and his mind was stripped of the world, as soon appeared. For as he read, and the waves of his heart rolled up and down, he raged at himself a while, then saw clearly and decided on a better course; and being Yours by now, he said to his friend, "I have broken free of those hopes of ours and made up my mind to serve God; and I am beginning on it now, this hour, in this place. If you do not want to imitate me, do not stand in my way." The other answered that he would stay with him, to share so glorious a reward and so glorious a service. And so both of them, being Yours now, were building the tower at the necessary cost: giving up everything they had and following You.

Then Ponticianus and the other man, who had been walking in another part of the garden, came looking for them and found them there, and reminded them that they should get back, since the day was nearly over. But the two told them their decision and their intention, and how that resolve had begun and settled in them, and begged them, if they would not join, at least not to make trouble about it. And the others, though not changed at all from what they had been, still wept for themselves, so he said, and congratulated them piously, and commended themselves to their prayers; and so, with their hearts still lingering on the earth, they went off to the palace. But the other two, fixing their hearts on heaven, stayed in that little house. And both of them were engaged to be married; and when their fiancées heard about it, they too dedicated their virginity to God.

That was Ponticianus' story. But while he was speaking, Lord, You were turning me round to face myself, taking me out from behind my own back where I had put myself so as not to look at myself, and setting me in front of my own face, so that I might see how foul I was, how crooked and dirty, spotted and ulcerated. And I looked, and I was horrified — and there was nowhere I could run to away from myself. And if I tried to turn my eyes away from me, he went on with his story, and You set me over against myself again and pushed me in front of my own eyes, so that I might find out my wickedness and hate it. I had known it, but I had pretended not to see it, turned a blind eye and forgotten it.

But now, the more warmly I loved the men whose healthy resolve I was hearing about, who had handed themselves over to You entirely to be cured, the more I loathed myself in comparison with them. Some twelve years of my life had gone by since my nineteenth, when on reading Cicero's Hortensius I had been stirred to a serious love of wisdom; and still I was putting off giving up merely earthly happiness and devoting myself to the search for that other thing, when not only the finding of it but the very search for it was to be preferred to the treasures and kingdoms of this world even when already found, and to bodily pleasures even when spread around me at my command. But I, wretched, most wretched, at the very beginning of my adolescence had begged You for chastity and said, "Give me chastity and self-control, but not yet." For I was afraid You would hear me too soon and cure me too soon of the disease of lust, which I wanted satisfied rather than put out. And I had wandered down crooked roads into a sacrilegious superstition, not that I was sure of it, but I preferred it to the alternatives, which I did not investigate religiously but attacked maliciously.

And I had thought that the reason I put off from day to day rejecting the hopes of this world and following You alone was that nothing certain appeared to steer my course by. And now the day had come when I was to be laid bare to myself, and my conscience was to reproach me. "Where are you now, my tongue? You said that while the truth was uncertain you did not care to throw off the baggage of empty things. Now it is certain, and that burden is still weighing you down, while people who have neither worn themselves out searching for it nor spent ten years and more thinking about it have had their shoulders lightened and been given wings to fly away." So I was gnawed at inside and thrown into a horrible confusion of shame, while Ponticianus talked. And when he had brought his story and his business to a close, he went his way, and I went into myself.

What did I not say against myself? With what whips of condemnation did I not lash my soul, to make it follow me as I struggled to go after You! And it drew back; it refused, and did not excuse itself. Every argument was used up and refuted; what was left was a mute shrinking; and it dreaded, as it would dread death, being held back from the flow of that habit it was wasting to death in.

Then, in this great struggle of my inner household, which I had raised so violently against my own soul in the chamber of my heart, troubled in mind and in face, I turned on Alypius. "What is the matter with us?" I burst out. "What is it? What did you hear? The unlearned get up and take heaven by force, and here we are with our learning and no heart, wallowing in flesh and blood! Are we ashamed to follow because others have gone ahead — and not ashamed of not even following?" Something like that I said, and my fever of mind tore me away from him, while he stared at me in astonishment and said nothing. For it was not my usual tone; and my forehead, my cheeks, my eyes, my colour, my voice said more of what was in my mind than the words I spoke.

There was a small garden belonging to our lodging, which we had the use of, as we had of the whole house, since the owner, our landlord, was not living there. The tumult in my chest had driven me out there, where nobody could interrupt the burning quarrel I had started with myself, until it should come out as You knew and I did not. I was only healthily out of my mind and dying in order to live, knowing what an evil thing I was and not knowing what good thing I was shortly to become. So I went off into the garden and Alypius came after me. His being there did not disturb my privacy; and how could he have left me in such a state? We sat down as far from the house as we could. I was troubled in spirit, and violently indignant that I was not entering into Your will and Your covenant, my God, which every bone in my body cried out to me to enter, and praised to the skies. And we do not get there by ships or carriages or on foot — not even as far as I had come from the house to the place where we were sitting. For to go there, and to arrive there, was nothing but to will to go, but to will it strongly and completely; not to twist and turn a crippled, half-divided will this way and that, with one part sinking as another rose.

Lastly, in the very fever of my hesitation, I made with my body a lot of the movements that people sometimes want to make and cannot, because they lack the limbs, or the limbs are tied, or weakened by illness, or hindered somehow. If I tore my hair, or hit my forehead, or locked my fingers together round my knee, I did it because I willed it. But I might have willed it and not done it, if my limbs had not had the power of movement to obey. So there were many things I did where willing was not by itself being able; and I did not do the thing I wanted incomparably more to do, and which I would be able to do the moment I willed it — because the moment I willed it, I would be willing it wholly. For in that case the ability was the same thing as the will, and to will was to do. And yet it was not done; and my body obeyed the feeblest willing of my soul more readily, moving its limbs at a nod, than my soul obeyed itself in carrying out, by the will alone, this one momentous act of will.

Where does this monstrous thing come from, and why? Let Your mercy shine out so that I can ask, in case the hidden punishments of men and the darkest agonies of the sons of Adam may perhaps answer me. Where does this monstrous thing come from, and why? The mind commands the body and is obeyed instantly; the mind commands itself and meets resistance. The mind orders the hand to move, and there is such readiness that the order is barely distinguishable from the obedience — and yet the mind is mind and the hand is body. The mind orders the mind, its own self, to will, and it does not do it. Where does this monstrous thing come from, and why? It orders itself, I say, to will, and would not give the order unless it willed; and what it orders is not done. But it does not will completely, and so it does not order completely. It orders only so far as it wills; and the thing ordered is not done exactly so far as it does not will. For the will orders that there be a will — not another will, but itself. But it does not order completely, and so what it orders does not happen. If the will were complete it would not even order itself to be, because it would be already. So it is not monstrous to will partly and refuse partly; it is a disease of the mind, which does not stand up wholly, being lifted by truth and weighed down by habit. And that is why there are two wills: because one of them is not complete, and what the one lacks the other has.

Let them perish from Your presence, God, as empty talkers and seducers of the soul do perish — the people who notice that in deliberating there are two wills, and then claim there are two minds in us of two kinds, one good and one evil. They are the evil ones themselves while they hold these evil views; and they will become good when they hold the truth and agree with the truth, so that Your apostle can say to them: you were once darkness, but now you are light in the Lord. But they want to be light not in the Lord but in themselves, imagining the nature of the soul to be what God is; and so they are made a thicker darkness by a dreadful arrogance, since they have gone further back from You, the true light that lights every man who comes into the world. Watch what you say, and blush for it; come near to him and be enlightened, and your faces shall not be ashamed. When I was deliberating about serving the Lord my God now, as I had long meant to, it was I who willed and I who refused: I, myself. I neither willed completely nor refused completely. So I was at war with myself and torn apart by myself. And this tearing happened to me against my will, and pointed not to the presence of another mind but to the punishment of my own. So it was no longer I who was doing it, but the sin that lived in me: the punishment of a sin committed more freely, in that I was a son of Adam.

For if there are as many contrary natures as there are conflicting wills, there will be not two but many. If a man is deliberating whether to go to their meeting-house or to the theatre, these Manichees cry out, "There you are: two natures. One good, pulling this way; one bad, pulling back that way." Where else, they say, could this hesitation between conflicting wills come from? But I say both are bad: the one that pulls towards them just as much as the one that pulls back to the theatre. They do not believe the will that pulls towards them is anything but good. Then what if one of us were deliberating, and in the struggle of his two wills were torn between going to the theatre and going to our church? Would these Manichees not be at a loss for an answer? For either they must admit, which they would hate to do, that the will leading to our church is good, just as theirs is who have received their mysteries and are held by them; or they must suppose two evil natures and two evil souls in conflict in one man, and then what they say will not be true, that one is good and the other bad; or they must be converted to the truth and stop denying that when a man deliberates, one soul wavers between contrary wills.

So let them stop saying, when they see two conflicting wills in one man, that the conflict is between two contrary souls of two contrary substances from two contrary principles, one good and one bad. For You, the true God, disprove and check and convict them. Take a case where both wills are bad: a man deliberating whether to kill a man with poison or with a sword; whether to seize this piece of somebody else's property or that one, when he cannot have both; whether to buy pleasure with extravagance or keep his money out of greed; whether to go to the races or the theatre, if both are on the same day; or, thirdly, to burgle somebody's house, if he gets the chance; or, fourthly, to commit adultery, if he has the opportunity at the same time — all these coinciding at the same moment, and all equally wanted, and none of them possible at once. They tear the mind between four, or, given the vast variety of things people want, even more conflicting wills; and yet the Manichees do not claim there are that many different substances. And the same holds for good wills. For I ask them: is it good to take pleasure in reading the apostle? Is it good to take pleasure in a sober psalm? Is it good to discuss the gospel? They will answer, to each, that it is good. What then if all of them give equal pleasure, and all at the same moment? Do these different wills not pull the mind about while it deliberates which to choose? And yet they are all good, and are at odds until one is chosen, and the whole will, which was divided among many, is carried towards it. So too when eternity delights us from above, and the pleasure of a temporary good holds us down below, it is one and the same soul that does not will this or that with a whole will; and that is why it is torn apart with painful perplexities, setting one thing first because of the truth, and not setting the other aside because of habit.

So I was sick in the soul and tormented, accusing myself far more bitterly than usual, rolling and turning in my chain until it should be wholly broken — the chain that now held me only just, but did still hold me. And You, Lord, pressed on me in my inward parts with a severe mercy, redoubling the lashes of fear and shame, in case I should give way again and, not bursting that one slight remaining tie, it should recover its strength and bind me tighter. For I said to myself, "Let it be done now, let it be done now." And as I spoke I very nearly did it. I all but did it, and did not do it; and yet I did not sink back to where I had been, but held my ground close by and caught my breath. And I tried again, and fell a little less short, and a little less, and all but touched it and took hold of it — and still did not reach it, or touch it, or take hold of it, hesitating to die to death and live to life. The worse that I was used to had more power over me than the better that I was not used to; and the very moment when I was to become other than I was, the nearer it came to me the greater the horror it struck into me. And it did not strike me back or turn me away; it held me in suspense.

The sheerest trifles of trifles and vanities of vanities, my old mistresses, still held me. They tugged at the garment of my flesh and whispered softly, "Are you throwing us off? And from this moment shall we never be with you again? And from this moment shall this and that be forbidden you for ever?" And what was it they suggested in what I have called "this and that" — what did they suggest, my God? Let Your mercy turn it away from the soul of Your servant. What filth they suggested, what disgrace! And by now I heard them much less than half, since they were not showing themselves openly and contradicting me but muttering behind my back, as it were, and secretly plucking at me as I went away, to make me look round at them. And yet they did hold me up, so that I hesitated to tear myself free of them and shake them off and leap over to where I was being called — while a violent habit kept saying to me, "Do you imagine you can live without them?"

But by now it spoke very faintly. For on the side I had set my face towards, and trembled to go, there appeared to me the chaste dignity of Self-Control: serene, and cheerful without being loose, honestly enticing me to come and not to hesitate, and stretching out to receive and embrace me, her holy hands full of crowds of good examples. There were so many young men and girls there, a multitude of the young and of every age, dignified widows and old women who had never married; and Self-Control herself in all of them, not barren but a fruitful mother of children of joy, by You her husband, Lord. And she smiled at me with a persuasive mockery, as if to say, "Can you not do what these young men and these girls can do? Or can any of them do it in themselves, and not rather in the Lord their God? The Lord their God gave me to them. Why are you standing on yourself, and so not standing at all? Throw yourself on him; do not be afraid, he will not pull back and let you fall. Throw yourself on him without fear; he will catch you and heal you." And I blushed deeply, because I could still hear the muttering of those trifles and was hanging in suspense. And again she seemed to say: "Stop your ears against those unclean parts of you on the earth, so that they may be put to death. They tell you about delights, but not as the law of the Lord your God does."

This argument in my heart was self against self, and nothing else. And Alypius, sitting close beside me, silently waited to see how my unusual state of feeling would come out.

But when deep reflection had drawn up all my misery from the secret bottom of my soul and heaped it in the sight of my heart, a huge storm rose, bringing a huge downpour of tears. And so that I might let the whole of it out with the noises it naturally makes, I got up and left Alypius; solitude struck me as better suited to the business of weeping. So I went far enough off that even his presence would not be a weight on me. That was the state I was in, and he noticed something of it; for I suppose I had said something in which the sound of my voice was choked with tears, and had got up on that. So he stayed where we had been sitting, utterly astonished. I threw myself down somehow under a fig tree and gave my tears free rein; and the floods poured out of my eyes, an acceptable sacrifice to You. And I said a great deal to You — not in these words, but to this effect: And You, Lord, how long? How long, Lord? Will You be angry for ever? Do not remember our old wrongdoings — for I felt I was still held by them. And I sent up these sorrowful words: How long, how long? Tomorrow and tomorrow? Why not now? Why is there not an end of my uncleanness this very hour?

I was saying this and weeping in the bitterest contrition of my heart, when suddenly I heard a voice from a neighbouring house, a boy's or a girl's, I do not know which, chanting and repeating over and over: "Take up and read; take up and read." At once my expression changed, and I began to think as hard as I could whether children ever sing such words in any kind of game; and I could not remember ever hearing anything like it. So I checked the flood of my tears and got up, taking it to be nothing but a command from God to open the book and read the first chapter I found. For I had heard about Antony that he came in during a reading of the gospel and took what was being read as spoken to him: Go, sell everything you have and give to the poor, and you shall have treasure in heaven; and come, follow me. And by that oracle he was converted to You at once. So I hurried back to where Alypius was sitting, since that was where I had put down the volume of the apostle when I got up. I snatched it, opened it, and read in silence the passage my eyes first fell on: Not in rioting and drunkenness, not in debauchery and licentiousness, not in strife and envy; but put on the Lord Jesus Christ, and make no provision for the flesh to gratify its desires. I did not want to read any further, and there was no need: for instantly, as the sentence ended, it was as though a light of certainty flooded into my heart and all the darkness of doubt vanished away.

Then, putting my finger or some other marker in the book, I closed it, and with a calm face told Alypius. And he told me what had been going on in him, which I did not know. He asked to see what I had read. I showed him, and he looked further on than I had read, and I did not know what came next. What came next was, Receive the man who is weak in the faith. He applied it to himself and told me so. And he was strengthened by that message; and by a good resolve entirely in keeping with his character, in which he had always been far better than I was, he joined me without any of the upheaval and delay. From there we went in to my mother and told her; she was overjoyed. We described in order how it had happened; she leapt for joy and triumphed, and blessed You, who are able to do more than we ask or think. For she could see that You had given her more for me than she had been in the habit of begging for with her pitiful and sorrowful groaning. For You converted me to Yourself so completely that I looked for neither a wife nor any hope of this world, standing on that rule of faith where You had shown me to her in a vision so many years before. And You turned her mourning into a joy far richer than she had wanted, and far more precious and pure than she had once asked for in wanting grandchildren of my body.

Book Nine: Baptism, Ostia, and the Death of Monica

Lord, I am Your servant; I am Your servant and the son of Your handmaid. You have broken my chains apart. I will offer You the sacrifice of praise. Let my heart and my tongue praise You, and let all my bones say, Lord, who is like You? Let them say it, and answer me, and say to my soul, I am your salvation. Who am I, and what am I? What evil has there not been, either in my actions, or, if not in my actions, in my words, or, if not in my words, in my will? But You, Lord, are good and merciful, and Your right hand looked into the depth of my death and emptied the pit of corruption out of the bottom of my heart. And this whole gift of Yours was to make me refuse what I willed and will what You willed. But through all those years, out of what low and deep recess was my free will called out in a moment, so that I could put my neck under Your easy yoke and my shoulders under Your light burden, Christ Jesus, my helper and my redeemer? How sweet it suddenly became to go without the sweetness of those toys! What I had dreaded losing was now a joy to let go. For You threw them out of me, true and highest sweetness. You threw them out, and came in Yourself in their place: sweeter than any pleasure, though not to flesh and blood; brighter than any light, and more hidden than any depth; higher than any honour, but not to those who are high in their own opinion. Now my soul was free of the biting cares of canvassing and acquiring, and of wallowing in filth, and of scratching the itch of lust. And I talked to You like an infant learning to speak — to my brightness, my riches, my health, the Lord my God.

And I decided, in Your sight, not to tear my tongue's service noisily away from the market in words, but to withdraw it quietly: so that young men who were not students of Your law or of Your peace, but of lying nonsense and legal skirmishing, should no longer buy weapons for their madness at my mouth. And very conveniently, there were only a few days left before the vintage holidays, and I decided to see them out and then resign in the ordinary way — and having been bought by You, never to go back on sale again. So our plan was known to You, and to men, apart from our own friends, it was not. For we had agreed among ourselves not to let it out to anybody; although, as we climbed up out of the valley of tears singing that song of ascents, You had given us sharp arrows and burning coals against the subtle tongue that appears to be advising us and would thwart us instead, and would eat us up out of love, as it eats its dinner.

You had pierced our hearts with Your love, and we carried Your words fixed in our insides, so to speak; and the examples of Your servants, whom You had turned from black to bright and from dead to living, all piled up together in the storehouse of our thoughts, kindled and burned up our heavy sluggishness so that we should not sink into the pit. They set us on fire so fiercely that all the blasts of subtle tongues from people opposing us could only inflame us further, not put us out. Still, since for the sake of Your name, which You have made holy across the earth, this vow and purpose of ours might well find people to praise it, it seemed like showing off not to wait for the holidays that were now so close, but to quit early from a public profession that everybody could see. Then everyone looking at what I had done, and noticing how near the vintage was that I had wanted to anticipate, would talk about me a great deal, as if I had wanted to look like somebody important. And what good would it have done me for people to speculate and argue about my intentions, and for what was good in us to be spoken of as evil?

There was another thing that had worried me at first. That very summer my lungs had begun to give way under too much literary work, so that I breathed deeply with difficulty and had a pain in my chest showing they were damaged, and I could not manage any full or extended speaking. This had worried me, because it nearly forced me to lay down the burden of teaching out of necessity, or at least to interrupt it if I could be cured and recover. But once the full desire for leisure — so that I might see that You are the Lord — had risen in me and settled there, then, my God, You know, I actually began to be glad that I had this secondary excuse, and a genuine one, that might soften the offence taken by people who, for their sons' sake, wished me never to have the freedom of Your sons. So, full of that gladness, I saw the interval out; it may have been some twenty days, and they were endured like a man. Endured, because the greed that used to carry part of this heavy business had left me, and I was on my own, and would have been overwhelmed if patience had not taken its place. Perhaps some of Your servants, my brothers, will say I sinned in this, in that with my heart wholly set on Your service I allowed myself to sit even one hour in the chair of lies. I shall not argue about it. But have You not, most merciful Lord, pardoned and remitted this sin too, along with my other most horrible and deadly sins, in the holy water?

Verecundus was worn down with worry about this blessedness of ours, because he saw that being held back by ties that bound him tightly, he would be separated from us. He was not yet a Christian himself, though his wife was one of the faithful; and this, more rigidly than any other chain, held him back from the journey we had now begun. He would not, he said, be a Christian on any other terms than the ones he could not manage. Even so, he courteously offered to let us stay at his country house as long as we were there. You will reward him, Lord, in the resurrection of the just, seeing that You have already given him the portion of the righteous. For although, while we were away and he was in Rome, he was seized by bodily illness, and being made a Christian and one of the faithful in the course of it, left this life — even so, You had mercy not only on him but on us, so that we should not be tortured by unbearable grief in remembering our friend's exceptional kindness to us and being unable to count him among Your flock. Thanks be to You, our God: we are Yours. Your promptings and consolations tell us so. Faithful to Your promises, You are now repaying Verecundus for his country house at Cassiciacum, where we rested in You from the fever of the world, with the eternal freshness of Your paradise; because You have forgiven him his sins upon earth, in that rich mountain, the mountain that yields milk, Your own mountain.

He had sorrow at that time; Nebridius had joy. For although he too, not being a Christian yet, had fallen into the pit of that most destructive error and believed the flesh of Your Son to be a phantom, he came out of it and believed as we did — not yet furnished with any of Your church's sacraments, but a most passionate searcher after truth. Not long after our conversion and rebirth by Your baptism, when he too was a faithful member of the Catholic Church and was serving You in perfect chastity and self-control among his own people in Africa, his whole household having been made Christian through him first, You released him from the flesh; and now he lives in Abraham's bosom. Whatever it is that this bosom means, there lives my Nebridius, my sweet friend, and Your son, Lord, adopted from being a freedman: there he lives. What other place could there be for such a soul? There he lives, in the place he used to ask me so many questions about, poor inexperienced man that I was. Now he does not put his ear to my mouth, but his spiritual mouth to Your fountain, and drinks as much wisdom as he can take in, in proportion to his thirst, endlessly happy. And I do not think he is so drunk on it that he forgets me, seeing that You, Lord, whom he drinks, are mindful of us. So that was our state then, comforting Verecundus, who was grieving, as far as friendship allowed, that our conversion had gone the way it did, and urging him to become faithful according to his own condition, that is, of a married man; and waiting for Nebridius to follow us, which he was on the very point of doing, so close was he. And so those days rolled by at last — they seemed long and many, because of the love I had for the untroubled freedom in which I would be able to sing to You from the marrow of my bones: My heart has said to You, I have sought Your face; Your face, Lord, will I seek.

Now the day came when I was actually to be free of my professorship of rhetoric, as in my mind I already was. And it was done. You rescued my tongue, as You had already rescued my heart. And I blessed You, rejoicing, and went off with all my household to the villa. What I did there in writing, which was by now enlisted in Your service — though still panting from the school of pride, as though in a breathing space — my books can testify: both what I debated with others and what I debated with myself alone in Your presence. And what I debated with Nebridius, who was away, my letters testify. And when shall I have time to recount all Your great benefits to us at that time, especially since I am hurrying on to still greater mercies? For my memory calls me back, and it is a pleasure to me, Lord, to confess to You by what inward goads You tamed me, and how You levelled me, lowering the mountains and hills of my high imaginings, straightening my crookedness and smoothing my rough roads. And how You also brought the brother of my heart, Alypius, under the name of Your only-begotten, our Lord and saviour Jesus Christ — a name he would not at first agree to have put in our writings, preferring that they should smell of the lofty cedars of the schools, which the Lord has now brought down, rather than of the wholesome herbs of the church, the antidote against serpents.

In what tones I spoke to You, my God, when I read the Psalms of David, those faithful songs and sounds of devotion which leave no room for a swollen spirit — still a catechumen, and a beginner in Your real love, resting at that villa with Alypius, another catechumen, and my mother staying close by us, in a woman's clothes with a man's faith, with the calm of age, a mother's love, a Christian's devotion. What tones I used to You in those Psalms, and how they set me alight towards You, and made me burn to recite them, if I could, through the whole world, against the pride of the human race! And yet they are sung through the whole world, and nobody can hide from Your heat.

With what violent and bitter sorrow I was angry at the Manichees — and then again pitied them, because they did not know those sacraments, those medicines, and were raging against the antidote that could have cured their raving. How I wished they had been somewhere near me then, without my knowing they were there, and could have watched my face and heard my words when I read the fourth Psalm in that time of rest, and could have seen how that Psalm worked on me: When I called, the God of my righteousness heard me; in trouble You gave me room. Have mercy on me, Lord, and hear my prayer. If only they could hear what I said over those words, without my knowing they heard, in case they thought I was saying it for their benefit! Because in truth I would not have said the same things, or said them the same way, if I had known they could see and hear me; and if I had said them, they would not have taken them the way they came out when I spoke by myself and for myself in front of You, out of the natural feelings of my soul.

I trembled with fear and then caught fire with hope and with delight in Your mercy, Father; and all of it came out through my eyes and my voice, when Your good Spirit turned to us and said, Sons of men, how long will you be slow of heart? Why do you love emptiness and go looking for lies? For I had loved emptiness and gone looking for lies. And You, Lord, had already magnified Your holy one, raising him from the dead and setting him at Your right hand, from where he was to send his promise from on high, the comforter, the Spirit of truth. And he had already sent him, and I did not know it. He had sent him, because he was now magnified, rising again from the dead and going up into heaven. Until then, the Spirit was not yet given, because Jesus was not yet glorified. And the prophet cries out, How long will you be slow of heart? Why do you love emptiness and go looking for lies? Know this: that the Lord has magnified his holy one. He cries out, How long? He cries out, Know this. And I, for all that time, not knowing, loved emptiness and looked for lies. So I heard and trembled, because it was spoken to people like the one I remembered being. In the phantoms I had taken for truths there was emptiness and lying; and I said a great deal aloud, earnestly and forcefully, in the bitterness of my memory. If only the people who still love emptiness and go looking for lies had heard it! Perhaps they would have been troubled and vomited it up, and You would have heard them when they cried out to You; for he died a real death in the flesh for us, who now pleads with You on our behalf.

I read on: Be angry, and do not sin. And how I was moved, my God, having now learned to be angry with myself over the past, so as not to sin in the future! And justly angry, since it was not some other nature belonging to a people of darkness that had sinned for me, as the people say who are not angry with themselves, and store up wrath against the day of wrath and of the revelation of Your just judgement. And my good things were now not outside me, and not looked for with the eyes of the flesh in the sun of this world; for those who want joy from outside soon grow empty and spend themselves on things seen and temporary, and in their starving thoughts they lick at the very shadows. If only they were worn out with their hunger and said, Who will show us good things? And we would say, and they would hear: The light of Your face is stamped on us. For we are not the light that lights every man; we are lit by You, so that having once been darkness we may be light in You. If only they could see the eternal light within, which, having tasted it, I was sorry I could not show them, so long as they kept bringing me their heart in their eyes, roving abroad away from You while they said, Who will show us good things? For there, where I was angry with myself in my own room, where I was inwardly stung, where I had made my sacrifice, killing my old self and beginning the plan of a new life, putting my trust in You — there You had begun to grow sweet to me, and had put gladness in my heart. And I cried out as I read it outwardly, finding it inwardly. And I did not want to be multiplied with worldly goods, wasting time and being wasted by time, when in Your eternal simple being I had other corn and wine and oil.

And with a loud cry of my heart I cried out at the next verse: peace! and the Selfsame! What was he saying — I will lie down and sleep? For who will stop us, when what is written comes to pass, Death is swallowed up in victory? And You are supremely the Selfsame, who do not change; and in You is a rest that forgets all labour, since there is nobody else with You, and we are not to go looking for all those other things that are not what You are. But You, Lord, alone have made me live in hope. I read, and caught fire; and I could not find what to do about those deaf and dead people, of whom I had been one myself — a pestilent creature, a bitter and blind bawler against those writings that are sweetened with the honey of heaven and lit with Your own light. And I was eaten up with zeal against the enemies of this Scripture.

When shall I recall everything that happened in those holidays? And yet I have not forgotten, and I will not pass over, the severity of Your whip and the wonderful speed of Your mercy. You tormented me then with toothache; and when it had reached the point where I could not speak, it came into my heart to ask all the friends who were there to pray to You for me, the God of every kind of health. And I wrote this on wax and gave it to them to read. And the moment we had knelt down in humble devotion, the pain went away. But what pain it had been, and how did it go? I was frightened, my Lord and my God, for I had never experienced anything like it since infancy. And the power of Your nod sank deep into me; and rejoicing in faith I praised Your name. And that faith would not let me be easy about my past sins, which were not yet forgiven me by Your baptism.

When the vintage holiday was over I gave notice to the people of Milan to find their students another master to sell them words, since I had both chosen to serve You and was no longer up to the professorship, with my difficulty in breathing and the pain in my chest. And by letter I told Your bishop, the holy man Ambrose, about my former errors and my present desires, asking his advice on which of Your Scriptures I had best read to become readier and fitter to receive so great a grace. He recommended the prophet Isaiah — I think because Isaiah more than the rest foreshadows the gospel and the calling of the Gentiles. But I did not understand the first passage of it, and imagining the whole book was like that, I put it aside to take up again when I had more practice in our Lord's own words.

Then, when the time came for me to give in my name, we left the country and went back to Milan. Alypius wanted to be born again in You along with me, being already clothed in the humility that suits Your sacraments; and he was a most valiant tamer of the body, to the point of walking barefoot over the frozen ground of Italy, which was an unusual venture. With us we took the boy Adeodatus, born in the flesh of my sin. You had made him excellently. He was not quite fifteen, and in intelligence he was ahead of many serious and learned men. I confess Your gifts to You, Lord my God, creator of everything and abundantly able to reshape our deformities; for I had no part in that boy except the sin. That we brought him up in Your discipline was inspired by You and by nobody else. I confess Your gifts to You. There is a book of ours called The Teacher; it is a dialogue between him and me. You know that everything given there to the person talking with me was his own thinking, in his sixteenth year. I found much else in him, more admirable still. That talent frightened me. And who but You could be the maker of such wonders? You soon took his life from the earth; and I now remember him without anxiety, with nothing to fear for his childhood or his youth or for the whole of him. Him we took with us, our contemporary in grace, to be brought up in Your discipline; and we were baptised, and anxiety about our past life left us. Nor could I get enough, in those days, of the marvellous sweetness of considering the depth of Your plans for the salvation of mankind. How I wept at Your hymns and canticles, cut to the quick by the voices of Your sweet-singing church! The voices flowed into my ears and the truth was distilled into my heart, and the feelings of my devotion overflowed, and the tears ran down, and I was happy in them.

It was not long since the church at Milan had begun to use this kind of consolation and encouragement, with the brothers joining in eagerly with harmony of voice and heart. For it had been a year, or not much more, since Justina, mother of the boy emperor Valentinian, had persecuted Your servant Ambrose in favour of her heresy, which the Arians had seduced her into. The devout people kept watch in the church, ready to die with their bishop, Your servant. There my mother, Your handmaid, taking a leading part in those anxieties and vigils, lived for prayer. We ourselves, not yet warmed by the heat of Your Spirit, were still stirred by the sight of the astonished and disturbed city. It was then that it was first laid down that hymns and psalms should be sung after the manner of the Eastern churches, so that the people should not grow faint through the tedium of their grief; and from that day to this the custom has been kept, with many, indeed almost all, of Your congregations in other parts of the world following it.

Then in a vision You revealed to that bishop of Yours where the bodies of the martyrs Gervasius and Protasius lay hidden, whom You had kept uncorrupted in Your secret store for so many years, so that You might bring them out at the right moment to check the fury of a woman, though she was an empress. For when they were found and dug up and carried with the proper honour to the Ambrosian basilica, not only were people troubled by unclean spirits cured, with the devils confessing themselves, but a certain man who had been blind for many years, a citizen well known in the town, asked what all the confused rejoicing was about; and hearing the answer, he leapt up and asked his guide to take him there. Once there, he begged to be allowed to touch the bier of Your saints with his handkerchief — Your saints, whose death is precious in Your sight. When he had done it and put the handkerchief to his eyes, they were opened at once. From there the story spread; from there Your praises glowed and shone; and from there the mind of that enemy of Yours, though not turned to a sound faith, was at least turned back from her fury of persecution. Thanks be to You, my God. Where have You brought my memory from and to, that I should confess these things to You as well, which, great as they are, I had passed over and forgotten? And even then, when the scent of Your ointments was so strong, we did not run after You. That is why I wept the more at the singing of Your hymns — having sighed after You for so long, and at last breathing You in, as far as breath can enter this house of grass we live in.
