You, who make men live of one mind in one house, joined Euodius to us as well, a young man from our own town. Being an officer at court, he had been converted to You and baptised before us, and had given up his secular service and buckled himself to Yours. We were together, and about to live together in our devout purpose. We looked for where we might serve You most usefully, and were on our way back to Africa together — and having got as far as Ostia, my mother left this life. I am leaving out a great deal, because I am hurrying on. Accept my confessions and thanksgivings, my God, for the countless things I say nothing about. But I shall not leave out whatever my soul can bring forth about that handmaid of Yours who brought me forth: in the flesh, so that I might be born into this temporary light, and in her heart, so that I might be born into the eternal one. It is not her gifts I want to speak of but Yours in her; for she neither made nor educated herself. You created her, and her father and mother had no idea what would come of them. And it was the sceptre of Your Christ, the discipline of Your only Son, in a Christian household, a good member of Your church, that brought her up in reverence for You.

And yet, for her good upbringing, she used to give credit not so much to her mother's diligence as to that of a certain very old servant, who had carried her father as a child the way small children get carried on the backs of older girls. For that reason, and for her great age and excellent way of life, she was well respected in that Christian household by the heads of it. So the charge of her master's daughters was entrusted to her, and she took it seriously, restraining them firmly when necessary with a holy severity and teaching them with a grave good sense. Except at the hours when they were fed very temperately at their parents' table, she would not let them drink even water, however parched they were — heading off a bad habit, and adding this wholesome advice: "You drink water now because you have no power to get wine. But when you are married and made mistresses of cellars and storerooms, you will despise water, and the habit of drinking will still be there." By this method of teaching, and by the authority she had, she held back the greediness of childhood and shaped the girls' very thirst to such an excellent moderation that they no longer wanted what they should not have.

And yet, as Your handmaid told me, her son, a love of wine had crept up on her. For when, as the custom was, she was told by her parents to draw wine from the cask, being a sober girl, she would hold the jug under the tap and, before pouring the wine into the flagon, sip a little with the tip of her lips; more than that her instincts refused. She did this not out of any craving for drink but out of the exuberance of youth, which boils over into playful mischief and is usually kept down in the young by the seriousness of their elders. And so, adding to that little a daily little more — for whoever despises small things will fall little by little — she had fallen into the habit of greedily draining her small cup nearly full of wine. Where was that discreet old woman then, and her firm prohibitions? Would anything have helped against a secret disease, if Your healing hand, Lord, had not been watching over us? With her father and mother and guardians away and You present, You who create, who call, and who work something towards the salvation of our souls even through the people set over us — what did You do then, my God? How did You cure her? How did You heal her? Did You not bring out of another soul a hard, sharp taunt, like a surgeon's knife out of Your secret store, and remove all that foulness with one cut?

For a maidservant who used to go down to the cellar with her fell into an argument, as happens, with her young mistress when they were alone, and taunted her with this fault in the most bitter and insulting terms, calling her a wine-swiller. Stung to the quick by that taunt, she saw how foul her fault was, and immediately condemned it and gave it up. As flattering friends corrupt us, so reproachful enemies mostly correct us. And yet You repay them not for what You do through them but for what they intended themselves. For that girl in her anger meant to hurt her young mistress, not to reform her; and she did it in private, either because that was where and when the quarrel happened to find them, or in case she should get into trouble herself for bringing it up so late. But You, Lord, governor of everything in heaven and earth, who turn the deepest currents to Your own purposes and direct the turbulent tide of the ages, healed one soul by the very unhealthiness of another — so that nobody who notices this should credit it to his own power, even when somebody he wanted to see reformed is reformed by words of his.

Brought up like this, modestly and soberly, and made subject to her parents by You rather than to You by her parents, as soon as she was of marriageable age she was given to a husband, and served him as her lord. And she did her best to win him over to You, preaching You to him by the way she lived; by which You adorned her, and made her reverently lovable and admirable to her husband. And she put up with his unfaithfulness in such a way as never to have a single quarrel with him about it, because she looked for Your mercy on him — that believing in You he might be made chaste. Besides this, he was as hot in his temper as he was in his affections; but she had learned not to resist an angry husband, not only in action but not even in words. Only when he had smoothed down and was calm and in a state to take it would she explain what she had done, if he had happened to take offence too hastily. In fact, while many women with gentler husbands still carried the marks of beatings on their faces, and would criticise their husbands' behaviour in casual conversation, she would criticise their tongues, giving them serious advice in the form of a joke: that from the moment they heard the marriage contract read to them they should count it as an indenture making them servants, and so, remembering their position, should not set themselves up against their masters. And when they, knowing what a bad-tempered husband she put up with, marvelled that it had never been heard or noticed by any sign that Patricius had beaten his wife, or that there had been so much as a day's domestic quarrel between them, and asked her the reason in confidence, she taught them the practice I have described. Those wives who followed it found the good of it and thanked her; those who did not found no relief, and suffered.

Her mother-in-law, too, was at first set against her by the whispering of malicious servants; and she so overcame her by attentiveness and persistent endurance and mildness that the older woman of her own accord told her son about the meddling tongues that had disturbed the domestic peace between herself and her daughter-in-law, and asked him to correct them. And when, in obedience to his mother and for the good order of the household, he had corrected the offenders with a beating, at the wish of the woman who had reported them, she promised the same reward to anyone else who spoke ill of her daughter-in-law to please her; and after that nobody dared, and they lived together with a remarkable sweetness of mutual kindness.

You also gave this great gift, my God, my mercy, to that good handmaid of Yours in whose womb You created me: that between any people who were quarrelling, wherever she could, she showed herself such a peacemaker that although she heard the bitterest things from both sides — the kind of thing that swollen and undigested anger breaks out into, when the raw material of hatred is breathed out in sour talk to a friend who is there about an enemy who is not — she would never disclose anything from one to the other except what might tend towards their reconciliation. This might seem a small good to me, if I did not know to my sorrow countless people who, through some horrible and widely spreading infection of sin, not only report to angry people what was said about them in anger, but add things that were never said at all. Whereas ordinary humanity ought to think it a small thing not to feed or increase ill will by bad words, unless one also works to put it out with good ones. That is what she was like, with You, her most inward teacher, teaching her in the school of the heart.

In the end she won her own husband over to You, at the very end of his earthly life; and she had no cause to complain of anything in him as a believer that she had put up with from him before he was one. She was also the servant of Your servants. Whichever of them knew her found much in her to praise and honour and love You for, because through the testimony of the fruits of a holy life they could feel Your presence in her heart. For she had been the wife of one man, had repaid her parents, had run her household devoutly, was well spoken of for her good works, had brought up children, going into labour over them again each time she saw them straying from You. And last of all, Lord, of all of us Your servants — whom by Your own gift You allow to speak — us who before she fell asleep in You lived united together, having received the grace of Your baptism, she took care of as though she had been mother of us all, and served as though she had been the child of us all.

The day was now coming on when she was to leave this life — a day You knew well and we did not — and it happened, arranged, as I believe, by Your own secret ways, that she and I were standing alone, leaning in a window that looked into the garden of the house where we were staying at Ostia. There, far from the noise of people, we were recovering from the exhaustion of a long journey and preparing for the voyage. So we were talking together, alone, very sweetly; and forgetting the things behind and reaching forward to the things ahead, we were asking each other, in the presence of the truth, which is You, what the eternal life of the saints would be like, which no eye has seen and no ear has heard and which has not entered into any human heart. But we gasped with the mouth of our heart after those heavenly streams of Your fountain, the fountain of life which is with You, so that being sprinkled from it according to our capacity, we might somehow think about so high a mystery.

And when our conversation had reached the point that the highest delight of the bodily senses, in the very purest material light, was, compared with the sweetness of that life, not only not worth comparing but not worth mentioning, we lifted ourselves up with a still more glowing love towards the Selfsame, and passed step by step through all bodily things, even the sky itself, from which the sun and moon and stars shine on the earth. And we soared higher still, by inward thought and talk and wonder at Your works; and we came to our own minds, and went beyond them, to arrive at that region of never-failing plenty where You feed Israel for ever on the food of truth, and where life is the Wisdom by whom all these things are made, and everything that has been and shall be. And she is not made, but is, as she has been, and so shall be for ever — or rather, "has been" and "shall be" are not in her, only "is", since she is eternal; for "has been" and "shall be" are not eternal. And while we were talking and panting after her, with one whole effort of our heart we just touched her; and we sighed, and left there, bound to her, the first fruits of the Spirit, and came back down to the noises our mouths make, where a spoken word has a beginning and an end. And what is like Your Word, our Lord, who remains in himself without growing old, and makes all things new?

We were saying this: suppose that for someone the tumult of the flesh fell silent; silent the images of earth and water and air; silent the poles of the sky; silent the soul to herself, going beyond herself by not thinking about herself; silent all dreams and imagined revelations, every tongue and every sign and everything that exists only by passing away — since all of them say, if anyone can hear, We did not make ourselves; he made us who remains for ever. Suppose that having said this they too fell silent, having only turned our ears towards the one who made them; and he alone spoke, not through them but by himself, so that we might hear his word, not through any tongue of flesh, nor an angel's voice, nor a clap of thunder, nor in the dark riddle of a likeness, but might hear the one we love in these things, might hear his very self without them — as the two of us were now straining, and in a flash of thought touched that eternal wisdom which remains above everything. Suppose this could be prolonged, and all other visions of a wholly different kind withdrawn, and this one alone should seize and absorb and wrap up the man who saw it in those inward joys, so that life could be for ever like that one moment of understanding we had just been sighing after. Would this not be, Enter into your master's joy? And when will that be? When we shall all rise again, though we shall not all be changed.

That is what I was saying — and even if not in exactly this way and in these same words, still, Lord, You know that on the day when we were talking about these things, and this world with all its delights became worthless to us as we spoke, my mother said: "Son, for my part I take no further pleasure in anything in this life. What I am still doing here, and why I am here, I do not know, now that my hopes in this world have been fulfilled. There was one thing I wanted to stay alive a while for, and that was to see you a Catholic Christian before I died. My God has done this for me more than abundantly, since I now see you despising earthly happiness as well, and made his servant. What am I doing here?"

What answer I gave her to this I do not remember. For scarcely five days later, or not much more, she went down with a fever; and one day in that illness she fainted and was withdrawn for a while from the things around her. We ran to her; but she was soon brought back to her senses, and looking at me and my brother standing by her, she asked us, "Where was I?" And then, looking hard at us, dazed with grief: "Here," she said, "you will bury your mother." I kept quiet and held back my tears. But my brother said something, wishing for her, as the happier lot, that she might die not in a foreign place but in her own country. At that she looked anxious and checked him with her eyes, for still having a taste for such things; and then she looked at me and said, "See what he says." And soon after, to both of us: "Lay this body anywhere; do not let any concern about that trouble you. This one thing I ask: that you remember me at the altar of the Lord, wherever you may be." And having got this thought out in whatever words she could, she fell silent, being wearied by her worsening illness.

But I, considering Your gifts, unseen God, which You pour into the hearts of Your faithful, out of which wonderful fruits grow, rejoiced and gave thanks to You, remembering what I had known before: how careful and anxious she had always been about her burial place, which she had provided and prepared for herself beside her husband's body. Because they had lived in great harmony together, she had also wanted — so little can the human mind take in things divine — to have this addition to that happiness, and to have it remembered among men, that after her pilgrimage across the seas the earthly part of this united pair had been allowed to be united under the same earth. But when this empty wish had begun to fade from her heart through the fullness of Your goodness I did not know; and I was delighted and full of wonder that she had said what she said — though indeed in that conversation of ours at the window, when she said, "What am I still doing here?", there had been no sign of any wish to die in her own country. I heard afterwards, too, that while we were at Ostia she talked one day, with a mother's confidence, when I was not there, with some of my friends about contempt for this life and the blessing of death. And when they were amazed at the courage You had given a woman, and asked whether she was not afraid to leave her body so far from her own city, she answered, "Nothing is far from God; and there is no danger that at the end of the world he will not know where to raise me up from." So on the ninth day of her illness, in the fifty-sixth year of her life and the thirty-third of mine, that religious and holy soul was freed from the body.

I closed her eyes; and an enormous grief flowed into my heart and was overflowing into tears, while at the same time my eyes, under the violent command of my mind, drank their own spring completely dry. And it went badly with me in that struggle. But when she breathed her last, the boy Adeodatus burst out into a loud lament; and being checked by all of us, he fell silent. In the same way a childish feeling in me, which was finding its outlet in weeping through my heart's youthful voice, was checked and silenced. For we did not think it right to mark that funeral with tearful lamentation and groaning, since that is mostly how people express grief for the dead as though they were unhappy or altogether finished — whereas she was neither unhappy in her death nor altogether finished. Of that we were assured on good grounds: the evidence of her good life and her unpretended faith.

What was it, then, that hurt me so badly inside, if not a fresh wound made by the sudden tearing away of that sweetest and dearest habit of living together? I did take joy in her testimony, when, in that last illness of hers, mixing her endearments with what I did for her, she called me dutiful, and mentioned with great affection that she had never heard a harsh or reproachful sound come out of my mouth against her. And yet, my God, who made us, what comparison is there between the honour I paid her and her slavery for me? So, deprived of so great a comfort in her, my soul was wounded, and that life was torn apart, which had been made one out of hers and mine together.

Once the boy had been quieted from his weeping, Euodius took up the psalter and began to sing, and our whole household answered him, the psalm: I will sing of mercy and judgement to You, Lord. And hearing what we were doing, many brothers and religious women gathered; and while those whose office it was made the arrangements for the burial in the usual way, I, in another part of the house where it was fitting, talked about something suited to the occasion with those who thought it right not to leave me. And with this balm of truth I eased the torment that You knew about and they did not, as they listened intently and supposed I had no feeling of grief at all. But in Your hearing, where none of them could hear, I blamed the weakness of my feelings and held back the flood of my grief; and it gave way to me a little, and then came back like a tide — though not so as to burst out in tears or change my expression. And I knew what I was holding down in my heart. And being very displeased that these human things had such power over me, things which must necessarily happen in the due order and appointment of our natural condition, I grieved over my grief with a fresh grief, and so was worn down by a double sorrow.

And so the body was carried to the burial; we went and came back without tears. Not even in the prayers we poured out to You, when the sacrifice of our ransom was offered for her, with the body already by the graveside before being laid in it, as the custom is there — not even during those prayers did I weep. Yet all that day I was heavily sad in secret, and with a troubled mind I asked You as best I could to heal my grief. And You did not, impressing on my memory, I believe, by this one instance, how strong the bond of every habit is, even on a soul that no longer feeds on a deceiving word. It also seemed a good idea to go and take a bath, having heard that the bath is called balneum from the Greek balaneion because it drives sadness out of the mind. And this too I confess to Your mercy, Father of the fatherless: that I bathed, and was the same afterwards as before. The bitterness of my grief could not be sweated out of my heart. Then I slept, and woke again, and found my grief had softened considerably. And as I was alone in my bed, I remembered those true verses of Your Ambrose. For You are the

	Maker of all things, Lord
	  and ruler of the height,
	who, dressing the day in light, have poured
	  soft sleep across the night,
	so that our tired limbs
	  may find their strength renewed,
	and hearts that sink and falter rise,
	  and sorrows be subdued.

And then little by little I got back my old way of thinking about Your handmaid: her holy way of life towards You, her holy tenderness and attentiveness towards us, which I had suddenly been deprived of. And I wanted to weep in Your sight, for her and for myself, on her behalf and on my own. And I let the tears I had been holding back overflow as much as they wanted, spreading my heart out on them; and it found rest in them, because it was in Your hearing and not in the hearing of some man who would have put a scornful interpretation on my weeping. And now, Lord, I confess it to You in writing. Let anyone read it who will and interpret it how he will; and if he finds it a sin that I wept for my mother for a small part of an hour — the mother who was dead for the time being to my eyes, who for so many years had wept for me so that I might live in Yours — let him not laugh at me, but rather, if he is a man of large charity, let him weep himself for my sins to You, the Father of all the brothers of Your Christ.

But now, with my heart cured of that wound, in which it might have seemed blameworthy for an earthly feeling, I pour out to You, our God, on behalf of that handmaid of Yours, a quite different kind of tears, flowing from a spirit shaken by thoughts of the dangers facing every soul that dies in Adam. And although she had been made alive in Christ even before her release from the flesh, and had lived to the praise of Your name in her faith and her way of life, still I do not dare to say that from the time You gave her new birth in baptism no word ever came out of her mouth against Your commandment. Your Son, the truth, has said: whoever says to his brother, You fool, shall be in danger of hell fire. And it will go badly even with the most praiseworthy human life, if You set mercy aside and examine it. But because You are not severe in enquiring after sins, we confidently hope to find some place with You. And whoever counts up his real merits to You, what is he counting up but Your own gifts? If only men would know themselves to be men, and whoever boasts would boast in the Lord.

So I, my praise and my life, God of my heart, setting aside for a moment her good deeds, for which I thank You with joy, now beg You for my mother's sins. Hear me, I entreat You, by the medicine of our wounds, who hung on the tree and now sits at Your right hand and pleads with You for us. I know that she acted mercifully, and forgave her debtors their debts from her heart. Forgive her debts too, whatever she may have run up in all those years since the water of salvation. Forgive her, Lord, forgive, I beg You; do not enter into judgement with her. Let Your mercy be lifted above Your justice, since Your words are true and You have promised mercy to the merciful — and You made them merciful in the first place, who will have mercy on whom You will have mercy, and will have compassion on whom You have had compassion.

And I believe You have already done what I ask; but accept, Lord, the freewill offerings of my mouth. For when the day of her dissolution was close, she gave no thought to having her body sumptuously wrapped or embalmed with spices, and did not want a choice monument or to be buried in her own country. These things she did not lay on us. She only wanted her name remembered at Your altar, which she had served without missing a single day, and from which she knew the holy sacrifice was distributed by which the record of debt against us is wiped out; through which the enemy was defeated, who, adding up our offences and looking for something to charge us with, found nothing in the one in whom we conquer. Who shall give him back the innocent blood? Who shall repay him the price he bought us with, and so take us away from him? To the sacrament of that ransom Your handmaid bound her soul by the bond of faith. Let nobody separate her from Your protection; let neither the lion nor the dragon get between by force or by trickery. For she will not answer that she owes nothing, in case she is convicted and seized by the crafty accuser; she will answer that her sins have been forgiven her by the one to whom nobody can repay the price that he, who owed nothing, paid for us.

So may she rest in peace with the husband before and after whom she never had another; whom she obeyed, patiently bringing forth fruit for You, so that she might win him too for You. And inspire, Lord my God, inspire Your servants my brothers, Your sons my masters, whom I serve with my voice and heart and pen: inspire all of them who read these confessions to remember at Your altar Monica Your handmaid, along with Patricius, once her husband, through whose bodies You brought me into this life, though how I do not know. Let them with devout affection remember my parents in this passing light; and my brothers under You our Father in our Catholic mother; and my fellow citizens in that eternal Jerusalem which Your pilgrim people sigh after from their setting out until their return. So that my mother's last request of me may be fulfilled for her more abundantly through the prayers of many, brought by these confessions of mine, than it could be through my prayers alone.

Book Ten: Memory

Let me know You, Lord, who know me; let me know You as I am known. Power of my soul, come into it and make it fit for Yourself, so that You may have and hold it without spot or wrinkle. This is my hope, and that is why I speak; and in this hope I rejoice, whenever my rejoicing is healthy. Other things in this life deserve less grief the more they are grieved over, and deserve more grief the less people grieve over them. For You love the truth, and the man who does the truth comes to the light. This is what I want to do in my heart in front of You, in confession — and in my writing, in front of many witnesses.

And from You, Lord, to whose eyes the depths of a man's conscience are naked, what could be hidden in me even if I refused to confess it? I would be hiding You from me, not me from You. But now, since my groaning is evidence that I am displeased with myself, You shine out and are pleasing and loved and longed for; so that I may be ashamed of myself and give myself up and choose You, and please neither You nor myself except in You. To You, then, Lord, I am open, whatever I am; and with what benefit I confess to You I have already said. And I do not do it with words and sounds of the flesh, but with the words of my soul and the cry of the thought that Your ear knows. For when I am bad, confessing to You is nothing but being displeased with myself; and when I am good, it is nothing but not taking the credit for it — because You, Lord, bless the godly man, but first You make him righteous while he is ungodly. So my confession, my God, in Your sight, is made silently and not silently: silently as far as sound goes, and crying out as far as feeling goes. For I say nothing right to men that You have not heard from me first; and You hear nothing of the kind from me that You have not first said to me.

So what have I to do with men, that they should hear my confessions — as if they could heal all my weaknesses? A race curious to know other people's lives and lazy about mending their own. Why do they want to hear from me what I am, when they will not hear from You what they are themselves? And how do they know, when they hear about me from me, whether I am telling the truth, seeing that no man knows what is in a man except the spirit of the man which is in him? But if they hear about themselves from You, they cannot say, "The Lord is lying." For what is hearing about themselves from You but knowing themselves? And who knows himself and says, "That is false," unless he is lying himself? But because love believes all things — at least among those it knits to itself and makes one — I too, Lord, will confess to You in such a way that men may hear, to whom I cannot prove that I am confessing truly. And yet they believe me, those whose ears love opens to me.

But You, my inmost physician, make plain to me what good it does. The confessions of my past sins, which You have forgiven and covered, so that You might bless me in Yourself, changing my soul by faith and by Your sacrament — those confessions, when they are read and heard, stir up the heart, so that it does not sleep in despair and say, "I cannot," but wakes up in the love of Your mercy and the sweetness of Your grace, by which anyone who is weak is strong, once through it he has become aware of his own weakness. And good people are glad to hear of the past evils of those who are now free of them: not because they are evils, but because they have been and are not. But with what benefit, Lord my God, to whom my conscience confesses daily, trusting more in the hope of Your mercy than in her own innocence — with what benefit, I ask, do I in this book confess to men as well, in Your presence, what I now am and not what I have been? That other benefit I have seen and spoken of. But what I now am, at the very moment of making these confessions, a good many people want to know, who have known me and who have not, who have heard from me or about me; and their ear is not at my heart, where I am, whatever I am. So they want to hear me confess what I am inside, where neither their eye nor their ear nor their understanding can reach; they want it, and are ready to believe — but will they know? For love, which makes them good, tells them that I do not lie in my confessions; and it is love in them that believes me.

But what benefit would they get from hearing it? Do they want to be glad along with me, when they hear how near I come to You by Your gift, and to pray for me when they hear how far I am held back by my own weight? To such people I shall reveal myself. For it is no small benefit, Lord my God, that thanks should be given to You by many on our behalf, and that You should be asked by many for us. Let a brotherly mind love in me what You teach us is to be loved, and lament in me what You teach us is to be lamented. Let it be a brotherly mind and not a stranger's — not the mind of those foreign children whose mouth talks emptiness and whose right hand is a right hand of wickedness, but the brotherly mind that rejoices for me when it approves of me and is sorry for me when it disapproves, because whether it approves or disapproves it loves me. To such people I shall reveal myself: they will breathe more easily at my good deeds and sigh over my bad ones. My good deeds are Your arrangements and Your gifts; my bad ones are my offences and Your judgements. Let them breathe more easily at the one and sigh at the other; and let hymns and weeping go up into Your sight out of the hearts of my brothers, who are Your censers. And You, Lord, take pleasure in the incense of Your holy temple; have mercy on me according to Your great mercy, for Your own name's sake; and in no way abandoning what You have begun, complete what is unfinished in me.

This is the benefit of confessing what I am, not what I have been: to confess it not only in front of You, in a secret exultation with trembling and a secret sorrow with hope, but also in the ears of the believing sons of men, who share my joy and are partners in my mortality, my fellow citizens and fellow pilgrims, some ahead of me and some following, companions on my road. These are Your servants and my brothers, whom You want to be Your sons; my masters, whom You have ordered me to serve if I want to live with You and from You. But this Word of Yours would be a small thing if it only gave orders by speaking, and did not go ahead by doing. So I do this in deed and in word; I do this under Your wings — and in far too much danger, if my soul were not made subject to You under Your wings, and my weakness known to You. I am a small child, but my Father lives for ever, and my guardian is enough for me. For he is the one who both fathered me and defends me; and You Yourself are all my good — You, almighty, who are with me before ever I am with You. So to those You have ordered me to serve, I shall reveal not what I have been but what I now am and what I still am. And yet I do not pass judgement on myself either. This, then, is how I would like to be heard.

For it is You, Lord, who judge me; because although nobody knows what is in a man but the spirit of the man which is in him, there is nevertheless something in a man that not even the spirit in him knows. But You, Lord, know all of him, since You made him. And I, though I despise myself in Your sight and reckon myself dust and ashes, still know something of You that I do not know of myself. And truly, now we see through a glass darkly, and not yet face to face. So as long as I am away from You I am more present to myself than to You; and yet I know of You that You cannot suffer, while about myself I do not know which temptations I can resist and which I cannot. And there is hope, because You are faithful, and will not let us be tempted beyond what we are able to bear, but will with the temptation also make a way of escape so that we can bear it. So I will confess what I know about myself, and I will confess what I do not know about myself. And that is because what I do know about myself, I know by Your shining on me; and what I do not know about myself, I go on not knowing until my darkness is made like noon in Your face.

Not with any doubt but with a settled certainty do I love You, Lord. You struck my heart with Your word, and I loved You. And the heavens too, and the earth and everything in them, tell me on every side to love You, and never stop saying so to everybody, so that they are without excuse. But more deeply You will have mercy on whom You will have mercy, and have compassion on whom You have had compassion; otherwise the heavens and the earth are speaking Your praises into deaf ears.

But what do I love, when I love You? Not the beauty of bodies, nor the loveliness of the passing seasons; not the brightness of light, so welcome to our eyes; not the sweet melodies of songs of every kind; not the fragrant smell of flowers and ointments and spices; not manna and honey; not limbs that are pleasant to embrace. None of these is what I love when I love my God. And yet I do love a kind of light, and a melody, and a fragrance, and a food, and an embrace, when I love my God: the light and melody and fragrance and food and embrace of my inner man — where there shines for my soul what no space can contain, and there sounds what time cannot carry away, and there smells what no breeze scatters, and there tastes what no eating diminishes, and there clings what no satisfaction parts us from. That is what I love when I love my God.

And what is this? I asked the earth, and it answered, "I am not he"; and everything in it said the same. I asked the sea and the deeps and the living creatures that creep in them, and they answered, "We are not your God; look above us." I asked the moving air, and the whole air with its inhabitants answered, "Anaximenes was mistaken; I am not God." I asked the sky, the sun, the moon, the stars: "We are not the God you are looking for either," they said. And I said to all the things that stand around the doorway of my flesh, "You have told me about my God, that you are not he; tell me something about him." And they cried out in a loud voice, "He made us." My questioning of them was my attention to them, and their form and beauty were the answer. And I turned to myself and said to myself, "Who are you?" And I answered, "A man." And there present themselves to me in me a soul and a body, the one outside and the other within. Which of these should I look for my God by? I had looked for him in the body, from earth up to heaven, as far as I could send my messengers, the beams of my eyes. But the inner is the better, since it was to the inner as presiding judge that all the bodily messengers reported the answers of heaven and earth and everything in them, when they said, "We are not God; he made us." These things my inner man knew through the service of the outer: I the inner knew them, I, the mind, through the senses of my body. I asked the whole structure of the world about my God, and it answered, "I am not he; he made me."

Is this bodily shape not visible to everyone whose senses work? Then why does it not say the same thing to everybody? Animals large and small see it and cannot ask it, because they have no reason set over their senses to judge what the senses report. But men can ask, so that the invisible things of God are clearly seen and understood by the things that are made. Yet by loving those things men are made subject to them, and subjects cannot judge. Nor do the creatures answer people who ask, unless the people can judge. And the creatures do not change their voice — that is, their appearance — so as to look one way to one man and another way to another, if one merely sees and another sees and asks. Appearing the same way to both, it is dumb to one and speaks to the other. Or rather it speaks to both, and only those understand who compare the voice they receive from outside with the truth inside. For truth says to me, "Neither heaven nor earth nor any other body is your God." Their very nature says this to anyone who sees them: "We are a mass, and a mass is less in one part of itself than in the whole." Now I am speaking to you, my soul; you are my better part, for you give life to the mass of my body, which no body can give to a body. But your God is even to you the life of your life.

What then do I love, when I love my God? Who is he, above the summit of my soul? By my very soul I shall climb up to him. I shall go beyond that power by which I am joined to my body and fill its whole frame with life; for by that power I cannot find my God, since if I could, a horse or a mule with no understanding could find him, seeing it is the same power by which their bodies live too. There is another power, not only the one by which I animate my flesh but the one by which I give it sensation, which the Lord has built for me: ordering the eye not to hear and the ear not to see, but the eye to be what I see through and the ear what I hear through, and giving each of the other senses its own particular seat and function; which are many, and which I, being one mind, work through. I shall go beyond this power of mine too, for horses and mules have this as well, since they perceive through the body too.

So I shall go beyond this power of my nature also, climbing by degrees to the one who made me. And I come to the fields and vast palaces of memory, where the treasures of countless images are stored, brought in from things of all kinds perceived by the senses. There is stored up as well everything else we think, by enlarging or reducing or in any other way varying the things the senses have reached; and whatever else has been entrusted to it and laid up there, which forgetfulness has not yet swallowed and buried. When I go in there, I ask for whatever I want to be brought out, and some things come instantly, while others have to be looked for longer and are fetched, as it were, out of some more inward container. Others come rushing out in crowds, and while one thing is wanted and asked for they leap forward as if to say, "Perhaps it is me?" These I drive away with the hand of my heart from the face of my remembering, until what I want is unveiled and comes into view out of its hiding place. Other things come up readily in unbroken order as they are called for, the ones in front making way for the ones behind; and as they make way they are hidden from view, ready to come again whenever I want. All this happens when I recite something by heart.

There everything is kept, sorted and under general headings, each having come in by its own entrance: light and all the colours and shapes of bodies by the eyes; all kinds of sound by the ears; all smells by the entrance of the nostrils; all tastes by the mouth; and by the sensation of the whole body, what is hard or soft, hot or cold, rough or smooth, heavy or light, whether outside the body or inside it. All these that great harbour of the memory receives in its countless secret and indescribable folds, to be available and brought out when needed, each having come in by its own gate and been stored there. Nor do the things themselves come in; only the images of the things perceived are there ready for thought to recall. How those images are formed, who can say — though it is quite plain which sense brought each of them in and stored it. For even while I am sitting in darkness and silence, I can produce colours in my memory if I want, and tell black from white and any other colours I please; and sounds do not break in and disturb the image drawn in by my eyes that I am reviewing, though they are there too, lying dormant and stored away separately. For I call for those as well and they appear at once. And though my tongue is still and my throat silent, I can sing as much as I like; and those images of colours, which are there all the same, do not push in and interrupt when another store is called for, which flowed in through my ears. And so with the other things heaped in by the other senses: I recall them at will. I can tell the smell of lilies from violets while smelling nothing; and I prefer honey to sweet wine, and smooth to rough, while tasting and touching nothing at the time, only remembering.

All this I do inside, in that huge court of my memory. For there heaven and earth and sea are present to me, and everything I could think about in them, apart from what I have forgotten. There too I meet myself, and recall myself, and when and where I did what, and how I felt about it. There is everything I remember, either from my own experience or on somebody else's word. Out of the same store I continually combine fresh and fresh likenesses of things I have experienced, or believed on the strength of what I have experienced, with the past; and from these I infer future actions and events and hopes, and reflect on all of these as present. "I will do this or that," I say to myself in that great container of my mind, stocked with the images of so many great things — "and this or that will follow." "If only this or that could happen!" "God forbid this or that!" That is how I talk to myself; and while I talk, the images of everything I mention are there, out of the same treasury of memory. And I could not speak of any of them if the images were missing.

Great is this power of memory, exceedingly great, my God: a vast and boundless room. Who has ever reached the bottom of it? And yet this is a power of mine, and belongs to my nature; and I do not myself take in everything that I am. So the mind is too narrow to hold itself. And where can what it does not hold of itself be? Outside it, and not inside? Then how does it not take itself in? An extraordinary astonishment comes over me at this; I am seized with amazement.

And men go off to marvel at the heights of mountains, and the huge waves of the sea, and the broad courses of rivers, and the sweep of the ocean, and the circling of the stars — and they pass themselves by. They do not marvel that when I spoke of all these things I was not seeing them with my eyes, and yet could not have spoken of them unless I were at that moment actually seeing, inwardly in my memory, the mountains and waves and rivers and stars I had seen, and the ocean that I take on trust — and seeing them with the same vast distances between them as when I saw them outside. And yet I did not draw them into myself by seeing them, when I looked at them with my eyes; they themselves are not in me, only their images. And I know by which bodily sense each of them was stamped on me.

But it is not only these things that the immeasurable capacity of my memory holds. Here too is everything I learned of the liberal arts and have not yet forgotten, moved as it were to some inner place which is not a place at all. And it is not the images of them that are there but the things themselves. For whatever I know of literature, or of the art of argument, or of how many kinds of question there are, exists in my memory in such a way that I have not taken in the image and left out the thing; it is not as if it had sounded and passed away like a voice fixed on the ear by an impression, which could be recalled as if it were still sounding when it is not; or as a smell, which while it passes and evaporates into the air affects the sense of smell and so conveys into the memory an image of itself, which we renew by remembering; or as food, which certainly has no taste any more once it is in the stomach and yet still in a manner tastes in the memory; or as anything the body perceives by touch, which the memory still conceives after it has been taken away from us. For those things are not transferred into the memory; only their images are caught up, with an admirable speed, and stored in remarkable cabinets, so to speak, and from there wonderfully brought out again by the act of remembering.

But now, when I hear that there are three kinds of question — whether a thing is, what it is, and what sort it is — I do indeed hold the images of the sounds that make up those words, and know that those sounds passed with a noise through the air and are now gone. But the things themselves that those sounds signify I never reached with any bodily sense, and never made out anywhere except in my mind; and it is not their images I have laid up in my memory but the things themselves. How they got into me, let them say if they can; for I have gone over all the entrances of my flesh and cannot find which one they came in by. The eyes say, "If those images were coloured, we reported them." The ears say, "If they made a sound, we announced them." The nostrils say, "If they smelled, they came past us." Taste says, "Unless they have a flavour, do not ask me." Touch says, "If it has no size, I did not handle it; and if I did not handle it, I gave no notice of it." So where and how did these things get into my memory? I do not know how. When I learned them, I was not taking somebody else's word for them but recognising them in my own mind; and approving them as true, I entrusted them to my memory, laying them up so as to bring them out whenever I wanted. So they were in my heart before I learned them, and yet they were not in my memory. Where then? And why, when they were spoken, did I recognise them and say, "That is right, that is true", unless they were already in the memory, but thrown back and buried in deeper recesses, so that if somebody else's prompting had not drawn them out, I might have been unable to conceive them at all?

So we find that to learn things of this kind — things whose images we do not take in through the senses, but which we perceive inwardly by themselves, without images, as they are — is nothing but to gather up by thinking, and by attending to make sure, the things the memory already held scattered and unarranged, so that they are laid ready to hand in that same memory where before they lay unknown, scattered and neglected, and so come readily to a mind grown familiar with them. And how many things of this sort my memory carries that have already been found out and, as I said, laid ready to hand — things we are said to have learned and come to know! If I stop calling them to mind even for a short time, they sink again and slip back, as it were, into deeper recesses, so that they have to be thought out from there again as if they were new, since they have nowhere else to be; they must be gathered together again in order to be known, that is, collected out of their scattering. This is where the Latin word for thinking comes from: cogo means to collect, and cogito, to think, means to collect again. And the mind has taken this word for its own, so that what is properly called thinking is not what is collected in any old way but what is re-collected, that is, brought together in the mind.

The memory also contains countless principles and laws of numbers and dimensions, none of them stamped there by any bodily sense, since they have no colour or sound or taste or smell or feel. I have heard the sound of the words that stand for them when they are discussed; but the sounds are one thing and the things another. The sounds are different in Greek and Latin; the things are neither Greek nor Latin nor any other language. I have seen architects' lines, the very finest, like a spider's thread; but those are still different — they are not the images of the lines my bodily eye showed me. Anyone knows them who recognises them inside himself without any conception whatever of a body. I have also perceived the numbers of the things we count with all the senses of my body; but the numbers we count with are different, and are not the images of those, and therefore they truly are. Let the man who cannot see them laugh at me for saying this, and I shall pity him for laughing.

All these things I remember, and I remember how I learned them. I have also heard, and remember, many things falsely objected against them; and though the objections are false, it is not false that I remember them. And I remember distinguishing between those truths and these falsehoods brought against them. And I can see that distinguishing them now is one thing, and remembering that I often distinguished them when I often thought about them is another. So I remember having often understood these things; and what I now make out and understand, I lay up in my memory, so that later I may remember that I understood it now. So I also remember having remembered; just as later, if I call to mind that I have been able to remember these things now, it will be by the power of memory that I call it to mind.

The same memory also contains the feelings of my mind — not in the way my mind itself contains them when it feels them, but in a quite different way, according to a power of its own. For without rejoicing I remember having rejoiced, and without sorrow I recall past sorrow. I look back without fear on having once been afraid, and call to mind a past desire without desire. Sometimes the opposite happens, and I remember a past sorrow with joy, and a past joy with sorrow. There is nothing remarkable in this as far as the body goes, since the mind is one thing and the body another; so if I remember a past bodily pain with joy, that is not so surprising. But this memory itself is mind. When we give something into somebody's charge to be kept in memory, we say, "Make sure you keep it in mind"; and when we forget, we say, "It did not come to my mind," or, "It slipped my mind," calling the memory itself the mind. That being so, how is it that when I remember a past sorrow with joy, the mind has joy and the memory has sorrow — the mind being joyful over the joyfulness in it, and the memory not being sad over the sadness in it? Does memory perhaps not belong to the mind? Who would say that? So the memory is, so to speak, the stomach of the mind, and joy and sadness are like sweet and bitter food, which, when committed to the memory, are passed into that stomach, where they can be stored but cannot be tasted. It is ridiculous to imagine these are really alike — and yet they are not entirely unlike.

But now, it is out of my memory that I bring it when I say there are four disturbances of the mind: desire, joy, fear, sorrow. And whatever I can argue about them, dividing each into its subordinate kinds and defining it, I find in my memory what to say and bring it out from there — and yet I am not disturbed by any of these disturbances when I remember them by calling them to mind. And before I recalled and brought them back, they were there, which is why they could be brought out by recollection. Perhaps, then, they come out of the memory as food is brought up out of the stomach by chewing the cud. Then why does the man who is arguing and recollecting like this not taste in the mouth of his thinking the sweetness of joy or the bitterness of sorrow? Is the comparison unlike in this, because it is not alike in every respect? For who would willingly talk about these things if, every time we named grief or fear, we were forced to be sad or afraid? And yet we could not talk about them if we did not find in our memory not only the sounds of the names, according to the images stamped there by the senses of the body, but ideas of the things themselves — which we never received by any bodily entrance, but which the mind, perceiving them by the experience of its own passions, committed to the memory, or which the memory retained of itself without being given them.

But whether by images or not, who can readily say? I name a stone, I name the sun, the things themselves not being present to my senses but their images to my memory. I name a bodily pain, and it is not present to me when nothing aches; yet unless its image were present in my memory I should not know what to say about it, or be able to tell pain from pleasure in discussion. I name bodily health; and being sound in body, the thing itself is present with me — and yet unless its image were also present in my memory, I could not possibly recall what the sound of the name means. And a sick man, hearing health named, would not recognise what was said, unless the same image were retained by the power of memory, though the thing itself were absent from his body. I name the numbers we count with; and it is not their images but the numbers themselves that are present in my memory. I name the image of the sun, and that image is present in my memory — for I do not recall the image of its image; the image itself is present to me as I call it to mind. I name memory, and I recognise what I name. And where do I recognise it, but in memory itself? Is memory also present to itself by an image, and not by itself?

And what about when I name forgetfulness, and recognise what I name? Where would I recognise it from, if I did not remember it? I am not talking about the sound of the word but about the thing it means; and if I had forgotten that, I could not recognise what the sound means. So when I remember memory, memory itself is present to itself through itself; but when I remember forgetfulness, both memory and forgetfulness are present — memory, by which I remember, and forgetfulness, which I remember. But what is forgetfulness except the absence of memory? How then is it present for me to remember it, when its being present makes remembering impossible? But if what we remember we hold in memory, and if without remembering forgetfulness we could never recognise the thing meant when we heard the word, then forgetfulness is retained by the memory. So it is present, so that we do not forget it — and being present, we forget. Are we to understand from this that forgetfulness, when we remember it, is present to the memory not by itself but by its image? Because if it were present by itself, it would not make us remember but forget. Who will search this out? Who will grasp how it works?

Lord, I certainly labour at it, and labour in myself; I have become a heavy soil needing too much sweat of the brow. We are not now investigating the regions of the sky, or measuring the distances of the stars, or asking how the earth is balanced. It is I myself who remember; I, the mind. There is nothing so remarkable if what I am not is far from me. But what is nearer to me than myself? And look: the power of my own memory is not understood by me, and I cannot so much as name myself without it. For what am I to say, when it is plain to me that I remember forgetfulness? Am I to say that what I remember is not in my memory? Or am I to say that forgetfulness is in my memory precisely so that I should not forget? Both are utterly absurd. What third way is there? How can I say the image of forgetfulness is retained by my memory and not forgetfulness itself, when I remember it? And how could I say even that, seeing that when the image of anything is stamped on the memory the thing itself must be present first for the image to be stamped from? For that is how I remember Carthage, and every place I have been, and the faces of men I have seen, and the things reported by the other senses, and the health or sickness of my body. When those things were present, my memory took images from them, which, being present with me, I could look at and bring back in my mind when I remembered them in their absence. If, then, this forgetfulness is held in the memory through its image and not through itself, then plainly it must once have been present for the image to be taken. But when it was present, how did it write its image in the memory, seeing that forgetfulness by being present rubs out even what it finds already noted down? And yet, in whatever way — and that way is past conceiving and past explaining — I am certain that I do remember forgetfulness itself, the very thing by which what we remember is rubbed out.

Great is the power of memory, a fearful thing, my God, a deep and boundless complexity; and this thing is the mind, and this is myself. What am I then, my God? What kind of nature am I? A life various and manifold and enormously immense. Look, in the plains and caves and caverns of my memory, countless and countlessly full of countless kinds of things — either by images, as with all bodies, or by being actually there, as with the arts, or by certain notions or impressions, as with the feelings of the mind, which the memory holds even when the mind is not feeling them, since whatever is in the memory is also in the mind — over all these I run and fly; I dive in on this side and on that as far as I can, and there is no end to it. So great is the power of memory, so great the power of life, even in a man who is mortal.

What shall I do, then, my true life, my God? I shall go beyond this power of mine that is called memory; I shall go beyond it, so that I may come near to You, sweet light. What do You say to me? See, I am climbing up through my mind towards You who remain above me. Yes, I shall now go beyond this power of mine that is called memory, wanting to reach You where You can be reached, and to hold fast to You where one can hold fast to You. For even animals and birds have memory; otherwise they could not find their way back to their dens and nests, or do many other things they are used to; indeed they could not get used to anything except by memory. So I shall go beyond memory too, so as to reach him who has separated me from the four-footed beasts and made me wiser than the birds of the air. I shall go beyond memory too — and where shall I find You, truly good and certain sweetness? And where shall I find You? If I find You outside my memory, then I do not hold You in my memory. And how shall I find You, if I do not remember You?

For the woman who had lost her coin and looked for it with a light would never have found it unless she remembered it. When it was found, how would she have known it was the right one unless she remembered it? I remember looking for and finding many things; and I know this from it, that when I was looking for one of them and was asked, "Is this it? Is that it?" I kept saying no until the thing I was looking for was held out to me. If I had not remembered it, whatever it was, then even if it were held out to me I still would not find it, because I could not recognise it. And that is always the way when we look for and find something lost. Even so, when something disappears from sight by accident but not from the memory — any visible object — its image is still kept inside, and it is looked for until it is restored to sight; and when it is found, it is recognised by the image that is within. And we do not say we have found what was lost unless we recognise it; and we cannot recognise it unless we remember it. So it was lost to the eyes but kept in the memory.

But what about when the memory itself loses something, as happens when we forget and go looking so as to recollect? Where, in the end, do we search, but in the memory itself? And there, if one thing happens to be offered instead of another, we reject it until what we are looking for comes to meet us; and when it does, we say, "This is it" — which we could not say unless we recognised it, or recognise it unless we remembered it. So we certainly had forgotten it. Or had the whole not escaped us, but the lost part was being looked for by the part we still had hold of, because the memory felt it was not carrying along everything it usually did, and, crippled by having its old habit cut short, demanded back what it was missing? For instance, if we see or think of somebody we know, and having forgotten his name try to recover it, then whatever else occurs to us will not connect with him, because it was not in the habit of being thought of along with him, and so it is rejected — until the right one presents itself, and our knowledge settles on it evenly as its accustomed object. And where does that come from, if not out of the memory itself? Even when we recognise it on being reminded by somebody else, that is where it comes from; for we do not accept it as something new, but on recollecting it we agree that the name given is the right one. If it had been completely rubbed out of the mind, we would not remember it even when reminded. For we have not yet completely forgotten what we remember having forgotten. And what we have completely forgotten, being lost, we cannot even look for.

How then do I look for You, Lord? For when I look for You, my God, I am looking for a happy life. I will look for You, so that my soul may live; for my body lives by my soul and my soul lives by You. How then do I look for a happy life, seeing that I do not have it until I can say, where I ought to say it, "This is enough"? How do I look for it? By remembering, as though I had forgotten it and remembered that I had forgotten it? Or by wanting to learn it as something unknown, either never having known it, or having forgotten it so thoroughly that I do not even remember forgetting it? Is a happy life not what everybody wants, and nobody at all does not want? Where have they known it, that they want it so? Where have they seen it, that they love it so? Certainly we have it, though how I do not know.

And there is another way of having it, by which a man is happy when he has it; and there are people who are blessed in hope. These have it in a lesser sense than those who have it in fact — and yet they are better off than those who are happy neither in fact nor in hope. And even these last, if they did not have it in some way, would not want to be happy as they do; and that they do want it is quite certain. So they have known it somehow, and have it by some sort of knowledge; what sort, I do not know, and I am puzzled whether it is in the memory. If it is, then we were once happy — whether each of us separately, or in the man who first sinned, in whom we all died and from whom we are all born into misery, I am not asking now. I only ask whether the happy life is in the memory. For we could not love it unless we knew it. We hear the name, and we all admit we want the thing; it is not the mere sound that pleases us. When a Greek hears the word in Latin he is not pleased, since he does not know what has been said; but we Latin speakers are pleased, and so would he be if he heard it in Greek — because the thing itself is neither Greek nor Latin, and Greeks and Latins and speakers of every other language long for it just as eagerly. So it is known to everybody; for if they could all be asked with one voice, "Would you like to be happy?" they would answer without hesitation that they would. And that could not be, unless the thing the name stands for were kept in their memory.

But is it kept there in the way a man who has seen Carthage remembers Carthage? No; a happy life is not seen with the eye, because it is not a body. Then as we remember numbers? No; a man who has those in his knowledge does not go on trying to attain them, whereas a happy life we have in our knowledge and therefore love, and still want to attain, so as to be happy. Then as we remember eloquence? No; for although on hearing that word too some people call the thing to mind who are not yet eloquent, and many want to be, from which it appears that it is in their knowledge — even so, they have observed other people being eloquent with their bodily senses, and have been delighted, and want to be like them. Though indeed they would not be delighted without some inward knowledge of it, or want to be like them unless they were delighted. But a happy life is something we do not experience in others by any bodily sense. Then as we remember joy? Perhaps; for I remember my joy even when I am sad, as I remember a happy life when I am unhappy. And I never saw, heard, smelled, tasted or touched my joy with any bodily sense; I experienced it in my mind when I rejoiced, and the knowledge of it stuck in my memory, so that I can recall it sometimes with disgust and sometimes with longing, depending on what sort of thing it was I remember having rejoiced in. For I have been steeped in a kind of joy even by foul things, which I now detest and curse when I recall them; and at other times by good and honourable things, which I recall with longing, though perhaps they are no longer here — and so I recall a former joy with sadness.

Where then, and when, did I experience my happy life, that I should remember it and love it and long for it? And it is not only I, or a few others; all of us would gladly be happy. And unless we knew it by some sort of definite knowledge, we would not want it with so definite a will. But how is it that if two men are asked whether they want to go to the wars, one might answer that he does and the other that he does not; but if they were asked whether they wanted to be happy, both would say at once and without hesitation that they did? And the only reason the one goes to the wars and the other does not is to be happy. Is it perhaps that one looks for his joy in this and another in that, and they all agree in wanting to be happy, as they would agree, if asked, that they wanted to have joy — and this joy is what they call a happy life? So although one gets this joy by one means and another by another, they all have one end they are trying to reach: joy. And this being something everybody must say they have experienced, it is therefore found in the memory and recognised whenever the phrase "a happy life" is mentioned.

Far be it, Lord, far be it from the heart of Your servant who is confessing to You here, far be it that whatever the joy might be, I should therefore think myself happy. For there is a joy that is not given to the ungodly but to those who love You for Your own sake, whose joy You Yourself are. And this is the happy life: to rejoice towards You, from You, for You. This is it, and there is no other. Those who think there is another are chasing some other joy, and not the true one — though even their will is not turned away from some semblance of joy.

So it is not certain that everybody wants to be happy, since those who do not want to rejoice in You, which is the only happy life, do not truly want a happy life. Or does everybody want it, but, because the flesh craves against the Spirit and the Spirit against the flesh so that they cannot do what they want, they fall back on what they can do and are satisfied with that — because what they cannot do, they do not want strongly enough to make themselves able? For if I ask anybody whether he would rather have his joy in truth or in falsehood, he will hesitate as little to say "in the truth" as to say that he wants to be happy. For a happy life is joy in the truth; and that is a joy in You, who are the truth, God my light, health of my face, my God. This is the happy life that everybody wants; this life, which alone is happy, everybody wants; joy in the truth is what everybody wants. I have met plenty of people who wanted to deceive; not one who wanted to be deceived. So where did they come to know this happy life, except where they also know the truth? For they love the truth too, since they do not want to be deceived. And when they love a happy life, which is nothing but rejoicing in the truth, then they love the truth as well — which they would not love if there were not some trace of it in their memory. Why then do they not rejoice in it? Why are they not happy? Because they are more strongly taken up with other things, which have more power to make them miserable than the thing they so faintly remember has to make them happy. There is still a little light in men; let them walk, let them walk, in case the darkness overtakes them.

But why does truth breed hatred, and why does Your man, preaching the truth, become an enemy to them, when a happy life is loved, which is nothing but rejoicing in the truth? It must be that truth is loved in such a way that people who love something else would like the thing they love to be the truth; and since they do not want to be deceived, they will not be convinced that they are. So they hate the truth for the sake of the thing they loved instead of it. They love the truth when it shows them light and hate it when it shows them up. Since they do not want to be deceived and do want to deceive, they love it when it reveals itself to them and hate it when it reveals them. And truth will pay them back for this: those who did not want to be shown up by it, it shows up against their will, and does not show itself to them. That is how the human mind is — blind and sick, foul and ugly, wanting to be hidden, and refusing to let anything be hidden from itself. And it gets the opposite of what it wants: it is not hidden from the truth, and the truth is hidden from it. And yet even in this misery it would rather rejoice in truths than in falsehoods. It will be happy, then, when it rejoices, with no distraction interrupting, in that one truth by whom everything true is true.

See what a space I have crossed in my memory looking for You, Lord; and I have not found You outside it. Nor have I found anything about You except what I have kept in my memory since I first learned You. For since I learned You, I have not forgotten You. Where I found truth, there I found my God, who is the truth itself; and since I learned that, I have not forgotten it. So since I learned You, You have lived in my memory, and there I find You when I call You to mind and take delight in You. These are my holy delights, which You have given me in Your mercy, having regard to my poverty.

But where in my memory do You live, Lord? Where do You live there? What kind of lodging have You made for Yourself? What kind of sanctuary have You built for Yourself? You have given my memory this honour, that You live in it — but in what quarter of it You live is what I am now considering. For in thinking about You I went past the parts of it that animals have too, since I did not find You there among the images of bodily things. And I came to the parts where I put the feelings of my mind, and did not find You there. And I went into the very seat of my mind, which it has in my memory, since the mind remembers itself too; and You were not there either. For as You are not a bodily image, and not the feeling of a living creature, as when we rejoice or grieve or desire or fear or remember or forget or the like, so neither are You the mind itself — because You are the Lord God of the mind. And all these things change, while You remain unchangeable above them all; and yet You have consented to live in my memory ever since I learned You. And why am I now asking in what part of it You live, as though there were parts in it? I am sure that You live in it, since I have remembered You ever since I learned You, and it is there that I find You when I call You to mind.

Where then did I find You, that I might learn You? For You were not in my memory before I learned You. Where then did I find You, that I might learn You, but in Yourself above me? There is no place; we go backwards and forwards, and there is no place. Everywhere, truth, You give audience to everyone who asks Your advice, and answer them all at once, though they ask about all sorts of different things. Clearly You answer, though not everybody clearly hears. Everyone consults You about what they want, though they do not always hear what they want. Your best servant is the one who looks not so much to hear from You what he himself wants, as rather to want what he hears from You.

Late have I loved You, beauty so old and so new: late have I loved You! And look, You were within me, and I was outside, and it was out there that I looked for You; deformed as I was, I flung myself on the beautiful things You have made. You were with me, and I was not with You. Those things kept me far away from You, things which would not exist at all unless they existed in You. You called and shouted and shattered my deafness. You flashed and blazed and scattered my blindness. You breathed out fragrance, and I drew in my breath and I pant for You. I tasted, and now I hunger and thirst. You touched me, and I burned for Your peace.

When I hold fast to You with the whole of myself, I shall nowhere have grief or labour, and my life shall wholly live, being wholly full of You. But since now You lift up whoever You fill, and I am not full of You, I am a burden to myself. My deplorable joys fight with my joyful sorrows, and which side has won I do not know. I am wretched: Lord, have pity on me. My bad sorrows fight with my good joys, and which side has won I do not know. I am wretched: Lord, have pity on me. I am wretched: look, I am not hiding my wounds. You are the doctor and I am the patient; You are merciful and I am miserable. Is not a man's life on earth all trial? Who wants troubles and difficulties? You command them to be endured, not to be loved. Nobody loves what he endures, though he may love enduring; for although he is glad that he endures, he would rather there were nothing for him to endure. In hard times I long for good ones; in good times I dread the hard ones. What middle ground is there between the two, where a man's life is not all trial? A curse on the world's prosperity, twice over: for fear of adversity, and for the corruption of joy. A curse on the world's adversity, twice over, and a third time: for the longing for prosperity, and because adversity itself is a hard thing, and because it may shatter our endurance. Is a man's life on earth not all trial, without a single break?

And all my hope is nowhere but in Your exceedingly great mercy. Give what You command, and command what You will. You command us self-control; and when I knew, says one, that nobody can be self-controlled unless God gives it, this itself was part of wisdom, to know whose gift it is. By self-control we are indeed gathered up and brought back into one, out of the many we were scattered into. For a man loves You too little if he loves anything alongside You that he does not love for Your sake. Love, always burning and never burning out! Charity, my God, set me alight. You command self-control: give what You command, and command what You will.

You command me self-control from the appetite of the flesh, the appetite of the eyes, and the ambition of the world. You command self-control from concubinage; and as for marriage itself, You have counselled something better than what You have permitted. And since You gave it, it was done, even before I became a minister of Your sacrament. But there still live in my memory, which I have said so much about, the images of such things as my bad habit fixed there. They come at me powerless while I am awake; but in sleep they come not only to give pleasure but even to win consent and something very like reality. So far does the illusion of an image prevail, in my soul and in my flesh, that when I am asleep, false visions persuade me to what true ones cannot when I am awake. Am I not then myself, Lord my God? And yet how much difference there is between myself and myself, in the moment when I pass from waking to sleeping, or come back from sleeping to waking! Where is reason then, which resists such suggestions when I am awake, and stands unshaken even if the things themselves are pressed on it? Is it shut up along with my eyes? Is it lulled asleep along with the senses of the body? And how is it that even in sleep we often do resist, and, remembering our resolve and staying chastely in it, give no consent to such enticements? And yet there is enough difference that when it goes the other way, we come back on waking to a clear conscience — and by that very difference we discover that we did not do what we are nevertheless sorry was in some way done in us.

Are You not mighty enough, God almighty, to heal all the diseases of my soul, and by Your more abundant grace to put out even the impure motions of my sleep? You will increase Your gifts in me more and more, Lord, so that my soul may follow me to You, disentangled from the birdlime of craving; so that it does not rebel against itself, and even in dreams does not consent to those debasing corruptions through images of sense — let alone commit them to the point of the body's pollution. It is not hard for the Almighty, who is able to do more than we ask or think, to bring it about that nothing of this sort should have the very slightest influence over the pure affections even of a sleeper, not even the sort of influence that a thought could hold back: and to bring it about not only over a lifetime but at my present age. But what I still am in this kind of evil of mine I have confessed to my good Lord, rejoicing with trembling in what You have given me, and mourning over what is still unfinished in me; and hoping that You will bring Your mercies to completion in me, right up to that perfect peace which my outward and inward man will have with You, when death is swallowed up in victory.

There is another evil of the day, and I wish the day's own evil were enough for it. By eating and drinking we repair the daily wearing away of our body, until You destroy both the stomach and the food, when You shall kill my emptiness with a wonderful fullness and clothe this corruptible thing with an eternal incorruption. But for now the necessity is sweet to me, and I fight against that sweetness so as not to be taken prisoner; and I carry on a daily war by fasting, often bringing my body into subjection — and my pains are removed by pleasure. For hunger and thirst are a kind of pain: they burn and kill like a fever, unless the medicine of food comes to our aid.
