And since that is ready to hand through the comfort of Your gifts, with which land and water and air serve our weakness, our calamity gets called gratification.

You have taught me this: that I should set about taking food as medicine. But while I am passing from the discomfort of emptiness to the contentment of being filled, the trap of craving is set for me in that very passage. For the passing is itself a pleasure, and there is no other way to get where we must go. And since health is the reason for eating and drinking, a dangerous pleasure attaches itself as an attendant, and mostly tries to get in front, so that it is for her sake that I do what I say I am doing, or want to do, for the sake of health. And the two do not have the same measure: what is enough for health is too little for pleasure. And it is often uncertain whether it is the body's necessary care still asking for sustenance, or a voluptuous deceitfulness of greed offering its services. The unhappy soul is glad of that uncertainty, and prepares an excuse in it to shield itself, glad that it is not clear what would be enough for a moderate health, so that under the cloak of health it can disguise the business of gratification. These temptations I try to resist daily, and I call on Your right hand and refer my perplexities to You, since I still have no settled policy about them.

I hear the voice of my God commanding: Do not let your hearts be weighed down with overeating and drunkenness. Drunkenness is far from me; may You have mercy, so that it does not come near. But overeating sometimes creeps up on Your servant; may You have mercy, so that it may be far from me. For nobody can be self-controlled unless You give it. Many things You give us when we pray for them; and whatever good we received before we prayed, we received from You — and indeed we received it beforehand so that we might afterwards know this. I was never a drunkard, but I have known drunkards made sober by You. So it was from You that those who were never such should not become such, as it was from You that those who had been such should not always be so; and it was from You that both should know who it was from. I heard another word of Yours: Do not go after your appetites, and turn away from your own pleasure. And by Your kindness I have heard a saying I have greatly loved: neither if we eat shall we have more, nor if we do not eat shall we have less — which is to say that the one will not make me rich, nor the other wretched. And I heard another: I have learned, in whatever state I am, to be content with it; I know how to have plenty and how to go without. I can do all things through Christ who strengthens me. There is a soldier of the heavenly camp, and not the dust that we are. But remember, Lord, that we are dust, and that You made man out of dust; and he was lost and is found. And he could not do this of himself, because the man I loved so much, saying this by the in-breathing of Your inspiration, was made of the same dust. I can do all things, he says, through him who strengthens me. Strengthen me, so that I can. Give what You command, and command what You will. He admits that he has received; and when he boasts, he boasts in the Lord. And I have heard another begging that he might receive: Take from me, he says, the desires of the belly. From which it appears, my holy God, that You give when what You command to be done is done.

You have taught me, good Father, that to the pure all things are pure, but that it is bad for a man who eats and gives offence by eating; and that every creature of Yours is good and nothing is to be refused that is received with thanksgiving; and that food does not commend us to God; and that no man should judge us over food or drink; and that the man who eats should not despise the man who does not, and the man who does not eat should not judge the man who does. These things I have learned; thanks be to You, praise be to You, my God, my master, knocking at my ears and lighting up my heart. Deliver me from every temptation. It is not uncleanness in food I am afraid of but the uncleanness of craving. I know that Noah was allowed to eat every kind of meat that was fit for food; that Elijah was fed on meat; that John, endowed with an admirable abstinence, was not polluted by feeding on living creatures, namely locusts. And I know that Esau was tricked by craving lentils, and that David blamed himself for wanting a drink of water, and that our king was tempted not over meat but over bread. And so the people in the desert also deserved rebuke, not for wanting meat but because in their desire for food they grumbled against the Lord.

Set among these temptations, then, I struggle daily against craving in eating and drinking. For it is not the sort of thing I can settle to cut off once and for all and never touch again, as I could with concubinage. The bridle of the throat has to be held tempered between slackness and stiffness. And who is there, Lord, who is not carried somewhat beyond the limits of necessity? Whoever he is, he is a great man; let him make Your name great. But I am not such a man, for I am a sinful man. And yet I too magnify Your name; and he who has overcome the world makes intercession to You for my sins, counting me among the weak members of his body, because Your eyes have seen what is unfinished in him, and in Your book all shall be written.

The attractions of smell do not concern me much. When they are missing I do not miss them; when they are there I do not refuse them; and I am always ready to be without them. So it seems to me — and perhaps I am deceived. For that too is a mournful darkness, by which my own capacities are hidden from me, so that when my mind asks itself about its own powers it does not readily dare to believe itself, because even what is in it is mostly hidden until experience brings it out. And nobody ought to feel safe in a life the whole of which is called a trial: a man who has been capable of turning from worse to better may just as well be turned from better to worse. Our only hope, our only confidence, our only sure promise is Your mercy.

The delights of the ear had bound and mastered me more firmly, and You loosened me and set me free. Now I do rest a little in the melodies that Your words breathe a soul into, when they are sung with a sweet and well-tuned voice — though not so as to be stuck there, since I can get free whenever I want. But along with the words, which are their life and by which they gain admission to me, the melodies look for a place of some honour in my affections, and I can hardly find them a suitable one. Sometimes I seem to myself to give them more honour than is fitting, when I feel that our minds are raised to a flame of devotion more holily and more fervently by the holy words themselves when they are sung than when they are not; and that the various feelings of our spirit have, by a sweet variety, their own proper measures in the voice and the singing, which stir them up by some hidden correspondence. But this contentment of the flesh, to which the soul must not be handed over to be enfeebled, often deceives me — the senses not waiting on reason patiently enough to follow her, but, having been let in merely for her sake, trying to run ahead and lead her. So in these things I sin without noticing, and notice afterwards.

At other times, over-anxious to avoid this very deception, I go wrong in being too strict — sometimes so far as to wish the whole melody of the sweet music used for David's psalter banished from my ears and from the church's too. And the safer way seems to me the one I have often been told about Athanasius, bishop of Alexandria, who made the reader recite the psalm with so slight an inflection of the voice that it was nearer speaking than singing. And yet again, when I remember the tears I shed at the psalm-singing of Your church in the first days of my recovered faith, and how at this time I am moved not by the singing but by the things sung, when they are sung with a clear voice and the most suitable setting, then I acknowledge the great usefulness of this practice. So I waver between the danger of pleasure and the proved wholesomeness, and I am inclined — though not pronouncing an irrevocable opinion — to approve the custom of singing in church, so that by the delight of the ears weaker minds may rise to a feeling of devotion. Yet when it happens that I am more moved by the voice than by the words being sung, I confess that I have sinned and deserve punishment, and then I would rather not hear the music. Look at the state I am in: weep with me, and weep for me, all of you who regulate your own feelings inwardly so that good action follows. Those of you who do not act, these things do not touch you. But You, Lord my God, listen; look, and see, and have mercy and heal me, You in whose presence I have become a problem to myself; and that is my weakness.

There remains the pleasure of these eyes of my flesh, to make my confessions about in the hearing of the ears of Your temple, those brotherly and devout ears; and so to conclude the temptations of the appetite of the flesh, which still attack me while I groan and long to be clothed with my house from heaven. The eyes love beautiful and varied shapes, and bright and soft colours. Let these not occupy my soul; let God occupy it instead, who made these things — very good indeed, but he is my good, not they. And they affect me while I am awake all day long, and I get no rest from them, as I do from music, and sometimes in silence from all voices. For this queen of colours, the light, bathing everything we look at, wherever I am during the day, gliding past me in all its various forms, soothes me even when I am busy with other things and not noticing it. And it winds itself in so strongly that if it is suddenly taken away it is looked for with longing, and if it stays away long it saddens the mind.

Light, which Tobit saw when, his own eyes closed, he taught his son the way of life, and went before him with the feet of charity, never straying! Light, which Isaac saw when his fleshly eyes were heavy and closed with age, and it was granted him to bless his sons without knowing which was which, and by blessing them to know them. Light, which Jacob saw when he too, blind with great age, with an illumined heart shed light in the persons of his sons on the different tribes of a future people, foreshadowed in them; and laid his hands, crossed in a mystery, on his grandsons by Joseph, not as their father corrected him by his outward eye, but as he himself inwardly discerned. This is the light; it is one, and all are one who see and love it. But that bodily light I was speaking of seasons the life of this world for its blind lovers with an enticing and dangerous sweetness. But those who know how to praise You for it, "Lord, creator of everything", take it up into Your hymns and are not taken up by it into their sleep. That is what I want to be. These seductions of the eyes I resist, so that my feet, which I walk Your road on, may not be trapped. And I lift my invisible eyes to You, so that You may pull my feet out of the snare. You pull them out again and again, since they get caught. You never stop pulling them out, while I keep tangling myself in the traps laid on every side; because You who keep Israel will neither slumber nor sleep.

What countless trifles men have added, made by all sorts of arts and manufactures, in our clothes and shoes and utensils and works of every kind, and in pictures and images too, far beyond any necessary and moderate use and any devout meaning — added just to tempt their own eyes! Outwardly following what they make themselves, and inwardly abandoning him by whom they were made themselves, and destroying what they were made to be. But I, my God and my glory, sing a hymn to You for this too, and dedicate praise to him who dedicates me; because those beautiful patterns which pass through men's souls into their skilful hands come from that beauty which is above our souls, which my soul sighs after day and night. But the makers and followers of the outward beauties take from there their rule for judging beauty, and not their rule for using it. And he is there, though they do not notice him, so that they might not wander off but keep their strength for You, and not scatter it abroad on pleasurable exhaustion. And I, though I say this and see it, get my own steps tangled in these outward beauties; and You pull me out, Lord, You pull me out, because Your loving-kindness is before my eyes. For I am wretchedly caught, and You mercifully pull me out — sometimes without my noticing, when I had only lightly touched them; at other times painfully, because I had stuck fast.

To this is added another form of temptation, dangerous in more ways. Besides the craving of the flesh, which consists in the delight of all the senses and pleasures, and in which its slaves waste away and perish far from You, the soul has, through the same bodily senses, a certain empty and prying desire, dressed up under the title of knowledge and learning: not of taking delight in the flesh, but of making experiments through the flesh. Since its seat is the appetite for knowledge, and sight is the sense chiefly used to gain knowledge, it is called in the language of Scripture the lust of the eyes. For seeing belongs properly to the eyes; and yet we use the word of the other senses too when we set them to look for knowledge. We do not say, listen how it flashes, or smell how it glows, or taste how it shines, or feel how it gleams; all of these are said to be seen. And yet we say not only, see how it shines, which the eyes alone can perceive, but also, see how it sounds, see how it smells, see how it tastes, see how hard it is. And so the general experience of the senses is called, as I say, the lust of the eyes, because the function of seeing, in which the eyes have the first claim, is taken over by the other senses on the analogy of sight when they go looking for any knowledge.

But from this it can be seen more plainly where the object of the senses is pleasure and where it is curiosity. Pleasure looks for objects that are beautiful, tuneful, fragrant, tasty, soft; curiosity looks for the opposite as well, for the sake of the trial — not for the sake of suffering the annoyance, but out of the appetite for trying and knowing. For what pleasure is there in seeing something in a mangled corpse that makes you shudder? And yet if one is lying nearby, they crowd round to be made sad and to turn pale. They are afraid to see it even in their sleep — as if anyone had forced them to see it while they were awake, or any report of its beauty had drawn them there! It is the same with the other senses, which it would take a long time to go through. From this disease of curiosity come all the strange sights put on in the theatre. From it men go on to search out the hidden powers of nature, which is beside our purpose, which it does no good to know, and which men want for nothing but the knowing. From it too, if magical arts are enquired into with the same aim of perverted knowledge. From it too, in religion itself, God is put to the test, when signs and wonders are demanded of him not for any good purpose but simply to try him out.

In this vast wilderness full of traps and dangers, I have cut off and thrown out of my heart a great many of them, as You have given me to do, God of my salvation. And yet when do I dare to say — since so many things of this kind buzz round our daily life on every side — when do I dare to say that none of this catches my attention or arouses an idle interest in me? It is true that the theatres do not carry me away now, and I do not care to know the courses of the stars, and my soul never consulted the ghosts of the dead; I detest all sacrilegious mysteries. And yet, Lord my God, to whom I owe humble and single-hearted service, by what tricks and suggestions does the enemy work on me to want some sign! But I beg You, by our king and by our pure and holy country Jerusalem, that as any consenting to it is far from me, so may it be further and further off still. But when I pray to You for somebody's salvation, my aim and intention are quite different. You give me, and will go on giving me, the will to follow You willingly, doing what You want.

Even so, in how many petty and contemptible things is our curiosity tempted daily, and how often do we give way? Who can count them? How often do we start out as though we were merely putting up with people telling us pointless stories, so as not to offend the weak, and then gradually take an interest in them? I no longer go to the circus to see a dog chasing a hare; but out in the country, if I pass one, that chase may distract me even from some weighty thought and pull me after it — not that I turn my horse aside, but I still turn my mind that way. And unless You, having made me see my own weakness, quickly warned me either to rise towards You by some thought arising from the sight itself, or else to despise it altogether and pass on, I stand there dully fixed on it. And what about when I am sitting at home and a lizard catching flies, or a spider tangling them as they blunder into her nets, takes my attention? Is it a different thing because they are only small creatures? I go on from them to praise You, the wonderful creator and orderer of everything — but that is not what draws my attention first. It is one thing to get up quickly and another not to fall. And my life is full of such things; and my one hope is Your wonderful great mercy. For when our heart becomes the container of such things and is overloaded with crowds of this abundant emptiness, then our prayers are often interrupted and distracted by it too; and while in Your presence we direct the voice of our heart to Your ears, this business of the greatest importance is broken off by the rushing in of I do not know what idle thoughts. Are we to count this too among the things of slight importance? Or can anything bring us back to hope except Your complete mercy, since You have begun to change us?

And You know how far You have already changed me, You who first healed me of the appetite for justifying myself, so that You might forgive all the rest of my wrongdoing, and heal all my weaknesses, and redeem my life from corruption, and crown me with mercy and pity, and satisfy my desire with good things. You who curbed my pride with reverence for You, and tamed my neck to Your yoke. And now I carry it and it is light to me, because that is what You promised and what You have done; and it truly was so, and I did not know it, when I was afraid to take it up.

But, Lord — You alone are Lord without pride, because You are the only true Lord and have no lord over You — has this third kind of temptation stopped in me too, or can it stop through this whole life? I mean, wanting to be feared and loved by men, for no other purpose than to get from it a joy which is no joy at all. It is a wretched life and a foul boastfulness. This above all is why men neither purely love nor purely fear You. And that is why You resist the proud and give grace to the humble; and why You thunder down on the ambitions of the world, and the foundations of the mountains shake. Since certain offices of human society make it necessary to be loved and feared by people, the enemy of our true blessedness presses hard on us, spreading his traps of "well done, well done" everywhere, so that when we snatch greedily at them we may be caught off guard, and part our joy from Your truth and settle it in the deceptions of men; and be pleased at being loved and feared, not for Your sake but in Your place. And so, having been made like him, he can have us for his own, not in the bonds of love but in the chains of punishment — he who planned to set his throne in the north, so that dark and frozen we might serve him, imitating You in a perverse and crooked way.

But we, Lord, are Your little flock; keep us for Your own, stretch Your wings over us and let us fly under them. Be our glory; let us be loved for Your sake, and let Your word be feared in us. A man who wants to be praised by men when You blame him will not be defended by men when You judge him, or rescued when You condemn him. But when it is not the sinner who is praised for the desires of his soul, nor the man who acts irreligiously who is called blessed, but a man is praised for some gift You have given him, and he takes more pleasure in the praise for himself than in having the gift he is praised for — then he too is praised while You disapprove; and the man who did the praising is better than the man praised. For the one took pleasure in a gift of God in a man, and the other was better pleased with the gift of a man than with the gift of God.

By these temptations we are attacked daily, Lord; we are attacked without a break. Our daily furnace is the human tongue. And here too You command us self-control. Give what You command, and command what You will. You know the groans of my heart on this matter, and the floods from my eyes. For I cannot find out how far I am cleansed of this plague, and I am very much afraid of my hidden sins, which Your eyes know and mine do not. In other kinds of temptation I have some means of examining myself; in this one, hardly any. In holding my mind back from the pleasures of the flesh and from idle curiosity, I can see how far I have got when I do without them, either by giving them up or by not having them, since then I can ask myself how much more or less troublesome it is not to have them. And riches, which are wanted so as to serve one or two or all three of these cravings — if the soul cannot tell whether it despises them while it has them, they can be thrown away so that it can test itself. But to be without praise, and test our powers that way, must we live badly, indeed so abandonedly and atrociously that nobody could know us without detesting us? What greater madness could be said or thought of? But if praise usually accompanies a good life and good works, and ought to, we should no more give up its company than give up the good life itself. And yet I do not know whether I can do well or badly without something, unless it is taken away.

What then do I confess to You about this kind of temptation, Lord? What, except that I am delighted by praise — but delighted more by truth itself than by praise? For if it were put to me whether I would rather be deranged and wrong about everything and praised by everybody, or consistent and quite settled in the truth and blamed by everybody, I know which I would choose. And yet I could wish that somebody else's approval did not even add to my joy over any good in me. But I admit it does add to it, and not only that: disapproval reduces it. And when I am troubled by this wretchedness of mine, an excuse occurs to me — and how much it is worth, You know, God, since it leaves me uncertain. For You have commanded us not only self-control, that is, what to hold our love back from, but righteousness too, that is, where to bestow it; and You have wanted us to love not only You but our neighbour as well. So often, when I am pleased by intelligent praise, I seem to myself to be pleased at my neighbour's competence or promise, or to be sorry for a fault in him when I hear him disparage either what he does not understand or what is good. For sometimes I am sorry at praise given to me, either when things are praised in me that I do not like in myself, or when smaller and slighter goods are valued more highly than they deserve. But again, how do I know whether I feel this way because I do not want the man praising me to disagree with me about myself — not because I am concerned for him, but because the same good things that please me in myself please me more when they please somebody else as well? For somehow I am not being praised when my judgement of myself is not being praised, since either things are praised that displease me, or things are praised more that please me less. So am I in doubt about myself in this matter?

Look: in You, truth, I see that I ought not to be moved by praise of myself for my own sake, but for my neighbour's good. And whether that is how it is with me I do not know. Here I know less about myself than about You. I beg You now, my God, reveal me to myself as well, so that I may confess to my brothers, who are to pray for me, where I find myself crippled. Let me examine myself again more carefully. If in the praise given me I am moved by my neighbour's good, why am I less moved when somebody else is unjustly disparaged than when it is me? Why am I more stung by an insult thrown at me than by one thrown just as unjustly at somebody else in front of me? Do I not know this either? Or is it in the end that I deceive myself, and do not do the truth before You in my heart and my tongue? Put this madness far from me, Lord, in case my own mouth becomes to me the sinner's oil that makes my head fat. I am poor and needy, and I am at my best when in hidden groans I displease myself and look for Your mercy, until what is lacking in my defective state is made new and complete, on into that peace which the eye of the proud does not know.

And yet the word that comes out of the mouth, and deeds that people know about, bring with them a most dangerous temptation through the love of praise, which canvasses and collects people's votes so as to establish a certain excellence of our own. It tempts even when I am condemning it in myself, on the very ground that I am condemning it; and it often boasts more emptily about its very contempt for empty boasting — so that it is no longer contempt for empty boasting that it boasts of, since it is not despising it while it is boasting of it.

Inside, too, inside there is another evil, arising out of a similar temptation, by which men become empty, pleasing themselves in themselves although they do not please others, or displease them, or do not care whether they please them. But in pleasing themselves they greatly displease You, not only by taking pleasure in things that are not good as though they were, but in Your good things as though they were their own; or if as Yours, then as though for their own merits; or even if as coming from Your grace, then not with a brotherly rejoicing but grudging that grace to others. In all these dangers and labours and the like You can see the trembling of my heart; and I feel my wounds being cured by You rather than never being inflicted by me.

Where have You not walked with me, truth, teaching me what to beware of and what to want, whenever I brought You what I could find down here and asked Your advice? With my outward senses, as far as I could, I surveyed the world, and observed the life my body has from me, and these senses of mine. From there I went into the recesses of my memory, those many spacious chambers wonderfully furnished with countless stores; and I considered it and stood aghast, being able to make out none of these things without You, and finding that none of them was You. Nor was I myself the one who found these things out — I who went over them all and worked to distinguish them and value each according to its worth, taking some things on the report of my senses and questioning others that I felt to be mixed up with myself, counting and distinguishing the reporters themselves, and in the great treasure house of my memory turning some things over, storing others up, drawing others out. Nor was I myself when I did this — that is, the power in me by which I did it was not I; and it was not You either, for You are the abiding light which I consulted about all these things, whether they were, and what they were, and how they were to be valued. And I heard You directing and commanding me. And this I often do; this delights me; and whenever I can get free of my necessary duties, this is the pleasure I fall back on. And in all these things I run over while consulting You, I can find no safe place for my soul except in You, where my scattered parts may be gathered and nothing of me may leave You. And sometimes You let me into a state of feeling deep inside me that is most unusual, rising to a strange sweetness, which, if it were completed in me, I do not know what there would be in it that did not belong to the life to come. But through my miserable encumbrances I sink back down into these lower things and am swept back by my old habit, and am held, and weep greatly, and am greatly held. So heavily does the burden of a bad habit weigh on us. Here I can stay and would not; there I would and cannot; and both ways I am miserable.

So I have considered the sicknesses of my sins under that threefold craving, and have called Your right hand to my help. For it was with a wounded heart that I looked at Your brightness, and, struck back, I said, "Who can get there? I am cast away from the sight of Your eyes." You are the truth presiding over everything; and I in my greed did not want to lose You but wanted to possess a lie along with You — just as nobody wants to tell a lie in such a way as to be ignorant of the truth himself. So I lost You, because You will not be possessed along with a lie.

Whom could I find to reconcile me to You? Was I to turn to the angels? With what prayers? With what sacraments? Many people trying to come back to You and unable to manage it by themselves have tried this, I hear, and fallen into a craving for strange visions, and been judged fit to be deceived. For being high-minded they looked for You by the pride of learning, puffing out their chests rather than beating them; and so by the agreement of their hearts they drew to themselves the princes of the air, fellow conspirators in their pride, by whom they were deceived through magical influences while looking for a mediator to be cleansed by — and there was none. For it was the devil, transforming himself into an angel of light. And it greatly enticed proud flesh that he had no body of flesh. For they were mortal and sinners; but You, Lord, to whom they proudly sought to be reconciled, are immortal and without sin. Now a mediator between God and man must have something like God about him and something like man; otherwise, if he were like man in both respects he would be far from God, and if like God in both he would be too unlike man — and either way he would not be a mediator. That deceitful mediator, then, by whom in Your secret judgements pride deserved to be deluded, has one thing in common with man, namely sin; another he would like to seem to have in common with God, and, not being clothed in mortal flesh, would boast of being immortal. But since the wages of sin is death, this he does have in common with men: that along with them he is condemned to death.

But the true mediator, whom in Your secret mercy You have shown to the humble and sent so that by his example they might learn that same humility, that mediator between God and man, the man Christ Jesus, appeared between mortal sinners and the immortal just one: mortal along with men, just along with God. So that, because the wages of righteousness is life and peace, he might by a righteousness joined to God cancel the death of sinners now made righteous — the death he chose to have in common with them. So he was shown to the holy men of old, so that they, through faith in his passion to come, as we through faith in it past, might be saved. For as man he was a mediator; but as the Word he was not in the middle between God and man, being equal with God, and God with God, and together one God.

How You have loved us, good Father, who did not spare Your only Son but handed him over for us who were ungodly! How You have loved us, for whom he who thought it no robbery to be equal with You was made obedient even to the death of the cross — he alone free among the dead, having the power to lay down his life and the power to take it up again; for us to You both victor and victim, and victor because victim; for us to You both priest and sacrifice, and priest because sacrifice; making us Your sons instead of Your servants by being born of You and serving us. So my hope in him is strong, that You will heal all my weaknesses through him who sits at Your right hand and pleads for us — otherwise I should despair. For my weaknesses are many and great, many and great; but Your medicine is mightier. We might have imagined Your Word to be far from any union with man, and despaired of ourselves, if he had not been made flesh and lived among us.

Frightened by my sins and by the weight of my misery, I had turned it over in my heart and made up my mind to run away into the wilderness. But You forbade me and gave me strength, saying: Christ died for all, so that those who live should no longer live for themselves but for him who died for them. See, Lord, I throw my care on You, so that I may live and consider the wonderful things of Your law. You know my lack of skill and my weaknesses; teach me and heal me. He, Your only Son, in whom all the treasures of wisdom and knowledge are hidden, has redeemed me with his blood. Let the proud not speak evil of me, because I think about my ransom, and eat and drink it and share it out; and being poor, I wanted to be filled from him, among those who eat and are satisfied — and they who seek the Lord shall praise him.

Book Eleven: Time and Eternity

Lord, since eternity is Yours, can You be ignorant of what I say to You? Or do You see in time what happens in time? Then why am I laying out so many accounts before You? Certainly not so that You might learn them from me, but to stir up my own devotion and my readers' towards You, so that we may all say, Great is the Lord and greatly to be praised. I have said it already and I shall say it again: I do this for love of Your love. For we pray, too, and yet truth has said, Your Father knows what you need before you ask. So it is our feelings that we lay open to You, confessing our own miseries and Your mercies to us, so that You may free us completely, since You have begun; so that we may stop being wretched in ourselves and be blessed in You — seeing You have called us to become poor in spirit, and gentle, and mourners, and hungry and thirsty for righteousness, and merciful, and pure in heart, and peacemakers. See, I have told You many things, as well as I could and as I wanted to, because You first wanted me to confess to You, my Lord God. For You are good, and Your mercy lasts for ever.

But how could I manage with the tongue of my pen to tell all Your encouragements, and all Your terrors and comforts and guidings, by which You brought me to preach Your word and give Your sacrament to Your people? And even if I could tell them in order, the drops of time are precious to me; and I have long been burning to meditate on Your law, and to confess to You in it both my skill and my lack of skill, the daybreak of Your enlightening and the remains of my darkness, until weakness is swallowed up by strength. And I do not want anything else to steal the hours I find free from the necessities of refreshing my body and my mind's powers, and from the service we owe to men, or which we pay them even when we do not owe it.

Lord my God, listen to my prayer, and let Your mercy hear my desire; for it is anxious not for myself alone but wants to serve brotherly love, and You see my heart, that this is so. I would sacrifice to You the service of my thought and my tongue; give me something that I may offer You. For I am poor and needy, and You are rich to all who call on You — You, who beyond the reach of care, care for us. Circumcise all rashness and all lying from both my inward and my outward lips; let Your Scriptures be my pure delight; let me neither be deceived in them nor deceive out of them. Lord, listen and take pity, Lord my God, light of the blind and strength of the weak, and light of those who see and strength of the strong as well: listen to my soul, and hear it crying out of the depths. For if Your ears are not with us in the depths too, where are we to go? Where cry out? The day is Yours and the night is Yours; at Your nod the moments fly by. Grant out of them a space for our thinking on the hidden things of Your law, and do not shut it against us who knock. For it is not for nothing that You wanted the dark secrets of so many pages written; and those forests are not without their deer, who retire into them and range and walk and feed and lie down and chew the cud. Complete me, Lord, and reveal them to me. Your voice is my joy; Your voice exceeds abundance of pleasures. Give me what I love; for I do love, and even that You gave me. Do not abandon Your own gifts, and do not despise Your green plant that is thirsty. Let me confess to You whatever I find in Your books, and hear the voice of praise, and drink in You, and consider the wonderful things out of Your law — from the very beginning, in which You made heaven and earth, right through to the everlasting reign of Your holy city with You.

Lord, have mercy on me and hear my desire. For it is not, I think, of the earth: not of gold and silver and precious stones, or fine clothes, or honours and offices, or the pleasures of the flesh, or even the necessities of the body for this pilgrim life of ours — all of which shall be added to those who seek Your kingdom and Your righteousness. See, Lord my God, where my desire lies. The wicked have told me of delights, but not like Your law, Lord. That is where my desire lies. See, Father, look and see and approve; and may it be pleasing in the sight of Your mercy that I should find grace before You, so that the inner parts of Your words may be opened to me as I knock. I beg You by our Lord Jesus Christ Your Son, the man of Your right hand, the Son of man, whom You have established for Yourself as mediator between us and You; through whom You sought us when we were not seeking You, and sought us so that we might seek You; Your Word, through whom You made all things, and me among them; Your only-begotten, through whom You called the believing people to adoption, and me among them. I beg You by him, who sits at Your right hand and pleads with You for us, in whom all the treasures of wisdom and knowledge are hidden. It is these I am looking for in Your books. Moses wrote of him; he says so himself; the truth says so.

I would like to hear and understand how "In the beginning You made heaven and earth." Moses wrote this; wrote it and went away, passed from here from You to You; and he is not in front of me now. If he were, I would take hold of him and ask him, and beg him in Your name to open these things to me, and would put the ears of my body to the sounds bursting from his mouth. And if he spoke Hebrew, it would strike my senses in vain and none of it would touch my mind; but if Latin, I would know what he said. But how would I know whether he was telling the truth? And if I knew even that, would I know it from him? Truly it would be within me, within, in the chamber of my thoughts, that truth — neither Hebrew nor Greek nor Latin nor barbarian, without organs of voice or tongue, without the sound of syllables — would say, "It is true," and I would say at once and confidently to that man of Yours, "You are telling the truth." So since I cannot ask him, I ask You, truth, of whom he was full when he spoke truth: You, my God, I beg, forgive my sins; and You who gave Your servant the gift of saying these things, give me too the gift of understanding them.

Look: heaven and earth exist, and they proclaim that they were created, since they change and vary. Whereas whatever has not been made and yet exists has nothing in it that it did not have before — and that is what changing and varying is. They proclaim also that they did not make themselves: "We exist because we have been made; so we did not exist before we existed, in a way that would let us make ourselves." And the evidence of the fact is the voice of those who say it. So it was You, Lord, who made them; You who are beautiful, since they are beautiful; You who are good, since they are good; You who are, since they are. And yet they are not beautiful or good or existent in the way that You their creator are: compared with You they are neither beautiful nor good, nor do they exist. This we know, thanks be to You. And our knowledge, compared with Your knowledge, is ignorance.

But how did You make heaven and earth? What was the engine of so mighty a fabrication? It was not like a human craftsman shaping one body out of another according to the judgement of his mind, which can somehow invest a thing with the form it sees inside itself by its inward eye. And where would he get the power to do that, unless You had made that mind? And he invests with a form something that already exists and has being — clay or stone or wood or gold or something of the sort. And where would those come from, if You had not appointed them? You made the craftsman his body; You made the mind that commands his limbs; You made the material he makes anything out of; You made the grasp by which he takes in his art and sees inside what he is doing outside; You made his bodily senses, by which, as by an interpreter, he can convey from mind to matter what he is doing, and report back to his mind what has been done, so that inside it may consult the truth presiding over it as to whether it has been done well. All these praise You, the creator of everything.

But how do You make them? How, God, did You make heaven and earth? Certainly it was not in heaven or on earth that You made heaven and earth; nor in the air or the waters, since these too belong to heaven and earth; nor did You make the whole world in the whole world, since there was no place to make it in before it was made, that it might exist. Nor did You hold anything in Your hand to make heaven and earth out of. Where would You have got that from, which You had not made, to make something else out of? For what exists, except because You exist? So You spoke, and they were made; and in Your Word You made them.

But how did You speak? In the way the voice came out of the cloud saying, This is my beloved Son? For that voice passed by and passed away; it began and ended; the syllables sounded and passed, the second after the first, the third after the second, and so on in order until the last after all the rest, and silence after the last. From which it is abundantly clear and plain that it was the movement of a creature that expressed it — a movement in time, serving Your eternal will. And these words of Yours, created for a moment, the outward ear reported to the intelligent soul, whose inward ear lay listening to Your eternal Word. But the soul compared these words sounding in time with that eternal Word of Yours in silence, and said, "It is different, utterly different. These words are far beneath me, and they do not exist, since they run away and pass; but the Word of my Lord remains above me for ever." If, then, You said in sounding and passing words that heaven and earth should be made, and so made heaven and earth, then there was a bodily creature before heaven and earth, by whose movements in time that voice could run its course in time. But there was nothing bodily before heaven and earth; or if there was, then surely You had created it without such a passing voice, in order to make out of it the passing voice by which to say, Let heaven and earth be made. For whatever it was that such a voice was made out of, unless it were made by You it could not exist at all. So by what Word did You speak, so that a body might be made, out of which these words might in turn be made?

So You are calling us to understand the Word — God with You, God — which is spoken eternally, and by which all things are spoken eternally. For what was spoken was not spoken one thing after another, one concluded so that the next might be spoken, but everything together and eternally. Otherwise we have time and change, and not a true eternity or a true immortality. This I know, my God, and I give thanks. I know it and confess it to You, Lord; and along with me, whoever is not ungrateful to sure truth knows it and blesses You. We know, Lord, we know: since in so far as anything is not what it was, and is what it was not, so far it dies and rises. So nothing of Your Word gives way or takes another's place, because it is truly immortal and eternal. And therefore to the Word co-eternal with You, You say all You say at once and eternally; and whatever You say shall be made, is made; and You do not make in any way except by saying. And yet the things You make by saying are not all made at once, or everlasting.

Why, Lord my God, I ask You? I see it after a fashion, but how to express it I do not know — unless it is that whatever begins to be and stops being, begins and stops at the moment when it is known in Your eternal reason that it ought to begin or stop; and in that reason nothing begins or stops. This is Your Word, which is also "the beginning, because it also speaks to us". So in the gospel he speaks through the flesh, and this sounded outwardly in men's ears, so that it might be believed and sought inwardly and found in the eternal truth, where the good and only master teaches all his disciples. There, Lord, I hear Your voice speaking to me, because the one who teaches us is speaking to us, while the one who does not teach us, though he speaks, is not speaking to us. And who teaches us now but the unchangeable truth? For even when we are warned through a changeable creature, we are only being led to the unchangeable truth, where we learn truly, standing and hearing him and rejoicing greatly at the bridegroom's voice, which restores us to him from whom we come. And therefore he is the beginning: because unless he stayed, there would be nowhere to come back to when we went astray. And when we come back from error, it is by knowing; and so that we may know, he teaches us, because he is the beginning, and speaks to us.

In this beginning, God, You have made heaven and earth: in Your Word, in Your Son, in Your power, in Your wisdom, in Your truth, wonderfully speaking and wonderfully making. Who shall take it in? Who shall declare it? What is it that gleams through me and strikes my heart without hurting it, so that I shudder and catch fire? I shudder in so far as I am unlike it; I catch fire in so far as I am like it. It is wisdom, wisdom itself, that gleams through me, cutting through my cloudiness — which closes over me again as I faint away from it, through the darkness that gathers on me for my punishment. For my strength is brought low in need, so that I cannot carry my own blessings, until You, Lord, who have been gracious to all my wrongdoing, heal all my weaknesses. For You will also redeem my life from corruption and crown me with loving-kindness and tender mercies, and satisfy my desire with good things, because my youth shall be renewed like an eagle's. For we are saved in hope, and so we wait with patience for Your promises. Let anyone who can, hear You speaking inwardly out of Your oracle; I shall boldly cry out, How wonderful are Your works, Lord — in wisdom You have made them all. And this wisdom is the beginning, and it was in that beginning that You made heaven and earth.

Are they not full of their old leaven, the people who say to us, "What was God doing before he made heaven and earth? For if he was unoccupied and doing nothing," they say, "why does he not go on doing nothing, from now on and for ever, as he did before? If some new movement arose in God, and a new will to make a creature he had never made before, how could that be a true eternity, in which a will arises that was not there before? For the will of God is not a creature but is prior to the creature, since nothing could be created unless the will of the creator had come first. So the will of God belongs to his very substance. And if something has arisen in God's substance that was not there before, then that substance cannot truly be called eternal. But if God's will has been from eternity that the creature should exist, why was the creature not from eternity too?"

People who talk this way do not yet understand You, wisdom of God, light of souls; they do not yet understand how the things are made that are made by You and in You. And yet they try to grasp eternal things, while their heart flutters between the movements of things past and things to come, and is still unsteady. Who will hold that heart still and fix it, so that it stands for a moment and for a moment catches the glory of that ever-fixed eternity, and compares it with the times, which are never fixed, and sees that it cannot be compared? And that a long time cannot become long except out of many movements passing by, which cannot be prolonged all together; whereas in the eternal nothing passes, but the whole is present, while no span of time is present all at once? And that all past time is driven on by time to come, and all time to come follows on the past, and all past and future is created and flows out of what is always present? Who will hold the heart of man so that it may stand still and see how eternity, always standing still, neither past nor to come, utters times past and times to come? Can my hand do this, or the hand of my mouth by speech bring about so great a thing?

See how I answer the man who asks, "What was God doing before he made heaven and earth?" I do not answer as somebody is said to have answered flippantly, dodging the pressure of the question: "He was preparing hell for people who pry into mysteries." Answering an enquiry is one thing; making fun of the enquirer is another. So that is not my answer. I would rather answer "I do not know" about what I do not know, than answer in a way that raises a laugh against a man who asks deep questions and wins praise for a man who gives false ones. But I say that You, our God, are the creator of every creature; and if by the name "heaven and earth" every creature is meant, then I say boldly that before God made heaven and earth he did not make anything. For if he made something, what did he make except a creature? And I wish I knew everything I want to know for my own good as well as I know that no creature was made before any creature was made.

But if some roving brain ranges over the images of times gone by, and is amazed that You, the God almighty and all-creating and all-sustaining, the maker of heaven and earth, held back from so great a work for countless ages before You would make it, let him wake up and consider that he is amazed at illusions. For where could countless ages have passed that You did not make, You who are the author and creator of all ages? Or what times could there be that were not made by You? Or how could they pass, if they never existed? So since You are the creator of all times, if there was any time before You made heaven and earth, why do they say You gave up working? That very time You made, and times could not pass before You made those times. But if there was no time before heaven and earth, why is it asked what You were doing then? For there was no "then" when there was no time.

Nor do You come before time by time; otherwise You would not come before all times. You come before all past things by the loftiness of an ever-present eternity, and You go beyond all future things because they are future, and when they come they will be past; but You are the same, and Your years do not run out. Your years neither come nor go, while ours both come and go, so that they may all come. Your years stand together, because they do stand; departing years are not pushed out by arriving ones, since they do not pass away — whereas ours will all exist only when they no longer exist. Your years are a single day; and Your day is not a daily one but a Today, seeing that Your Today does not give way to a tomorrow, since neither does it take the place of a yesterday. Your Today is eternity; and therefore You fathered the co-eternal one, to whom You said, Today I have begotten You. You have made all things; and before all times You are, and there was no time in which there was no time.

So there was no time at which You had not made anything, since You made time itself. And no times are co-eternal with You, because You remain; and if they remained, they would not be times.

For what is time? Who can readily and briefly explain it? Who can even take it in in thought, so as to say a word about it? And yet what do we mention more familiarly and knowingly in conversation than time? And we understand it when we speak of it, and understand it when we hear somebody else speak of it. What is time, then? If no one asks me, I know; if I want to explain it to someone who asks, I do not know. And yet I say boldly that I know this much: that if nothing passed away there would be no past time, and if nothing were coming there would be no future time, and if nothing existed there would be no present time. So how do those two times exist, the past and the future, seeing that the past now is not and the future is not yet? And as for the present, if it were always present and never passed into the past, it would not be time at all but eternity. So if present time only comes into existence, if it is to be time, by passing into the past, how can we say that it exists either — when the cause of its existing is that it shall not exist? So that we cannot truly say that time exists, except because it is tending not to exist.

And yet we say "a long time" and "a short time" — and still, only of past or future time. A long time past we call, for instance, a hundred years ago; and a long time to come, a hundred years hence. And a short time past we call, say, ten days ago; and a short time to come, ten days hence. But in what sense is something long or short that does not exist? For the past is not now, and the future is not yet. So let us not say "it is long", but of the past, "it was long", and of the future, "it will be long."

Lord, my light, will Your truth not laugh at man here too? For that past time which was long: was it long when it was already past, or when it was still present? It could be long when there was something there that could be long; but once past it no longer existed, and so what did not exist at all could not be long either. So let us not say, "past time was long", since we shall not find anything that was long, seeing that once it was past it no longer existed. Let us say instead, "that present time was long" — because when it was present it was long. It had not yet passed away into non-existence, and so there was something there that could be long; but once it was past, what ceased to exist ceased to be long as well.

Let us see, then, soul of man, whether present time can be long; for it is given to you to feel and to measure lengths of time. What will you answer me? Are a hundred years, when present, a long time? Look first at whether a hundred years can be present. For if the first of these years is now running, it is present, but the other ninety-nine are to come and so are not yet; and if the second year is running, one is now past, another present, and the rest to come. And so if we take any year in the middle of the hundred to be present, all the ones before it are past and all the ones after it are to come; so a hundred years cannot be present. But see at least whether the one now running is present. For if the current month is its first, the rest are to come; if the second, the first is already past and the rest are not yet. So the year now running is not present either; and if it is not present as a whole, then the year is not present. For twelve months make a year, of which whichever one is running is present, while the rest are past or to come. Though the current month is not present either, but only one day of it — the rest being to come if it is the first, past if the last, and if any in the middle, then part past and part to come.

See how the present time, which alone we found could be called long, is cut down to the length of barely one day. But let us examine that too, since not even one day is present as a whole. It is made up of twenty-four hours of night and day, of which the first has the rest to come, and the last has them past, and any in the middle has those before it past and those behind it to come. And that one hour passes away in flying particles. Whatever of it has flown away is past; whatever remains is to come. If an instant of time can be conceived that cannot be divided into the smallest particles of moments, that alone is what may be called present. And it flies with such speed from future into past that it cannot be extended by the slightest pause; for if it could, it would be divided into past and future. The present has no extension.

So where is the time we can call long? Is it the future? Of the future we do not say, "it is long", because it does not yet exist so as to be long; we say, "it will be long". So when will it be? If even then, while it is still to come, it will not be long — since what can be long does not yet exist — and if it will therefore be long only when, out of a future that does not yet exist, it begins to exist and has become present, so that there is something there that can be long: then present time cries out, in the words above, that it cannot be long.

And yet, Lord, we do perceive intervals of time and compare them, and say that some are shorter and others longer. We even measure how much longer or shorter this time is than that, and answer, "This is double, or triple"; or, "That is single, or just the same as this." But we measure times as they pass, by perceiving them. As for the past, which no longer exists, or the future, which does not exist yet, who can measure them? Unless a man dares to say that what does not exist can be measured. So when time is passing it can be perceived and measured; but once past it cannot, because it does not exist.

I am asking, Father, not asserting: my God, direct me and guide me. Who will tell me that there are not three times, past, present and future, as we learned as boys and taught boys ourselves — but only the present, because the other two do not exist? Or do they exist too, and when it becomes present out of the future, does it come out of some hidden place, and when it retreats from present into past, does it go into one? For where did the men who foretold things to come see them, if they did not yet exist? What does not exist cannot be seen. And men who relate things past could not relate them if they did not make them out in their minds; and if those things did not exist, they could not be made out at all. So things past and things to come do exist.

Let me look further, Lord. My hope, do not let my purpose be thrown into confusion. If times past and to come exist, I want to know where they are. And even if I cannot manage that, I do know that wherever they are, they are not there as future or as past, but as present. For if they are future there too, they are not there yet; and if they are past there too, they are no longer there. So wherever whatever exists is, it is only as present. Although when past events are related, it is not the things themselves that are drawn out of the memory, which are past, but words conceived from the images of the things, which as they passed left their traces in the mind through the senses. My childhood, which no longer exists, is in past time, which no longer exists; but now, when I recall its image and describe it, I am looking at it in the present, because it is still in my memory. Whether there is a similar cause for foretelling things to come — so that images of things which do not yet exist may be perceived beforehand, already existing — I confess, my God, I do not know. But this I do know: that we generally think ahead about our future actions, and that this forethinking is present, while the action we are thinking ahead about does not yet exist, because it is to come. And when we have set about it and begun to do what we were thinking of, then the action will exist, because then it is no longer future but present.

Whichever way, then, this secret pre-perceiving of things to come works, only what exists can be seen. And what now exists is not future but present. So when things to come are said to be seen, it is not the things themselves that are seen, which do not yet exist — that is, which are to be — but perhaps their causes or their signs, which already exist. So they are not future but present to those who now see the thing from which the future is foreconceived in the mind and foretold. And these foreconceptions exist now; and those who foretell those things are looking at conceptions present in front of them. Let the great variety of things supply me an example. I look at the dawn; I predict that the sun is about to rise. What I am looking at is present; what I am signalling ahead is to come — not the sun, which already exists, but the sunrise, which does not exist yet. And yet if I did not picture the sunrise itself in my mind, as I am doing now while I speak of it, I could not foretell it. But the dawn light I make out in the sky is not the sunrise, though it comes before it; and neither is that picture in my mind. Those two are seen as present now, so that the other, which is to be, may be foretold. So future things do not yet exist; and if they do not yet exist, they do not exist; and if they do not exist, they cannot be seen. But they can be foretold from present things, which already exist and are seen.

You then, ruler of Your creation: by what means do You teach souls things to come? For You taught Your prophets. By what means do You, to whom nothing is to come, teach things to come — or rather, teach present things about the future? For what does not exist cannot be taught. This road is too far beyond my sight; it is too mighty for me, I cannot reach it. But from You I can, when You grant it, sweet light of my hidden eyes.

What is clear and plain now is that neither things to come nor things past exist. Nor is it properly said that there are three times, past, present and to come; but perhaps it might properly be said that there are three times: a present of things past, a present of things present, and a present of things future. For these three do exist in some fashion in the soul, and I do not see them anywhere else: the present of things past is memory; the present of things present is sight; the present of things future is expectation. If we may put it that way, then I see three times, and I admit there are three. Let it also be said that there are three times, past, present and to come, in our incorrect way of speaking. I do not object or argue or find fault, so long as what is said is understood — that neither what is to be exists now, nor what is past. There are very few things we say properly; most things we say improperly, and still what is meant is understood.

I said just now that we measure times as they pass, so as to be able to say that this time is twice as long as that, or that this is just the same as that, and so with any other measurable parts of time. So, as I said, we measure times as they pass. And if anyone asked me how I know, I might answer: I know that we do measure, and that we cannot measure things that do not exist, and that things past and to come do not exist. But how do we measure present time, seeing it has no extension? It is measured while it is passing, and when it has passed it is not measured, since there will be nothing there to measure. But where does it pass from, and by what road, and where to, while it is being measured? Where from, but from the future? By what road, but through the present? Where to, but into the past? So from what does not yet exist, through what has no extension, into what no longer exists. And yet what are we measuring, if not time in some extension? For we do not say single and double and triple and equal, or anything else of the sort that we say about time, except of extensions of time. So in what extension do we measure time as it passes? In the future, which it passes from? But we do not measure what does not yet exist. In the present, which it passes through? But an extension of no size we do not measure. In the past, which it passes into? But we do not measure what no longer exists either.

My soul is on fire to know this most intricate riddle. Do not shut it away, Lord my God, good Father; through Christ I beg You, do not shut these familiar yet hidden things away from my longing, so that it is stopped from getting into them; let them dawn through Your enlightening mercy, Lord. Whom shall I ask about these things? And to whom shall I confess my ignorance more usefully than to You, to whom these studies of mine, so violently kindled towards Your Scriptures, are no trouble? Give me what I love; for I do love, and even that You gave me. Give, Father, who truly know how to give good gifts to Your children. Give, because I have taken it on myself to know, and there is trouble in front of me until You open it up. By Christ I beg You, in his name, holy of holies, let nobody disturb me. For I believed, and therefore I speak. This is my hope; this is what I live for, that I may contemplate the delights of the Lord. You have made my days grow old, and they pass away, and how, I do not know. And we talk about time and time, times and times: "How long since he said this?" "How long since he did that?" "How long since I saw that?" "This syllable takes twice the time of that short one." These are the words we speak and hear, and are understood and understand. They are utterly obvious and ordinary, and the very same things are also far too deeply hidden, and their discovery would be something new.

I once heard a learned man say that time is constituted by the motions of the sun, the moon and the stars, and I did not agree. Why should the motions of all bodies not be times just as well? Or if the lights of heaven stopped and a potter's wheel went round, would there be no time for us to measure those turnings by, and to say either that it moved with equal intervals, or that if it turned sometimes slower and sometimes faster, some turns were longer and others shorter? Or, while we said this, would we not be speaking in time ourselves? Or would there be in our words some short syllables and some long, except because these sounded in a shorter time and those in a longer? God, grant men to see in a small thing the principles common to things great and small. The stars and lights of heaven are also for signs and for seasons and for years and for days; they are. And yet I would not say that the going round of that wooden wheel was a day, nor would he say that it was therefore not time.

I want to know the force and nature of time, by which we measure the motions of bodies and say, for example, that this motion is twice as long as that. For I ask this. "Day" means not only the sun's stay above the earth, according to which day is one thing and night another, but also its whole circuit from east round to east again, according to which we say "so many days have passed", the night being included when we say "so many days", and the nights not counted separately. Seeing, then, that a day is completed by the motion of the sun and by his circuit from east round to east, I ask: does the motion alone make the day, or the length of time in which the motion is completed, or both? If the first, then we should have a day even if the sun finished his course in as small a space of time as an hour. If the second, then it would not make a day if between one sunrise and the next there were only as short a stay as an hour; the sun would have to go round twenty-four times to complete one day. If both, then neither could be called a day: not if the sun ran his whole round in the space of an hour, and not if, while the sun stood still, as much time went by as the sun usually takes to run his whole course from morning to morning.

So I shall not now ask what it is that is called a day, but what time is — the time by which, measuring the circuit of the sun, we should say that it was finished in half the time it usually takes, if it were finished in as small a space as twelve hours; and comparing the two, should call this a single time and that a double one, even supposing the sun to run his round from east to east sometimes in the single and sometimes in the double time. So let nobody tell me that the motions of the heavenly bodies constitute times; because when at one man's prayer the sun stood still until he could finish his victorious battle, the sun stood still and time went on. For that battle was fought and finished in its own allotted space of time. So I perceive time to be a kind of extension. But do I perceive it, or only seem to perceive it? You, light and truth, will show me.

Do You tell me to agree, if somebody defines time as "the motion of a body"? You do not. That no body is moved except in time, I hear; that You say. But that the motion of a body is time, I do not hear; You do not say it. For when a body is moved, I measure by time how long it moves, from the moment it began to move until it stopped. And if I did not see when it began, and it goes on moving so that I do not see when it ends, I cannot measure it — except perhaps from the moment I began looking until I stop. And if I look a long while, I can only say it was a long time, and not how long, because when we say "how long" we do it by comparison: "this is as long as that", or "twice as long as that", or the like. But when we can mark the distances of the places the moving body goes from and to, or of its parts if it turns as on a lathe, then we can say precisely in how much time the motion of that body, or of its part, from this place to that, was completed. So since the motion of a body is one thing, and what we measure its length by is another, who cannot see which of the two is rather to be called time? And if a body is sometimes moving and sometimes standing still, then we measure by time not only its motion but its standing still as well; and we say, "It stood still as long as it moved", or "It stood still twice or three times as long as it moved", or any other proportion our measuring has either established or guessed at — more or less, as we usually say. So time is not the motion of a body.

And I confess to You, Lord, that I still do not know what time is; and again I confess to You, Lord, that I know I am saying this in time, and that having spoken about time for a long while, that very "long while" is not long except by a lapse of time. So how do I know this, when I do not know what time is? Or is it perhaps that I do not know how to express what I do know? How wretched I am, that I do not even know what I do not know. Look, my God, I am not lying in front of You; my heart is as I speak. You will light my candle; You, Lord my God, will lighten my darkness.

Does my soul not most truly confess to You that I do measure times? So do I measure, my God, and not know what I am measuring? I measure the motion of a body by time; and do I not measure the time itself? Or could I really measure the motion of a body — how long it went on, and in how long a stretch it could get from this place to that — without measuring the time it moved in? And this time itself, how do I measure it? Do we measure a longer time by a shorter one, as we measure the length of a beam by the length of a forearm? For that is how we seem to measure the length of a long syllable by the length of a short one, and to say that this is double that. So we measure the lengths of stanzas by the lengths of the lines, and the lengths of the lines by the lengths of the feet, and the lengths of the feet by the lengths of the syllables, and the lengths of the long syllables by the lengths of the short ones — not measuring by the pages, since then we would be measuring spaces and not times, but as we utter the words and they pass by, and we say, "It is a long stanza, being made of so many lines; long lines, since they consist of so many feet; long feet, since they are drawn out through so many syllables; a long syllable, since it is double a short one." But we do not get any certain measure of time this way either, since it may be that a shorter line, pronounced more fully, takes up more time than a longer one pronounced in a hurry. And the same for a line, a foot, a syllable.

So it seemed to me that time is nothing but a stretching out — of what, I do not know; and I wonder whether it is not of the mind itself. For what, I ask You, my God, am I measuring when I say either indefinitely, "This is a longer time than that", or definitely, "This is double that"? That I am measuring time I know; and yet I am not measuring future time, since it does not yet exist; nor present time, since it is not stretched out over any extension; nor past time, since it no longer exists. So what am I measuring? Times as they pass, and not times past? That is what I said.

Courage, my mind, and push on hard. God is our helper; he made us, and not we ourselves. Push on to where truth begins to dawn. Suppose the voice of a body begins to sound, and does sound, and goes on sounding; and then it stops. There is silence now, and that voice is past and is no longer a voice. Before it sounded it was to come, and could not be measured because it did not yet exist; and now it cannot be measured, because it no longer exists. So it could be measured while it was sounding, because then there was something there that could be measured. But even then it was not standing still; it was passing on and passing away. Could it be measured all the more for that? For while it was passing it was being stretched over some extension of time, so that it could be measured — since the present has no extension. So if it could be measured then, suppose another voice has begun to sound and is still sounding in one continuous tenor without any break. Let us measure it while it sounds; since once it has stopped sounding it will be past and there will be nothing left to measure. Let us measure it, then, and say how much it is. But it is still sounding, and it cannot be measured except from the moment it began to the moment it stopped; for the interval between is exactly what we measure, namely from some beginning to some end. So a voice that has not yet ended cannot be measured, so as to say how long or short it is, or to call it equal to another, or double a single one, or anything of the kind. But when it has ended, it no longer exists. How can it be measured then? And yet we do measure times — and not the times that do not yet exist, nor those that no longer exist, nor those that are not stretched out by any duration, nor those that have no boundaries. So we measure neither times to come, nor times past, nor present times, nor passing times; and yet we do measure times.

Deus Creator omnium — this line of eight syllables alternates short and long. The four short ones, the first, third, fifth and seventh, are single in relation to the four long ones, the second, fourth, sixth and eighth. Each of these has twice the time of each of those. I pronounce them, I report on them, and I find it so, as anyone's plain sense perceives. By plain sense, then, I measure a long syllable by a short one, and I sensibly find it to have twice as much. But when one sounds after the other, if the first is short and the second long, how am I to hold on to the short one, and how, in measuring, am I to apply it to the long one so as to find that this has twice as much — seeing the long one does not begin to sound until the short one stops? And do I measure that very long syllable as present, when I do not measure it until it has ended? And its ending is its passing away. So what is it that I am measuring? Where is the short syllable I measure with? Where is the long one I measure? Both have sounded, have flown, have passed away, are no more. And yet I measure, and answer confidently, so far as one can rely on a practised sense, that this syllable is single and that one double in extension of time. And I could not do this unless they were already past and finished. So it is not the syllables themselves, which no longer exist, that I measure, but something in my memory, which stays fixed there.

It is in you, my mind, that I measure times. Do not interrupt me — that is, do not interrupt yourself with the tumult of your own impressions. In you I measure times. The impression that passing things make in you stays behind when they are gone; and it is this, still present, that I measure, not the things that passed by to make the impression. This is what I measure when I measure times. So either this is time, or I do not measure times at all.

And what about when we measure silence, and say that this silence lasted as long as that voice did? Do we not stretch out our thought to the measure of a voice, as if it were sounding, so as to be able to report on the intervals of silence in a given space of time? For though the voice and the tongue are still, in thought we go over poems and lines and any other discourse, or the dimensions of motions, and report on their lengths of time, how much this is compared with that, exactly as if we were pronouncing them aloud. If a man wanted to utter a prolonged sound, and had settled in his thought how long it should be, he has already gone through a stretch of time in silence, and, committing it to memory, he begins to utter the sound, which goes on until it is brought to the end he had proposed. Or rather, it has sounded and will sound: the part of it that is finished has sounded, and the rest will sound. And so it goes on, until the present attention carries the future over into the past, the past growing by the shrinking of the future, until by the using-up of the future the whole is past.

But how is that future shrunk or used up, when it does not yet exist? And how is that past increased, when it no longer exists — except that in the mind that does all this there are three things going on? For it expects, and it attends, and it remembers; so that what it expects passes, through what it attends to, into what it remembers. Who then denies that things to come do not yet exist? And yet there is in the mind an expectation of things to come. And who denies that past things no longer exist? And yet there is still in the mind a memory of things past. And who denies that present time has no extension, since it passes away in a moment? And yet our attention lasts, and through it what will be present goes on to become absent. So it is not future time that is long, since it does not yet exist; a long future is a long expectation of the future. And it is not past time that is long, since it no longer exists; a long past is a long memory of the past.

Suppose I am about to recite a psalm I know. Before I begin, my expectation is stretched over the whole of it; but once I have begun, however much of it I peel off into the past is stretched along my memory. So the life of this action of mine is divided between my memory, as to what I have recited, and my expectation, as to what I am about to recite. And attention is present with me, so that through it what was future may be carried over and become past.
