And the more this is done again and again, the more the expectation is shortened and the memory lengthened, until the whole expectation is used up, when the whole action is finished and has passed into memory. And what happens in the whole psalm happens in each separate part of it and each separate syllable. The same holds in that longer action of which this psalm may be a part; the same holds in the whole life of a man, of which all the man's actions are parts; the same holds through the whole age of the sons of men, of which all men's lives are parts.

But because Your loving-kindness is better than all lives, look: my life is nothing but a being pulled apart, and Your right hand held me up — in my Lord, the Son of man, the mediator between You, the one, and us, the many; many too through our many distractions among many things. So that through him I might take hold of the one in whom I have been taken hold of, and be gathered up again out of my old way of life to follow the one: forgetting what lies behind, and not pulled apart but stretched out — not towards things that shall be and shall pass away, but towards the things that lie ahead; not distractedly but intently, following on for the prize of my heavenly calling, where I may hear the voice of Your praise and contemplate Your delights, which neither come nor pass away. But now my years are spent in mourning. And You, Lord, are my comfort, my everlasting Father; and I have been split up among times whose order I do not know; and my thoughts, the very inmost parts of my soul, are torn and mangled by tumultuous varieties, until I flow together into You, purified and melted down by the fire of Your love.

And now I shall stand and grow firm in You, in my mould, which is Your truth. And I shall not put up with the questions of people who, by a diseased craving that is its own punishment, thirst for more than they can hold, and say, "What was God doing before he made heaven and earth?" Or, "How did it come into his mind to make anything, having never made anything before?" Grant them, Lord, to think carefully about what they are saying, and to discover that "never" cannot be said where there is no time. So when he is said "never to have made", what is that but to say he made it "at no time"? So let them see that time cannot exist without created being, and stop talking this nonsense. May they also be stretched out towards the things that lie ahead, and understand that You are before all times, the eternal creator of all times, and that no times are co-eternal with You, and no creature, even if there is some creature before all times.

Lord my God, what a depth is that recess of Your mysteries, and how far the consequences of my sins have thrown me from it! Heal my eyes, so that I may share the joy of Your light. Certainly, if there were a mind gifted with such vast knowledge and foreknowledge as to know everything past and to come the way I know a psalm I know well, that mind would be extraordinarily wonderful and frighteningly amazing: nothing past and nothing to come in later ages would be any more hidden from it than, when I sang that psalm, it was hidden from me what and how much of it had gone by from the beginning, and what and how much was left to the end. But far be it, You who are the creator of the universe, the creator of souls and bodies — far be it that You should know all things past and to come in any such way. Far, far more wonderfully and far more mysteriously do You know them. For the feelings of a man singing something he knows, or listening to a song he knows well, are varied and his senses divided by his expectation of the words to come and his memory of the ones past; and nothing of the kind happens to You, unchangeably eternal, that is, the eternal creator of minds. So just as You in the beginning knew heaven and earth without any variation in Your knowledge, so in the beginning You made heaven and earth without any division in Your action. Whoever understands this, let him confess to You; and whoever does not understand, let him confess to You. How high You are, and the humble in heart are Your dwelling place; for You raise up those who are bowed down, and they do not fall, since You are their raising up.

Book Twelve: Heaven and Earth

My heart, Lord, touched by the words of Your holy Scripture, is very busy in the middle of this poverty of my life. And that is why, most of the time, the poverty of human understanding is rich in words: because enquiring has more to say than discovering, and asking takes longer than getting, and our hand that knocks has more work to do than our hand that receives. We hold the promise; who shall cancel it? If God is for us, who can be against us? Ask, and you shall have; seek, and you shall find; knock, and it shall be opened to you. For everyone who asks receives, and he who seeks finds, and to him who knocks it shall be opened. These are Your own promises; and who need be afraid of being cheated, when it is the truth that promises?

The lowliness of my tongue confesses to Your highness that You made heaven and earth: this sky I see, and this earth I walk on, from which comes the earth I carry about with me. You made it. But where is that heaven of heavens, Lord, which we hear of in the words of the psalm: The heaven of heavens belongs to the Lord, but the earth he has given to the children of men? Where is that heaven which we do not see, and to which everything we do see is earth? For this bodily whole, not being wholly everywhere, has received its share of beauty in these lower parts, of which the lowest is this earth of ours; but to that heaven of heavens, even the sky over our earth is only earth. Indeed both these great bodies may not absurdly be called earth in relation to that unknown heaven, which is the Lord's and not the sons' of men.

And this earth was invisible and without form, and there was some depth of an abyss over which there was no light, because it had no shape. So You commanded it to be written that darkness was over the face of the deep. And what is that but the absence of light? For if there had been light, where would it have been, except above everything, lighting it? So where light was not, what was the presence of darkness but the absence of light? Darkness was over it, then, because light was not over it, as where there is no sound there is silence. And what is it to have silence there, except to have no sound there? Have You not taught this soul that confesses to You, Lord? Have You not taught me, Lord, that before You formed and diversified this formless matter there was nothing — no colour, no shape, no body, no spirit? And yet not altogether nothing; for there was a certain formlessness, without any beauty.

Then what should it be called, so that it might be conveyed in some measure to duller minds, except by some ordinary word? And what in all the world can be found nearer to an absolute formlessness than earth and the deep? Occupying the lowest level, they are less beautiful than the higher parts, which are all transparent and shining. So why should I not conceive the formlessness of matter — which You had created without beauty, to make this beautiful world out of — to be suitably suggested to men by the name of earth invisible and without form?

So that when thought looks for what the senses can conceive under this, and says to itself, "It is no intelligible form, like life or justice, since it is the matter of bodies; and it is no object of the senses, since being invisible and without form there was nothing in it for sight or sense" — while a man's thought says this to itself, it may be trying either to know it by being ignorant of it, or to be ignorant of it by knowing it.

But if I wanted, Lord, to confess to You with my tongue and my pen everything You have taught me about that matter — whose name I had heard before without understanding it, when people who did not understand it told me about it, so that I conceived of it as having countless different forms, and therefore did not conceive of it at all, my mind tossing up and down foul and horrible "forms" out of all order, but "forms" all the same; and I called it formless not because it lacked all form but because it had the kind my mind would turn away from if it were presented, as strange and jarring, and human weakness would be troubled by. And still what I conceived was formless not by being deprived of all form but in comparison with more beautiful forms; and true reason did persuade me that I had to strip it entirely of every last remnant of form, if I wanted to conceive matter absolutely without form. And I could not. It was easier for me to imagine that a thing deprived of all form did not exist at all than to conceive of something between form and nothing — neither formed nor nothing, a formless almost-nothing. So my mind gave up questioning my spirit about it, my spirit being full of images of formed bodies and changing and varying them at will. And I turned to bodies themselves and looked more deeply into their changeableness, by which they stop being what they were and begin to be what they were not; and I suspected that this very shifting from form to form takes place through a certain formless state, and not through sheer nothing. But I longed to know this, not merely to suspect it. If my voice and my pen were to confess to You everything, all the knots You untied for me in this question, what reader could hold out to take it all in? And my heart shall not for all that stop honouring You, and singing praise, for the things it is not able to express.

For the changeableness of changeable things is itself capable of all the forms those changeable things get changed into. And what is this changeableness? Is it soul? Is it body? Is it whatever it is that constitutes soul or body? If one could say "a nothing-something", an "is-that-is-not", I would say that was it. And yet in some way it did already exist, in being capable of receiving these visible and composite shapes.

But where did it get this degree of being from, if not from You, from whom all things come in so far as they are? But so much the further from You the more unlike You they are — for it is not a farness of place. So You, Lord, who are not one thing in one place and something else in another, but the selfsame, and the selfsame, and the selfsame, holy, holy, holy, Lord God Almighty, did in the beginning, which is from You, in Your wisdom, which was born of Your own substance, create something, and that out of nothing. For You created heaven and earth not out of Yourself, since then they would have been equal to Your only-begotten Son and thereby to You as well; and there is no way it would be right for anything to be equal to You that was not from You. And there was nothing else besides You out of which You might create them, God, one Trinity and threefold unity. So it was out of nothing that You created heaven and earth: a great thing and a small thing; for You are almighty and good, to make everything good, both the great sky and the tiny earth. You existed, and there was nothing besides out of which You created heaven and earth: two sorts of thing, one near to You and the other near to nothing; one which You alone should be above, and one which nothing should be below.

But that heaven of heavens was for Yourself, Lord. And the earth which You gave to the sons of men to see and touch was not the earth we now see and touch. For it was invisible and without form, and there was a deep over which there was no light — or, darkness was above the deep, that is, more than in the deep. Because this deep of waters, visible now, has even in its depths a light suited to its nature, perceptible in some degree to the fishes and creeping things at the bottom. But that whole deep was almost nothing, because up to that point it was altogether without form; and yet there was already something there that could be formed. For You, Lord, made the world out of a matter without form, which You made out of nothing as next to nothing, so as to make out of it the great things that we sons of men marvel at.

For this bodily sky is truly wonderful; and about that firmament between water and water, on the second day, after the creation of light, You said, Let it be made, and it was made. And that firmament You called heaven — the sky, that is, over this earth and sea, which You made on the third day by giving a visible shape to the formless matter You had made before all days. For You had already made a heaven before all days; but that was the heaven of this heaven, since in the beginning You had made heaven and earth. But this earth which You made was formless matter, because it was invisible and without form, and darkness was over the deep. And out of this invisible and formless earth, out of this formlessness, this almost-nothing, You could make all these things that this changeable world consists of, without ever consisting steadily; whose very changeableness shows itself in this, that times can be observed and counted in it. For times are made by the alterations of things, as the shapes are varied and turned whose matter is the invisible earth I have spoken of.

And so the Spirit, the teacher of Your servant, when it tells us that in the beginning You created heaven and earth, says nothing of times, nothing of days. For truly that heaven of heavens which You created in the beginning is some intelligent creature which, though in no way co-eternal with You, the Trinity, nevertheless shares in Your eternity, and through the sweetness of that most happy contemplation of You strongly restrains its own changeableness; and without any fall since its first creation, holding close to You, is set beyond all the rolling change of times. And that very formlessness of the earth, invisible and without form, is not counted among the days either. For where there is no shape and no order, nothing comes or goes; and where that is not, plainly there are no days, and no succession of stretches of time.

Let the light, the truth, the light of my heart and not my own darkness, speak to me. I fell away into that darkness and was blacked out; but even from there, even from there I loved You. I went astray and remembered You. I heard Your voice behind me calling me back, and hardly heard it through the uproar of the enemies of peace. And now, look, I am coming back, distressed and panting after Your fountain. Let nobody stop me. Of this I shall drink, and so live. Let me not be my own life; from myself I lived badly; I was death to myself; and I come alive again in You. Speak to me; talk with me. I have believed Your books, and their words are utterly full of mystery.

You have already told me with a strong voice, Lord, in my inner ear, that You are eternal, You who alone have immortality; since You cannot be changed in shape or in motion, and Your will is not altered by times — seeing that no will that varies is immortal. This is clear to me in Your sight; and let it be made clearer and clearer to me, I beg You, and in the making clear of it, let me soberly stay under Your wings. You have also told me with a strong voice, Lord, in my inner ear, that You have made all the natures and substances which are not what You are and yet exist; and that the only thing that is not from You is what does not exist; and the movement of the will away from You who are, towards what exists in a lesser degree, because such a movement is transgression and sin; and that nobody's sin either harms You or disturbs the order of Your rule, first or last. This is clear to me in Your sight; and let it be made clearer and clearer to me, I beg You; and in the making clear of it, let me soberly stay under Your wings.

You have also told me with a strong voice, in my inner ear, that neither is that creature co-eternal with You whose happiness You alone are, and which with the most persevering purity, drawing its nourishment from You, nowhere and at no time puts forth its natural changeableness; and which, with You always present to it, and keeping itself towards You with its whole love, having no future to expect and carrying nothing it remembers into the past, is neither altered by any change nor pulled apart into times. Blessed creature, if such there is, for holding close to Your blessedness; blessed in You, its eternal inhabitant and its enlightener! And I cannot find a name I would sooner give the heaven of heavens which is the Lord's than Your house — which contemplates Your delights without any lapse into going out to something else; one pure mind, most harmoniously one, by that settled state of peace of the holy spirits who are citizens of Your city in heavenly places, far above the heavenly places we can see.

By this a soul whose pilgrimage has been made long and far away may understand, if she now thirsts for You, if her tears have now become her bread while they say to her every day, Where is your God? — if she now asks one thing of You and wants it, that she may live in Your house all the days of her life. And what is her life but You? And what are Your days but Your eternity, like Your years which do not run out, because You are always the same? By this, then, may the soul that is able understand how far You are above all times, eternal — seeing that Your house, which at no time went off into a far country, though it is not co-eternal with You, yet by continually and unfailingly holding close to You suffers no changeableness of times. This is clear to me in Your sight; and let it be made clearer and clearer to me, I beg You; and in the making clear of it, let me soberly stay under Your wings.

There is, I do not know what, a kind of formlessness in the changes of these last and lowest creatures. And who will tell me — unless it is somebody who through the emptiness of his own heart wanders and tosses himself about among his own fancies — who but such a man would tell me that if every shape were so worn away and used up that there remained only that formlessness through which a thing was changed and turned from one shape into another, that this could produce the succession of times? Plainly it could not; because without a variety of motions there are no times, and there is no variety where there is no shape.

Considering these things, as far as You give me to, my God, as far as You stir me up to knock, and as far as You open to me when I knock, I find that You have made two things not within the compass of time, neither of which is co-eternal with You. One is so formed that without any pause in its contemplation and without any interval of change, though changeable, yet unchanged, it may thoroughly enjoy Your eternity and unchangeableness. The other was so formless that it had nothing in it that could be changed from one form to another, of motion or of rest, so as to become subject to time. But You did not leave this formless, because before all days, in the beginning, You created heaven and earth: the two things I have been speaking of. But the earth was invisible and without form, and darkness was over the deep. In these words the formlessness is conveyed to us — so that minds unable to conceive an utter absence of all form without arriving at nothing may be drawn on by degrees — out of which another heaven might be created, along with a visible and well-formed earth; and the waters variously ordered; and whatever else in the forming of the world is recorded to have been created, and created not without days; and that as being of such a nature that the successive changes of times could take place in them, since they are subject to appointed alterations of motions and forms.

This, then, is what I understand, my God, when I hear Your Scripture saying, In the beginning God made heaven and earth, and the earth was invisible and without form, and darkness was over the deep, and not mentioning what day You created them. This is what I understand. It is because of the heaven of heavens — that intelligent heaven, whose intelligences know everything at once, not in part, not darkly, not through a glass, but as a whole, plainly, face to face; not this thing now and that thing next, but, as I said, knowing everything at once, without any succession of times — and because of the earth invisible and without form, which is also without any succession of times, the succession that gives us "this thing now, that thing next", since where there is no form there is no distinction of things. It is on account of these two, then, a primal formed thing and a primal formless thing — the one heaven, but the heaven of heaven; the other earth, but the earth invisible and without form — it is because of these two that I understand Your Scripture to have said, without mentioning days, In the beginning God created heaven and earth. For it immediately added which earth it was talking about; and also, in that the firmament is recorded as created on the second day and called heaven, it conveys to us which heaven it had spoken of before, without mentioning days.

What a wondrous depth Your words have! Their surface is in front of us, inviting little children; and yet they are a wondrous depth, my God, a wondrous depth! It is awesome to look into: an awe of honour and a trembling of love. Its enemies I hate violently; if only You would kill them with Your two-edged sword, so that they might stop being enemies to it — for that is the way I love to see them killed to themselves, that they may live to You. But there are others who are not fault-finders but admirers of the book of Genesis. "The Spirit of God," they say, "who wrote these things through his servant Moses, would not have wanted these words understood in that way. He would not have wanted it understood as you say, but otherwise, as we say." To them, with You, God of all, as judge, I answer this.

"Will you say it is false, what truth tells me with a strong voice in my inner ear about the eternity of the creator: that his substance is in no way changed by time, and his will is not separate from his substance? So that he does not will one thing now and another next, but once and at once and always wills everything that he wills; not again and again, and not now this and now that; and does not afterwards will what he did not will before, or stop willing what he willed before, because such a will is changeable, and nothing changeable is eternal — and our God is eternal. And again, what he tells me in my inner ear: the expectation of things to come becomes sight when they have come, and this same sight becomes memory when they are past. Now all thought that varies like this is changeable; and nothing changeable is eternal — and our God is eternal." These things I infer and put together, and find that my God, the eternal God, has not made any creature by any new act of will, and that his knowledge admits nothing transitory. "What will you say to that, objectors? Are these things false?" "No," they say. "What then? Is it false that every nature already formed, and every matter capable of form, exists only from him who is supremely good, because he supremely is?" "We do not deny that either," they say. "What then? Do you deny that there is a certain lofty creature, holding to the true and truly eternal God with so chaste a love that although it is not co-eternal with him it is still not detached from him, and does not dissolve into the variety and succession of times, but rests in the truest contemplation of him alone?" Because You, God, show Yourself to the man who loves You as much as You command, and are enough for him; and so he does not turn away from You, or towards himself. This is the house of God, not made of earthly stuff, and not of any heavenly bulk either, but spiritual, and a partaker in Your eternity, because it never lapses, for ever. For You have made it fast for ever and ever; You have given it a law which it shall not pass. And yet it is not co-eternal with You, God, because it is not without a beginning: it was made.

For although we find no time before it, since wisdom was created before all things — not that wisdom which is altogether equal and co-eternal with You, our God, its Father, and by which all things were created, and in which, as the beginning, You created heaven and earth, but the wisdom that is created, that is, the intelligent nature which by contemplating the light is itself light. For this too, though created, is called wisdom. And as much difference as there is between the light that lights and the light that is lit, so much there is between the wisdom that creates and the wisdom that is created; as between the righteousness that makes righteous and the righteousness that is made by being justified. For we too are called Your righteousness; a certain servant of Yours says so: That we might be made the righteousness of God in him. So since a certain created wisdom was created before all things — the rational and intelligent mind of that chaste city of Yours, our mother which is above and is free and eternal in the heavens (in what heavens, if not in those that praise You, the heaven of heavens? Because this too is the heaven of heavens belonging to the Lord) — although we find no time before it, since what was created before all things also comes before the creature of time, still the eternity of the creator himself is before it, from whom, being created, it took its beginning: not a beginning of time, since time did not exist yet, but of its creation.

So it is from You, our God, in such a way as to be altogether other than You, and not the selfsame. Because although we find no time before it, and not even in it, since it is always fit to look on Your face and is never drawn away from it, and so is not varied by any change — still there is in it a liability to change, from which it would grow dark and cold, except that by a strong love holding close to You, like perpetual noon, it shines and glows from You. House of light and delight! I have loved your beauty, and the place where the glory of my Lord lives, your builder and owner. Let my wandering sigh after you; and I say to him who made you, let him take possession of me too, in you, since he made me as well. I have gone astray like a lost sheep; and yet on the shoulders of my shepherd, your builder, I hope to be carried back to you.

"What do you say to me, objectors, whom I was addressing, who nevertheless believe Moses to have been the holy servant of God and his books the oracles of the Holy Spirit? Is this house of God not eternal in the heavens, in its own measure, though not co-eternal with God — when you look for changes of times in it in vain, because you will not find them? For that to which it is always good to hold fast to God surpasses all extension and all revolving periods of time." "It is," they say. "Then out of everything my heart cried out to my God, when inwardly it heard the voice of his praise — what part of it do you say is false? Is it that the matter was without form, in which, because there was no form, there was no order? But where there was no order there could be no succession of times; and yet this 'almost nothing', in so far as it was not altogether nothing, certainly came from him from whom whatever is comes, in whatever degree it is." "That too," they say, "we do not deny."

With these people I now talk a little in Your presence, my God, who grant that all these things are true which Your truth whispers to my soul. As for those who deny them, let them bark and deafen themselves as much as they like; I shall try to persuade them to be quiet and to open a way in themselves for Your word. And if they refuse and push me away, I beg You, my God, do not be silent to me. Speak truly in my heart, for only You speak that way. And I shall leave them alone, blowing on the dust outside and raising it into their own eyes; and I shall go into my room and sing a song of loves to You there, groaning with groans too deep for words on my journey, and remembering Jerusalem, with my heart lifted up towards it: Jerusalem my country, Jerusalem my mother, and Yourself who rule over it, its enlightener, father, guardian, husband, pure and strong delight and solid joy and all good things beyond words, and all at once, because You are the one sovereign and true good. And I shall not be turned away until You gather everything that I am out of this scattered and disordered state into the peace of that dearest mother of ours, where the first fruits of my spirit already are, and from which I am assured of these things; and until You shape and confirm it for ever, my God, my mercy.

But those who do not call all these truths false, who honour Your holy Scripture as set out by holy Moses, putting it, as we do, at the summit of authority to be followed, and yet contradict me on some point — to them I answer this. Judge for Yourself, our God, between my confessions and these men's contradictions.

For they say, "Even if these things are true, Moses did not mean those two when, by revelation of the Spirit, he said, In the beginning God created heaven and earth. He did not mean by the word heaven that spiritual or intelligent creature which always looks on the face of God, or by the word earth that formless matter." "What then?" "That man of God," they say, "meant what we say; this is what he was declaring by those words." "What?" "By the name of heaven and earth," they say, "he wanted first to signify, in a general and compressed way, all this visible world, so as afterwards, by listing the several days, to lay out in detail and piece by piece all the things which the Holy Spirit was pleased to announce this way. For the people he was speaking to were so rough and unspiritual that he thought them fit to be trusted only with the knowledge of God's visible works." They do agree, however, that the words earth invisible and without form, and that dark deep, out of which it is afterwards shown that all these visible things we know were made and arranged during those days, may not unreasonably be understood of this formless first matter.

But what if somebody else says that this same formlessness and confusion of matter was first conveyed under the name of heaven and earth for this reason: that out of it this visible world was created and completed, with all the natures that appear most obviously in it, and that this world is often called by the name of heaven and earth? And what if another says that invisible and visible nature is not inappropriately called heaven and earth, and that the whole creation which God made in his wisdom, that is, in the beginning, was covered by those two words — but that, since all things are made not of the substance of God but out of nothing (because they are not what God is, and there is a changeable nature in all of them, whether they endure, as the eternal house of God does, or are changed, as the soul and body of man are), therefore the common matter of all things visible and invisible, as yet unformed though capable of form, out of which both heaven and earth were to be created (that is, the invisible and the visible creature, once formed), was given the same names as the earth invisible and without form and the darkness upon the deep — but with this distinction, that by the earth invisible and without form is understood bodily matter before it has been qualified by any form, and by the darkness upon the deep, spiritual matter, before it underwent any restraint on its unlimited fluidity or received any light from wisdom?

There is still room for a man to say, if he likes, that the already completed and formed natures, visible and invisible, are not what is meant by the name of heaven and earth when we read, In the beginning God made heaven and earth; but that the still unformed beginning of things, the stuff apt to receive form and making, was called by those names, because it contained confusedly, not yet distinguished by their qualities and forms, all those things which, now sorted into order, are called heaven and earth, the one being the spiritual and the other the bodily creation.

Having heard and carefully considered all this, I shall not quarrel over words; that is good for nothing but the ruin of the hearers. But the law is good for building up, if a man uses it lawfully, since its end is love out of a pure heart and a good conscience and unfeigned faith. And our master knew well which two commandments he hung all the law and the prophets on. So what harm does it do me, my God, light of my eyes in secret, while I zealously confess these things — since different things may be understood under these words which are nevertheless all true — what harm, I say, does it do me if I think the writer meant something other than somebody else thinks he meant? All of us readers are certainly trying to trace out and understand the meaning of the man we are reading; and since we believe he speaks the truth, we do not dare to imagine he said anything we ourselves know or think to be false. So while every man tries to understand in the holy Scriptures the same thing the writer understood, what harm is there if a man understands something You, the light of all truth-speaking minds, show him to be true, even though the man he is reading did not understand this — seeing that he too understood a truth, though not this truth?

For it is true, Lord, that You made heaven and earth. And it is true too that the beginning is Your wisdom, in which You create everything. And true again that this visible world has for its greater part the heaven and the earth, which briefly take in all made and created natures. And true too that whatever is changeable gives us to understand a certain lack of form, by which it receives a form, or is changed, or turned. It is true that whatever holds so fast to the unchangeable form that although it is liable to change it is never changed, is subject to no times. It is true that the formlessness which is almost nothing cannot be subject to the alteration of times. It is true that the thing something is made out of may, by a certain manner of speaking, be called by the name of the thing made out of it; so that the formlessness out of which heaven and earth were made might be called heaven and earth. It is true that of things having form, none is nearer to having no form than the earth and the deep. It is true that not only every created and formed thing but everything capable of being created and formed was made by You, from whom all things come. It is true that whatever is formed out of what had no form was unformed before it was formed.

Out of these truths, which are not doubted by those whose inward eye You have enabled to see such things, and who unshakeably believe Your servant Moses to have spoken in the Spirit of truth — out of all of these, then, one is taken by the man who says, In the beginning God made the heaven and the earth, meaning "in his Word co-eternal with himself, God made the intelligible and the sensible, or the spiritual and the bodily creature." Another is taken by the man who says, In the beginning God made heaven and earth, meaning "in his Word co-eternal with himself, God made the whole bulk of this bodily world, together with all the visible and known creatures it contains." Another by the man who says, In the beginning God made heaven and earth, meaning "in his Word co-eternal with himself, God made the formless matter of creatures spiritual and bodily." Another by the man who says, In the beginning God created heaven and earth, meaning "in his Word co-eternal with himself, God created the formless matter of the bodily creature, in which heaven and earth still lay confused, which, now distinguished and formed, we see today in the bulk of this world." Another by the man who says, In the beginning God made heaven and earth, meaning "at the very beginning of creating and working, God made that formless matter, confusedly containing in itself both heaven and earth; out of which, being formed, they now stand out and are visible, with everything in them."

And as for understanding the words that follow, out of all those truths one man chooses this for himself, who says, But the earth was invisible and without form, and darkness was upon the deep, meaning "the bodily thing God made was as yet a formless matter of bodily things, without order and without light." Another says, The earth was invisible and without form, and darkness was upon the deep, meaning "this whole thing, which is called heaven and earth, was still a formless and dark matter, out of which the bodily heaven and the bodily earth were to be made, with all the things in them that our bodily senses know." Another says, The earth was invisible and without form, and darkness was upon the deep, meaning "this whole thing, which is called heaven and earth, was still a formless and dark matter, out of which was to be made both that intelligible heaven, elsewhere called the heaven of heavens, and the earth, that is, the whole bodily nature, under which name this bodily sky is included as well; in a word, out of which every visible and invisible creature was to be created." Another says, of The earth was invisible and without form, and darkness was upon the deep, that "Scripture did not call that formlessness by the name of heaven and earth; the formlessness already existed, and it is what he called the earth invisible and without form, and the darkness upon the deep; and he had said before that God made heaven and earth, namely the spiritual and the bodily creature." Another says, The earth was invisible and without form, and darkness was upon the deep, meaning "there already was a certain formless matter, of which Scripture said before that God made heaven and earth, namely the whole bodily bulk of the world, divided into two great parts, upper and lower, with all the common and familiar creatures in them."

Suppose somebody tried to argue against these last two opinions like this. "If you will not allow that this formlessness of matter seems to be called by the name of heaven and earth, then there was something God had not made, out of which to make heaven and earth; for Scripture has not told us that God made this matter, unless we understand it to be meant by the name of heaven and earth, or of earth alone, when it says, In the beginning God made the heaven and earth. So that in what follows, and the earth was invisible and without form, although it pleased him to call the formless matter that, we are to understand no other matter than the one God made, of which it is written above, God made heaven and earth." The supporters of either of those two last opinions will answer, on hearing this: "We do not deny that this formless matter was created by God, the God from whom all things come, and all very good. Just as we say that what is created and formed is a greater good, so we admit that what is made capable of creation and form is a lesser good, but good all the same. What we say is that Scripture has not written down that God made this formlessness, just as it has not written down many other things — the cherubim and seraphim, and the ones the apostle distinctly speaks of, thrones, dominions, principalities, powers. And that God made all these is perfectly obvious. Or if in the words he made heaven and earth everything is included, what shall we say of the waters over which the Spirit of God moved? If they are included in the word earth, then how can formless matter be meant by that name of earth, when we can see how beautiful the waters are? Or if it is taken that way, why is it written that the firmament was made out of that same formlessness and called heaven, and not written that the waters were made? For the waters do not stay formless and invisible, since we watch them flowing so beautifully. But if they received that beauty when God said, Let the waters under the firmament be gathered together, so that the gathering itself is their forming, what is to be said about the waters above the firmament? If they were formless they would not have deserved so honourable a place, and it is not written by what word they were formed. So if Genesis is silent about God's making of something which nevertheless neither sound faith nor well-grounded understanding doubts that God did make; and if no sober teaching will dare to claim these waters are co-eternal with God on the grounds that we find them mentioned in the book of Genesis without finding when they were created — why then, since truth teaches us, should we not understand that formless matter, which this Scripture calls the earth invisible and without form and the dark deep, to have been created by God out of nothing, and therefore not co-eternal with him, even though this history has left out when it was created?"

Having heard and taken in all this, as far as my weak capacity allows — and I confess it to You, Lord, who know it — I see that two sorts of disagreement can arise when something is put into words by truthful reporters. One concerns the truth of the things; the other concerns the meaning of the reporter. For we ask one question about the making of the creation, namely what is true; and quite another about what Moses, that excellent minister of Your faith, wanted his reader and hearer to understand by those words. As for the first sort, away with everyone who imagines he knows as a truth something that is false; and as for the second, away with everyone too who imagines Moses wrote things that are false. But let me be united in You, Lord, with the others, and take delight in You with those who feed on Your truth, in the largeness of love; and let us come together to the words of Your book and look in them for Your meaning, through the meaning of Your servant, by whose pen You handed them out.

But which of us, among the many truths that occur to enquirers in these words as they are understood in different ways, will so hit on that one meaning as to be able to say, "This is what Moses thought", and "This is what he wanted understood in that history", with the same confidence as he would say, "This is true", whether Moses thought it or not? For look, my God: I, Your servant, who in this book have vowed a sacrifice of confession to You, and pray that by Your mercy I may pay my vows to You — can I, with the same confidence with which I say that in Your unchangeable Word You created all things visible and invisible, also say that Moses meant nothing but this when he wrote, In the beginning God made heaven and earth? No. Because I do not see in his mind that he was thinking of this when he wrote these things, in the way I do see it to be certain in Your truth. He might have had his thoughts on God's beginning to create when he said in the beginning; and by heaven and earth in this place he might have meant no formed and completed nature, whether spiritual or bodily, but both of them just begun and still formless. For I can see that whichever of the two had been said, it might have been said truly; but which of the two he was thinking of in these words I cannot see. Although whether it was one of these, or some other sense I have not mentioned here, that so great a man saw in his mind when he uttered these words, I have no doubt that he saw it truly and expressed it fittingly.

So let nobody harass me by saying, "Moses did not think what you say but what I say." For if he asked me, "How do you know that Moses thought what you infer from his words?" I ought to take it in good part and would answer, perhaps, as I have above, or at greater length if he would not give way. But when he says, "Moses did not mean what you say but what I say," and yet does not deny that what each of us says may both be true — then, my God, life of the poor, in whose heart there is no contradiction, pour a softening dew into my heart, so that I may patiently put up with people who say this to me: not because they have a divine Spirit and have seen in the heart of Your servant what they say, but because they are proud, not knowing Moses' opinion but loving their own, not because it is the truth but because it is theirs. Otherwise they would love another true opinion just as much, as I love what they say when they say something true — not because it is theirs but because it is true, and on that very ground not theirs, because it is true. But if they love it because it is true, then it is both theirs and mine, being common to all lovers of truth. But when they insist that Moses did not mean what I say but what they say, that I do not like and do not love. Even if it were so, their rashness belongs not to knowledge but to over-boldness, and its parent was not insight but vanity.

And that, Lord, is why Your judgements are terrible: seeing that Your truth is neither mine nor his nor anybody else's, but belongs to all of us, whom You call publicly to share in it, warning us terribly not to treat it as our private property, in case we are deprived of it. For whoever claims as his own what You offer to everyone to enjoy, and wants what belongs to all to be his, is driven out of what is common into what is his own — that is, out of truth into a lie. For whoever speaks a lie speaks it out of his own.

Listen, God, best of judges; truth itself, listen to what I shall say to this objector. Listen, for I speak in front of You and in front of my brothers, who use Your law lawfully to the end of love. Listen and see, if it pleases You, what I shall say to him. This is the brotherly and peaceable word I give him back: "If we both see that what you say is true, and both see that what I say is true, then where, I ask you, do we see it? Not I in you, and not you in me, but both of us in the unchangeable truth itself, which is above our souls." So since we are not fighting over the light of the Lord God itself, why are we fighting over the thoughts of our neighbour, which we cannot see the way the unchangeable truth is seen? For if Moses himself appeared to us and said, "This is what I meant," we still would not see it, but would believe it. So let us not be puffed up, one against another, beyond what is written. Let us love the Lord our God with all our heart and all our soul and all our mind, and our neighbour as ourselves. And unless we believe that Moses meant, in whatever he did mean in those books, to serve these two commandments of love, we shall make God a liar by imagining our fellow servant's mind to be other than what he has taught us. See now how foolish it is, out of such an abundance of perfectly true meanings as can be drawn out of those words, to insist rashly on which of them Moses chiefly meant, and by destructive quarrelling to offend against love itself, for whose sake he said everything he said, and whose words we are setting out to expound.

And yet I, my God, lifter-up of my humility and rest of my labour, who hear my confessions and forgive my sins — since You command me to love my neighbour as myself, I cannot believe that You gave a smaller gift to Moses Your faithful servant than I would wish or want You to have given me, had I been born when he was and had You set me in that office, so that through the service of my heart and my tongue those books might be given out which for so long afterwards were to profit all nations, and from such a height of authority were to overtop everything said by false and proud teachings. Truly, if I had then been Moses — for we all come from the same lump, and what is man, except that You are mindful of him? — if I had then been what he was, and been commanded by You to write the book of Genesis, I would have wanted such a power of expression and such a style given to me that those who cannot yet understand how God creates would not reject what I said as beyond them; and those who had got that far would find that whatever true opinion they had reached by thinking was not left out of those few words of Your servant; and that if another man had discovered another truth by the light of truth, that too should not fail to be discoverable in the same words.

For just as a spring within a narrow compass is more abundant, and supplies a flow for more streams over wider spaces, than any one of those streams which after a long distance is drawn off from the same spring, so the account given by that steward of Yours, which was to benefit many people who would discuss it, overflows out of a narrow allowance of language into streams of the clearest truth, from which every man may draw for himself such truth as he can on these subjects — one man one truth, another another, by longer roundabout stretches of talk.

For some people, when they read or hear these words, imagine that God, like a man or some mass endowed with unlimited power, by some new and sudden decision created heaven and earth outside himself, at a certain distance, as it were: two great bodies, above and below, in which everything was to be contained. And when they hear, God said, Let it be made, and it was made, they imagine words that began and ended, sounding in time and passing away, after whose departure the thing that had been commanded came into being; and whatever else of the sort their familiarity with the material world would suggest. In these people, still small children and unspiritual, while their weakness is carried along by this humble kind of speech as in a mother's arms, faith is wholesomely built up, by which they hold it certain that God made all the natures their eye sees around them in such admirable variety. And if anyone despises these words as too simple, and with a proud weakness stretches himself out beyond the sheltering nest, he will, alas, fall badly. Have pity, Lord God, in case passers-by trample the unfledged bird; and send Your angel to put it back in the nest, so that it may live until it can fly.

But there are others for whom these words are no longer a nest but a deep shady orchard; and they see the fruit hidden in it, and fly joyfully round and seek it out with cheerful calls and pluck it. For in reading or hearing these words they see that all times past and to come are surpassed by Your eternal and stable remaining; and yet that there is no creature formed in time that You did not make. Whose will, because it is the same as what You are, made everything, not by any change of will and not by a will that did not exist before; and these things were not made out of Yourself, in Your own likeness, which is the form of all things, but out of nothing: a formless unlikeness, which was to be formed by Your likeness, returning to Your unity according to its appointed capacity, so far as is given to each thing in its kind, and might all be made very good — whether they stay around You, or, removed by degrees in time and place, make or undergo the beautiful variations of the universe. These things they see and rejoice at, in the small degree they can here, in the light of Your truth.

Another man bends his mind on the words, In the beginning God made heaven and earth, and sees in them wisdom, which is the beginning because it also speaks to us. Another likewise bends his mind on the same words and by beginning understands the start of created things: in the beginning he made, as if it said, he first made. And among those who understand in the beginning to mean "in Your wisdom You created heaven and earth", one believes that the matter out of which heaven and earth were to be created is what is called heaven and earth there; another that they are natures already formed and distinguished; another that one is a formed nature, and a spiritual one, under the name heaven, and the other a formless bodily matter under the name earth. And those again who take the names heaven and earth to mean matter still formless, out of which heaven and earth were to be formed, do not all understand it the same way either: one takes it as the matter out of which both the intelligible and the sensible creation were to be completed; another only as the matter out of which this sensible bodily mass was to be made, holding in its vast lap these visible and ordinary natures. Nor do those who believe that creatures already ordered and arranged are what is called heaven and earth here all understand the same thing: one takes it as both the invisible and the visible, the other as the visible only, in which we see this bright sky and dark earth with the things they contain.

But a man who understands in the beginning he made in no other sense than "at first he made" can only truly understand heaven and earth as the matter of heaven and earth, that is, as the whole intelligible and bodily creation. For if he wanted to understand it as the universe already formed, it may fairly be asked of him, "If God made this first, what did he make afterwards?" And after the universe he will find nothing, whereupon he must hear another question against his will: "How did God make this first, if there was nothing after it?" But when he says that God first made matter formless and then formed it, there is nothing absurd about it, so long as he is qualified to tell what comes before by eternity, what by time, what by choice, and what in origin. By eternity, as God is before all things; by time, as the flower is before the fruit; by choice, as the fruit is before the flower; by origin, as the sound is before the tune.

Of these four, the first and last are understood with extreme difficulty, the two in the middle easily. For it is a rare and too lofty a vision to see Your eternity, Lord, unchangeably making changeable things, and so being before them. And who again is sharp-sighted enough to make out without great trouble how the sound is therefore before the tune? Because a tune is a formed sound; and a thing not formed can exist, whereas what does not exist cannot be formed. So the matter is before the thing made, and not because it makes the thing, seeing it is itself rather made; and it is not before by any interval of time. For we do not first in time utter formless sounds without singing and then afterwards adapt and shape them into the form of a melody, as wood or silver is shaped into a chest or a vessel. Such materials do also come before, in time, the forms of the things made out of them; but in singing it is not so, for when a song is sung its sound is heard, and there is not first a formless sound afterwards formed into a melody. For every sound passes away as soon as it is made, and you cannot find anything to call back and compose by art. So the melody is concentrated in its sound, and that sound of it is its matter. And this is formed so as to be a tune; and therefore, as I said, the matter of the sound is before the form of the tune — not before by any power it has to make the tune, for a sound is in no way the craftsman of the tune, but something bodily, subjected to the soul that sings, to make a tune out of. And it is not first in time, since it is given out along with the tune. And it is not first in choice, since a sound is not better than a tune, a tune being not only a sound but a beautiful sound. But it is first in origin, because a tune does not receive form so as to become a sound; a sound receives a form so as to become a tune.

By this example let anyone who can understand how the matter of things was made first and called heaven and earth, because heaven and earth were made out of it. And yet it was not made first in time, because it is the forms of things that give rise to time; and that matter was formless, whereas now it is, in time, an object of the senses, together with its form. And yet nothing can be said about that matter except as though it came earlier in time, whereas in value it is last, since formed things are superior to formless ones; and it is preceded by the eternity of the creator, so that there might be something out of nothing, out of which something might be created.

In this diversity of true opinions, let truth herself produce concord. And may our God have mercy on us, so that we may use the law lawfully, the end of the commandment being pure love. And if anyone asks me by this rule, "Which of these was Your servant Moses' meaning?" — this would not be the language of my confessions if I did not confess to You, "I do not know." And yet I do know that those senses are true, except for the unspiritual ones, about which I have said what seemed necessary. And even those hopeful little children who think that way have this benefit, that the words of Your book do not frighten them, delivering high things humbly, and a copious meaning in few words. And all of us who, I confess, see and express the truth delivered in those words, let us love one another, and love together You our God, the fountain of truth, if it is truth we are thirsty for and not empty things. And let us so honour this servant of Yours, the steward of this Scripture, full of Your Spirit, as to believe that when by Your revelation he wrote these things, he intended the meaning that stands out among them for light of truth and for fruitfulness of profit.

So when one man says, "Moses meant what I mean", and another, "No, he meant what I mean", I think I speak more reverently when I say, "Why not both, if both are true?" And if there is a third or a fourth — indeed if anyone sees any other truth in those words — why may he not be believed to have seen all of them, this man through whom the one God tuned the holy Scriptures to the understanding of many, who would see in them things that were true but different? For I certainly say this, and I say it fearlessly from my heart: if I were to write anything that was to have supreme authority, I would rather write in such a way that whatever truth anyone could grasp about these matters could be conveyed by my words, than set down one meaning of my own so clearly as to exclude all the rest, which, not being false, could not offend me. So I shall not be so rash, my God, as not to believe that You granted as much to that great man. Without doubt, when he wrote those words, he perceived and had in mind whatever truth we have been able to find in them — and whatever we have not been able to find, and are still not able to, but which may be found in them.

Finally, Lord, who are God and not flesh and blood: if the man saw less, could anything be hidden from Your good Spirit, who shall lead me into the land of uprightness — anything that You Yourself were going to reveal by those words to readers in times to come, even though the man through whom they were spoken had in mind, perhaps among many true meanings, some one of them? And if that is how it is, let the meaning he had in mind be the highest of all. But to us, Lord, either reveal that same meaning, or any other true one You please; so that whether You show us the same thing You showed Your servant, or something else by way of those words, You may feed us and error may not deceive us. See, Lord my God, how much we have written on a few words — how much! What strength of ours, what ages would be enough for all Your books at this rate? So allow me to confess to You more briefly in them, and to choose some one true, certain and good sense that You inspire in me, even where many may occur — this being the law of my confession: that if I should say what Your minister intended, that is right and best, since that is what I should aim at; and if I should not manage it, that I should still say what Your truth willed to tell me by his words, since it was Your truth that revealed to him too what it willed.

Book Thirteen: The Days of Creation

I call on You, my God, my mercy, who created me and did not forget me when I forgot You. I call You into my soul, which You are preparing for Yourself by the longing You breathe into her. Do not abandon me now that I am calling on You, when You went ahead of me before I called, and pressed me with all sorts of repeated calls so that I might hear You from far off, and turn round, and call on You who were calling after me. For You, Lord, wiped out all my bad deserts, so as not to pay back into my hands what I fell away from You with; and You went ahead of all my good deserts, so as to pay back the work of Your own hands, with which You made me. Because before I existed, You existed; and I was nothing that You could grant existence to. And yet here I am, out of Your goodness, which came before everything You have made me of and made me into. For You had no need of me, and I am no such good thing as to be of help to You, my Lord and God: not by serving You as though You would grow tired of working, or as though Your power would be less if my service were missing; not by cultivating Your service as if it were a piece of land that must lie uncultivated unless I cultivated You. I serve and worship You so that I may receive well-being from You, from whom it comes that I have a being capable of well-being at all.

For it is out of the fullness of Your goodness that Your creation exists: so that a good which could bring You no profit at all, and was not out of You, in case it should be equal to You, might still exist, since it could be made by You. What claim on You did heaven and earth have, which You made in the beginning? Let those spiritual and bodily natures which You made in Your wisdom say what claim they had on You to depend on You — even in that state of theirs, just begun and formless, whether spiritual or bodily, ready to fall away into an unrestrained liberty and a far-off unlikeness to You. The spiritual, though without form, is superior to the bodily even when the bodily is formed; and the bodily, though without form, is better than it would be if it were altogether nothing. And they depend on Your Word as formless things, unless by that same Word they are brought back to Your unity, given form, and made all very good by You, the one sovereign good. What claim did they have on You even to be formless, when they would not have been even that except from You?

What claim did bodily matter have on You even to be invisible and without form? It would not have been even that if You had not made it; and therefore, because it did not exist, it could have no claim on You to be made. Or how could the just-begun spiritual creature have had a claim on You even to ebb and flow darkly like the deep, unlike You, unless it had been turned by the same Word to the one by whom it was created, and been so lit by him as to become light — not equally with him, but conformed to the form which is equal to You? For as in a body, existing is not the same thing as being beautiful, since otherwise it could not be ugly, so likewise for a created spirit, living is not the same thing as living wisely, since otherwise it would be wise unchangeably. But it is good for it always to hold fast to You, in case the light it has gained by turning to You is lost by turning away from You, and it slips back into a life like the dark deep. For we ourselves also, who as to the soul are a spiritual creature, once turned away from You our light, were in that life darkness; and we still labour among the remains of our darkness, until in Your only Son we become Your righteousness, like the mountains of God. For we have been Your judgements, which are like the great deep.

What You said at the beginning of creation, Let there be light, and there was light, I take, not unsuitably, to be about the spiritual creature: because there was already a kind of life for You to illuminate. But just as it had no claim on You for a life that could be enlightened, so neither, now that it existed, had it any claim to be enlightened. For its formless state could not be pleasing to You unless it became light — and became light not by simply existing but by looking at the illuminating light and holding fast to it. So that its living, and living happily, it owes to nothing but Your grace, having been turned by a change for the better towards the one who cannot be changed for worse or better, which is what You alone are, because You alone simply are. For You it is not one thing to live and another to live blessedly, since You are Your own blessedness.

What then could be lacking to Your good, which You Yourself are, even if these things had either never existed or remained without form — these things which You made not out of any lack but out of the fullness of Your goodness, holding them in and turning them into form, not as though Your joy were completed by them? For to You, being perfect, their imperfection is displeasing; and so they were perfected by You and please You — not as though You were imperfect and were to be perfected by their perfecting. For Your good Spirit was borne over the waters and not borne up by them, as if he rested on them. Those on whom Your good Spirit is said to rest, he causes to rest in himself. But Your incorruptible and unchangeable will, in itself all-sufficient for itself, was borne over that life You had created, for which living is not the same as living happily, since it lives even while ebbing and flowing in its own darkness. And it remains for it to be turned to him by whom it was made, and to live more and more by the fountain of life, and in his light to see light, and be perfected and enlightened and made beautiful.

Now the Trinity appears to me in a glass darkly, which is You, my God — because You, Father, in him who is the beginning of our wisdom, which is Your wisdom, born of Yourself, equal to You and co-eternal, that is, in Your Son, created heaven and earth. We have now said much about the heaven of heavens, and about the earth invisible and without form, and about the dark deep, with reference to the wandering instability of its spiritual deformity, unless it had been turned towards him from whom it had the degree of life it then had, and by his enlightening became a beautiful life, and the heaven of that heaven which was afterwards set between water and water. And under the name of God I now took the Father, who made these things, and under the name of Beginning the Son, in whom he made these things; and believing, as I did, in my God as Trinity, I searched further in his holy words — and there was Your Spirit moving over the waters. There is the Trinity, my God: Father and Son and Holy Spirit, creator of all creation.

But what was the reason, truth-speaking light? To You I lift up my heart; let it not teach me empty things; scatter its darkness. Tell me, I beg You, by our mother, love — tell me the reason, I beg You, why after the mention of heaven, and of the earth invisible and without form, and darkness upon the deep, Your Scripture should only then mention Your Spirit. Was it because it was fitting that knowledge of him should be conveyed as being "borne above", and this could not be said unless the thing over which Your Spirit may be understood to have been borne were mentioned first? For he was not borne above the Father or the Son, and could not rightly be said to be borne above if he were borne over nothing. So first the thing had to be mentioned that he might be borne over, and then he, whom it was fitting to speak of only as being borne. But why was it not fitting for knowledge of him to be conveyed otherwise than as being borne above?

Let anyone who can, follow Your apostle here with his understanding, where he says: Because Your love is poured into our hearts by the Holy Spirit who is given to us; and where, on the subject of spiritual gifts, he teaches and shows us a more excellent way of love; and where he bends his knee to You for us, so that we may know the surpassing knowledge of the love of Christ. And that is why from the beginning he was borne surpassingly above the waters. Whom shall I say this to? And how am I to speak of the weight of evil desires, pulling down to the steep abyss, and of how love raises us up again by Your Spirit, who was borne above the waters? Whom shall I say it to, and how? For it is not in space that we sink and rise. What could be more like, and yet what less? They are affections, they are loves: the uncleanness of our spirit flowing away downwards under the load of cares, and the holiness of Yours raising us up by love of an untroubled rest, so that we may lift our hearts to You, where Your Spirit is borne above the waters, and come to that surpassing rest when our soul has passed through the waters that give no footing.

Angels fell away, and the soul of man fell away, and so marked out the abyss in that dark depth, ready for the whole spiritual creation, if You had not said from the beginning, Let there be light — and there was light, and every obedient intelligence of Your heavenly city held fast to You and rested in Your Spirit, who is borne unchangeably over everything changeable. Otherwise even the heaven of heavens would have been in itself a dark deep; but now it is light in the Lord. For even in that miserable restlessness of the spirits who fell away and discovered their own darkness when stripped of the clothing of Your light, You reveal well enough how noble a thing You made the reasoning creature: nothing less than You will be enough to give it a happy rest, and so not even itself. For You, our God, will lighten our darkness; from You our garment of light rises; and then our darkness shall be as noonday. Give Yourself to me, my God, restore Yourself to me. I love, and if that is too little, let me love more strongly. I cannot measure so as to know how much love is still lacking in me, before my life may run into Your embrace and never turn away until it is hidden in the hidden place of Your presence. This only I know: that it goes badly with me except in You — not only outside myself but inside myself too; and all abundance that is not my God is emptiness to me.

But was neither the Father nor the Son borne above the waters? If this means in space, like a body, then neither was the Holy Spirit. But if it means the unchangeable surpassingness of divinity above everything changeable, then Father and Son and Holy Spirit were all borne above the waters. Why then is it said of Your Spirit only? Why is it said of him alone, as though he had been in a place, who is not in a place, and of whom alone it is written that he is Your gift? In Your gift we rest; there we enjoy You. Our rest is our place. Love lifts us there, and Your good Spirit lifts our lowliness up from the gates of death. In Your good pleasure is our peace. A body strives by its own weight towards its own place. Weight does not pull only downwards, but towards a thing's own place. Fire tends upwards, a stone downwards. They are driven by their own weight; they look for their own places. Oil poured under water is lifted above the water; water poured on oil sinks below the oil. They are driven by their own weights to look for their own places. When they are out of their order they are restless; put back in order, they are at rest. My weight is my love; by it I am carried wherever I am carried. By Your gift we are set alight and carried upwards; we glow inwardly and go forward. We climb Your paths that are in our heart, and sing a song of ascents; we glow inwardly with Your fire, with Your good fire, and we go, because we are going upwards to the peace of Jerusalem — for I was glad when they said to me, We will go up to the house of the Lord. There Your good pleasure has placed us, so that we may want nothing else but to stay there for ever.

Blessed creature, which, being itself other than You, has known no other condition than this: that as soon as it was made it was, without any interval, carried aloft by Your gift, which is borne above everything changeable — carried aloft by that call in which You said, Let there be light, and there was light. Whereas in us this happened at different times, in that we were darkness and are made light; but of that creature it is only said what it would have been if it had not been enlightened. And this is said as though it had been unsettled and dark before, so that the cause by which it was made otherwise might appear: namely that being turned to the unfailing light it became light. Let anyone who can, understand this; let him ask You for it. Why should he trouble me, as though I could enlighten any man who comes into this world?

Which of us takes in the almighty Trinity? And yet which of us does not speak of it, if it is indeed that we are speaking of? Rare is the soul that, while speaking of it, knows what it is speaking of. And people argue and struggle, and without peace nobody sees that vision. I wish people would consider these three things that are in themselves. These three are indeed something quite other than the Trinity; I only say where they may practise on themselves, and there test and feel how far off they are. The three I mean are: to be, to know, and to will. For I am, and I know, and I will. I am knowing and willing; and I know myself to be and to will; and I will to be and to know. In these three, then, let anyone who can make out how inseparable a life there is — one life, one mind, one essence; and how inseparable a distinction there is, and yet a distinction. Surely a man has it in front of him; let him look into himself and see, and tell me. But when he discovers something and can say something about these three, let him not therefore think he has found the unchangeable one who is above them, who is unchangeably, and knows unchangeably, and wills unchangeably. And whether because of these three there is also a Trinity in God, or whether all three are in each, so that the three belong to each, or whether both at once, wonderfully, simply and yet manifoldly, itself a boundary to itself within itself, yet unbounded, by which it is and is known to itself and is enough for itself, unchangeably the selfsame by the abundant greatness of its unity — who can readily conceive this? Who could express it in any way at all? Who would pronounce on it rashly?

Go on with your confession, my faith; say to the Lord your God: Holy, holy, holy, Lord my God, in Your name we have been baptised — Father, Son and Holy Spirit; in Your name we baptise — Father, Son and Holy Spirit. Because among us too, in his Christ, God made heaven and earth, namely the spiritual and the unspiritual people of his church. And our earth, before it received the form of doctrine, was invisible and without form, and we were covered with the darkness of ignorance. For You corrected man for his wrongdoing, and Your judgements were like the great deep to him. But because Your Spirit was borne above the waters, Your mercy did not abandon our misery, and You said, Let there be light — Repent, for the kingdom of heaven is at hand. Repent: let there be light. And because our soul was troubled within us, we remembered You, Lord, from the land of Jordan, and that mountain equal to Yourself but made small for our sakes; and our darkness displeased us, and we turned to You, and there was light. And we were once darkness, and are now light in the Lord.

But so far by faith and not by sight; for we are saved by hope, and hope that is seen is not hope. So far deep still calls to deep, but now in the voice of Your waterfalls. So far the man who says, I could not speak to you as spiritual but as unspiritual — even he does not yet think he has taken hold of it, and forgets the things that are behind and reaches forward to the things ahead, and groans under his burden, and his soul thirsts for the living God as the deer for the water-brooks, and says, When shall I come? — wanting to be clothed with his house from heaven; and calls to this lower deep, saying, Do not be conformed to this world, but be transformed by the renewing of your mind; and, Do not be children in understanding, but in malice be children, so that in understanding you may be grown up; and, Foolish Galatians, who has bewitched you? But now no longer in his own voice, but in Yours, who sent Your Spirit from above through him who went up on high and opened the floodgates of his gifts, so that the force of his streams might gladden the city of God. It is for him that this friend of the bridegroom sighs, having now the first fruits of the Spirit laid up with him, and yet still groaning inwardly, waiting for the adoption, that is, the redemption of his body. For him he sighs, being a member of the bride; for him he is jealous, being a friend of the bridegroom; for him and not for himself he is jealous, because it is in the voice of Your waterfalls and not in his own voice that he calls to that other depth, over which, being jealous, he is afraid — in case, as the serpent tricked Eve by his cunning, so their minds should be corrupted from the purity that is in our bridegroom, Your only Son. What a light of beauty that will be, when we shall see him as he is, and those tears have passed away which have been my food day and night, while they say to me every day, Where is Your God now?

I too say it, my God: where are You? See where You are! In You I breathe a little, when I pour out my soul by myself in the voice of joy and praise, the sound of a man keeping festival. And then it grows sad again, because it slips back and becomes a deep — or rather perceives that it is still a deep. My faith speaks to it, the faith You have lit to light my feet in the night: why are you sad, my soul, and why do you trouble me? Hope in the Lord; his word is a lamp to your feet. Hope and endure, until the night is over — the mother of the wicked, the anger of the Lord — of which we too were once children, who were once darkness, and whose leavings we still carry about in our body, dead because of sin; until the day breaks and the shadows run away. Hope in the Lord: in the morning I shall stand in Your presence and contemplate You; I shall confess to You for ever. In the morning I shall stand in Your presence and see the health of my face, my God, who will also bring our mortal bodies to life by the Spirit who lives in us, because in mercy he has been borne over our inner dark and floating deep. From him we have received a down payment on this pilgrimage, that we should now be light, while we are saved by hope, and are children of the light and children of the day, and not children of the night or of the darkness — which nevertheless we once were. Between them and us, in this uncertainty of human knowledge, You alone divide, You who test our hearts and call the light day and the darkness night. For who tells us apart but You? And what do we have that we have not received from You? Out of the same lump vessels are made for honour, and others out of it for dishonour.

And who except You, our God, made for us that firmament of authority over us in Your divine Scripture? For it is said, The sky shall be folded up like a scroll; and now it is stretched over us like a skin. For Your divine Scripture has a higher authority since the mortals through whom You gave it out to us underwent their mortality. And You know, Lord, You know how You clothed men in skins when they became mortal through sin. So You have stretched out the firmament of Your book like a skin — Your harmonising words, which You spread over us by the ministry of mortal men. For it was by their very deaths that the solid firmament of authority in Your words, as set out by them, was more loftily stretched over everyone under it; while they were alive here it was not stretched so high. You had not yet spread the heavens out like a skin; You had not yet enlarged in every direction the glory of their deaths.

Let us look, Lord, at the heavens, the work of Your fingers; clear from our eyes the cloud You have spread beneath them.
